Regularized limits of Stokes matrices, isomonodromy deformation and crystal basis
Xiaomeng Xu

Abstract
In the first part of the paper, we solve the boundary and monodromy problems for the isomonodromy equation of the n × n n\times n meromorphic linear system of ordinary differential equations with Poncaré rank 1 1 . In particular, we derive an explicit expression of the Stokes matrices of the linear system, via the boundary value of the solutions of the isomonodromy equation at a critical point. Motivated by this result, we then describe the regularized limits of Stokes matrices as the irregular data u = diag ⁡ ( u 1 , … , u n ) u={\rm diag}(u_{1},...,u_{n}) in the linear system degenerates, i.e., as some u i , u j , … , u k u_{i},u_{j},...,u_{k} collapse. The prescription of the regularized limit is controlled by the geometry of the De Concini-Procesi wonderful compactification space. As applications, many analysis problems about higher rank Painlevé transcendents can be solved. In the second part of the paper, we show some important applications of the above analysis results in representation theory and Poisson geometry: we obtain the first transcendental realization of crystals in representations of 𝔤 ​ 𝔩 n \mathfrak{gl}_{n} via the Stokes phenomenon in the WKB approximation; we develop a wall-crossing formula that characterizes the discontinuous jump of the regularized limits of Stokes matrices as crossing walls in the compactification space, and interpret the known cactus group actions on crystals arising from representation theory as a wall-crossing phenomenon; and we find the first explicit linearization of the standard dual Poisson Lie group for U ⁡ ( n ) U(n) .



1
Introduction and main results

In recent years there has been considerable interests in analyzing the Stokes matrices of a meromorphic linear systems of ordinary differential equations at a second order pole, and the associated isomonodromy deformation equations. The sources of these interests are
quite diverse, which include many subjects in mathematics and physics, like the theory of Gromov-Witten and Frobenius manifolds
[
23
,
36
,
32
]
, stability conditions
[
18
]
, higher Painlevé transcendents
[
47
,
62
]
, Poisson groups and simple models for the wild non-abelian Hodge correspondence
[
11
,
10
]
, representation theory of quantum groups
[
76
,
75
]
, and so on.

Yet some basic properties of the Stokes matrices and the solutions of associated isomonodromic deformation equations, as special functions, still remain almost unexplored, like their behavior at singularities, their WKB approximation, and their explicit expressions and so on. These unresolved problems are obstacles to the substantial role of the Stokes phenomenon in the related areas. In this paper, these problems are studied. Most importantly, an explicit expression of the Stokes matrices is derived via the isomonodromy approach. It provides us a manipulable analysis model for the study of the Stokes phenomenon itself and the relations with other subjects. Some applications of the model in representation theory and Poisson geometry are given in this paper: among them, the most noteworthy ones are the transcendental realization of crystal basis and the cactus group action. More applications of the model are presented in a series of follow-up works, some of them can be found in
[
4
,
54
,
71
,
74
,
77
,
78
,
79
]
.

Other than various new transcendental realizations of the known algebraic structures, including Gelfand-Tsetlin basis, quantum groups, crystals and cactus groups given in this paper, we are more interested in the role of the algebraic structures in the study of the Stokes phenomenon itself. For example, the representation theoretic data can be used to characterize the Stokes phenomenon in the WKB approximation of the meromorphic ODEs, which is still an open problem. Additionally, they help to study some basic problems of the isomonodromy equations. So another main idea conveyed in this article is the importance of algebraic structures in understanding the Stokes phenomenon.

1.1
Explicit expression of Stokes matrices via the boundary value

Let
\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
denote the space of
n\times n
diagonal matrices
u={\rm diag}(u_{1},...,u_{n})
with distinct real eigenvalues. Let
{\rm Herm}(n)
denote the space of
n\times n
Hermitian matrices.
Let us consider the
n\times n
linear system of partial differential equations for a function
F(z,u_{1},...,u_{n})\in{\rm GL}(n)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial z}

\displaystyle=\left({\mathrm{i}}u-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\Phi(u)}{z}\right)\cdot F,

(1)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial u_{k}}

\displaystyle=\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{k}z-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}{\rm ad}^{-1}_{u}{\rm ad}_{E_{k}}\Phi(u)\right)\cdot F,\ \text{for all}\ k=1,...,n,

(2)

where the residue
\Phi(u)=\Phi(u_{1},...,u_{n})\in{\rm Herm}(n)
is a solution of the
isomonodromy differential equation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial u_{k}}=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}[\Phi,{\rm ad}^{-1}_{u}{\rm ad}_{E_{k}}\Phi],\ \text{for all}\ k=1,...,n.

(3)

Here
{\mathrm{i}}=\sqrt{-1}
,
E_{k}
is the
n\times n
diagonal matrix whose
(k,k)
-entry is
1
and other entries are
0
. And
u
in the coefficients of the linear system represents the diagonal matrix
{\rm diag}(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})
.
Note that
{\rm ad}_{E_{k}}\Phi
takes values in the space
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}^{od}
of off diagonal matrices and that the adjoint operator
{\rm ad}_{u}

is invertible when restricted to
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}^{od}
. One checks that (
3
) is the compatibility condition of the linear system. Our first result solves a Riemann-Hilbert problem of the linear system. In particular, the following Theorem
1.1
gives a natural parameterization of the linear systems by the boundary value of
\Phi(u)
, and Theorem
1.2
expresses the monodromy data explicitly via the parameterization. Let us now give more details.

For any fixed
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, the ordinary differential equation (
1
) has a unique formal solution
\hat{F}(z)
around

z=\infty
. Then the standard theory of resummation states that there exist certain sectorial regions around
z=\infty
, such that on each of these sectors there is a unique (therefore canonical) holomorphic solution with the prescribed asymptotics
\hat{F}(z)
. These solutions are in general different (that reflects the Stokes phenomenon), and the transition between them can be measured by a pair of Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,\Phi(u))\in{\rm GL}(n)
. The Stokes matrices
S_{+}
and
S_{-}
are upper and lower triangular matrices and, due to the real condition
u\in\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
and
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, are complex conjugate to each other. See Section
2
for more details. Varying
u
, the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,\Phi(u))\in{\rm GL}(n)
of the system are constant (independent of
u
), and this is why the equation (
3
) is called isomonodromy.

Following Miwa
[
62
]
, the
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-valued solutions
\Phi(u)
of the equation (
3
) with
u_{1},...,u_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
have the strong Painlevé property: they are multi-valued meromorphic functions of
u_{1},...,u_{n}
and the branching occurs when
u
moves along a loop around the fat diagonal

\Delta=\{(u_{1},...,u_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}~|~u_{i}=u_{j},\text{for some }i\neq j\}.

Thus, according to the original idea of Painlevé, they can be a new class of special functions. The problem of determining their behavior at the fixed
critical singularities is left open. The following theorem treats this problem for the
{\rm Herm}(n)
-valued solutions. First according to Boalch
[
11
]
, the
{\rm Herm}(n)
-valued solutions
\Phi(u)
of (
3
) are real analytic on each connected component of
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
. In Section
3.2
and
3.3
, we prove

Theorem 1.1
.

For any solution
\Phi(u)
of the isomonodromy equation (
3
) on the connected component
U_{\rm id}:=\{u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})~|~u_{1}<\cdots<u_{n}\}
, there exists a unique constant
\Phi_{0}\in{\rm Herm}(n)
such that as the real numbers
\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\rightarrow+\infty
for all
k=2,...,n-1
,

\displaystyle\Phi(u)=

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}{\left((u_{2}-u_{1})^{\frac{\delta_{1}(\Phi_{0})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot\overrightarrow{\underset{k=2,...,n-1}{\prod}}\left(\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\right)^{\frac{\delta_{k}(\Phi_{0})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)}\Phi_{0}+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}\right),

(4)

where
{\rm Ad}(g)X=gXg^{-1}
for any
g\in U(n)
and
X\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, the product
\overrightarrow{\prod}
is taken with the index
i
to the right of
j
if
i>j
. And
\delta_{k}(\Phi)
is the Hermitian matrix with entries

\delta_{k}(\Phi)_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\Phi_{ij},&\text{if}\ \ 1\leq i,j\leq k,\ \text{or}\ i=j\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

(Here we use the big
\mathcal{O}
notation, in particular each
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}\right)
stands for a remainder whose norm is less than
M\times\left(\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\right)^{-1}
for a positive real number
M
as
\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}
big enough.) Furthermore, given any
\Phi_{0}\in{\rm Herm}(n)
there exists a unique real analytic solution
\Phi(u)
of (
3
) with the prescribed asymptotics (
4
).

From the isomonodromy equation, we see that any solution
\Phi(u)
is invariant under the translation action on
\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, that is
\Phi(u_{1},...,u_{n})=\Phi(u_{1}+c,...,u_{n}+c)
. Let
\mathbb{R}
act on
\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
by translation, then
\Phi(u)
is defined on
\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{R}
. Let
u_{\rm cat}
denote the following limit

u_{2}-u_{1}\rightarrow 0+\text{ and }\frac{u_{j}-u_{j-1}}{u_{j-1}-u_{j-2}}\rightarrow+\infty\text{ for all }j=3,...,n.

(5)

The limit is a point, called a caterpillar point in the literature
[
69
]
, in the De Concini-Procesi space that is a certain compactification of

\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{R}
, see Section
1.3
. In this paper, we do not distinguish the limit (
5
) and the point in the De Concini-Procesi space.
We call the regularized limit
\Phi_{0}\in{\rm Herm}(n)
the boundary value at
u_{\rm cat}
, and denote by
\Phi(u;\Phi_{0})
the unique solution of (
3
) on
U_{\rm id}
with the prescribed boundary value
\Phi_{0}
.
Following Theorem
1.1
, the boundary value
\Phi_{0}\in{\rm Herm}(n)
describes the leading asymptotics of
\Phi(u;\Phi_{0})
as
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
.

In Section
3
, we solve the monodromy problem of the linear system (
1
)-(
2
) with
\Phi(u)=\Phi(u;\Phi_{0})
.
We denote by
\{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}\}_{i=1,...,k}
the eigenvalues of the left-top
k\times k
submatrix of
\Phi_{0}\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, and
(\Phi_{0})_{kk}
the
k
-th diagonal element. Then

Theorem 1.2
.

The sub-diagonal entries of the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}\left(u,\Phi(u;\Phi_{0})\right)
of the linear system (
1
) are given by

\displaystyle(S_{+})_{k,k+1}

\displaystyle=2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\cdot{\rm exp}{\left(\frac{\small{(\Phi_{0})_{kk}+(\Phi_{0})_{k+1,k+1}}}{4}\right)}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)}{\prod_{l=1}^{k+1}\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k+1)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)}\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma\left(\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)}{\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k-1)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)}\cdot{\Delta^{1,...,k-1,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(\frac{\Phi_{0}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)},

\displaystyle(S_{-})_{k+1,k}

\displaystyle=-2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\cdot{\rm exp}{\left(\frac{\small{(\Phi_{0})_{kk}+(\Phi_{0})_{k+1,k+1}}}{4}\right)}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)}{\prod_{l=1}^{k+1}\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\lambda^{(k+1)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)}\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma\left(-\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)}{\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\lambda^{(k-1)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)}\cdot{\Delta^{1,...,k-1,k+1}_{1,...,k-1,k}\left(\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}-\Phi_{0}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)}.

where
k=1,...,n-1
and
\Delta^{1,...,k-1,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}(\frac{\Phi_{0}-{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})
is the
k
by
k
minor of the matrix
\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}(\Phi_{0}-{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}\cdot{\rm Id}_{n})
formed by the first
k
rows and
1,...,k-1,k+1
columns (here
{\rm Id}_{n}
is the rank
n
identity matrix).
Furthermore, the other entries are determined by the sub-diagonal ones in a systematic way, and thus are also given by explicit expressions, see Section
3.10
.

Remark 1.3
.

For the special case
n=3
, Theorem
1.1
and
1.2
recover Jimbo’s asymptotic and monodromy formula for the Painlevé VI transcendents, see
[
74
]
for more details. From this viewpoint, they provide the asymptotic and monodromy formula for the higher Painlevé transcendents.

We also remark that the asymptotics of
\Phi(u)
in Theorem
1.1
is only a local analysis, while many global properties of
\Phi(u;\Phi_{0})
can be obtained from Theorem
1.2
. That is the power of the Riemann-Hilbert approach to the study of the nonlinear differential equations.

Theorem
1.1
gives a parameterization of the Hermitian matrix valued solutions of the isomonodromy equation (
3
), and Theorem
1.2
computes explicitly the Stokes matrices of the corresponding linear equation
1
via the parameterization. Therefore, we obtain an explicit Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff map (a diffeomorphism) from the space
{\rm Herm}(n)
of meromorphic linear systems to the space of Stokes matrices via the equivalences

\displaystyle\Big\{\Phi_{0}\in{\rm Herm}(n)\Big\}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\Big\{\text{solutions $\Phi(u;\Phi_{0})\in{\rm Herm}(n)$ of the isomonodromy equation \eqref{introisoeq} on $U_{\rm id}$}\Big\}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\Big\{\text{linear systems of PDEs \eqref{introisoStokeseq1} and \eqref{introisoStokeseq2}}\Big\}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\Big\{\text{space of Stokes matrices $S_{\pm}\left(u,\Phi(u;\Phi_{0})\right)$}\Big\}.

The Poisson geometric nature of the diffeomorphism is unveiled in Theorem
1.13
.

1.2
Regularized limits of Stokes matrices at a caterpillar point

Consider a linear system of meromorphic ordinary differential equations for a function
F(z)\in{\rm GL}(u,\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle\frac{dF}{dz}=\left({\mathrm{i}}u-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{A}{z}\right)\cdot F,

(6)

where
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
and
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
. For a generic
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, the limit of the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,A)
, as some components
u_{i}
of
u
collapse, do not exist. See the rank 2 example in Example
3.36
. Motivated by Theorem
1.1
and
1.2
, the behaviour of the Stokes matrices at the singularities is studied in this paper. For example, a manipulation of Theorem
1.1
and
1.2
shows that (see Section
3.8
for a proof)

Proposition 1.4
.

For any
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, the sub-diagonal entries of
S_{+}(u,A)
, as
\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\rightarrow+\infty
for all
k=2,...,n-1
, are

\displaystyle(S_{+})_{k,k+1}=2\pi{\mathrm{i}}e^{\frac{(A)_{kk}+(A)_{k+1,k+1}}{4}}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)}{\prod_{l=1}^{k+1}\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k+1)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)}\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma\left(\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)}{\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k-1)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)}\Delta^{1,...,k-1,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(\frac{A-{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\cdot f^{(k)}_{i}(A)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\right)^{-1}\right),

where the singular part

f^{(k)}_{i}(A)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}(u_{k}-u_{k-1})^{\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(A)-A_{kk}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}(u_{k+1}-u_{k})^{\frac{A_{k+1,k+1}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}},&\text{if}\ \ k\geq 2,\\
(u_{2}-u_{1})^{\frac{A_{22}-\lambda^{(1)}_{1}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}},&\text{if}\ \ k=1.\end{array}\right.

(7)

Here recall
\{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}\}_{i=1,...,k}
denote the eigenvalues of the left-top
k\times k
submatrix of
A
.
Similarly, the leading terms of all entries of
S_{\pm}(u,A)
can be given explicitly.

Following Proposition
1.4
, the fast spin terms (
7
)
(whose norm is
1
since
\lambda^{(k)}_{i}
are real numbers) in the expression prevent the Stokes matrix
S_{+}(u,A)
from having a limit.
However, the upshot is that
S_{\pm}(u,A)
have properly regularized limit at
u_{\rm cat}
, i.e., as all
u_{1},...,u_{n}
collapse in the speed that
u_{i}
approaches to
u_{i-1}
much faster than
u_{i-1}
approaches to
u_{i-2}

. To be more precise, for any
u
and
A
, let us introduce the unitary matrix

G(u,A)=({u_{2}-u_{1}})^{\frac{{\rm log}\left(\delta_{1}(S_{-}(u,A))\delta_{1}(S_{+}(u,A))\right)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot\overrightarrow{\underset{k=2,...,n-1}{\prod}}\left(\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\right)^{\frac{{\rm log}\left(\delta_{k}(S_{-}(u,A))\delta_{k}(S_{+}(u,A))\right)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}},

(8)

where
{\rm log}(\delta_{k}(S_{-})\delta_{k}(S_{+}))
is the logarithm of the positive definite Hermitian matrix
\delta_{k}(S_{-}(u,A))\delta_{k}(S_{+}(u,A))
(see Section
2
for the positive definiteness). Then
we have (see Section
3.9
for a proof)

Theorem 1.5
.

For
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, the limit of the matrix valued function

\lim_{u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}}G(u,A)\left(S_{-}(u,A)S_{+}(u,A)\right)G(u,A)^{-1}=S_{-}\left(u,\Phi(u;A)\right)S_{+}\left(u,\Phi(u;A)\right).

(9)

Here by definition,
\Phi(u;A)
is the solution of isomonodromy equation on
U_{\rm id}
with the given boundary value
A
at
u_{\rm cat}
. Therefore, by Theorem
1.2
the limit, i.e., the right hand side of (
9
), as a function of
A
, has a closed formula (provided replacing
\Phi_{0}
by
A
in Theorem
1.2
).

The regularization of
S_{-}(u,A)S_{+}(u,A)
by the unitary matrix given in (
8
) has a geometric interpretation as a fast spin on Liouville tori of the Gelfand-Testlin integrable system, see Section
3.9
. The theorem states that
S_{\pm}(u,A)
have regularized limits
S_{\pm}(u,\Phi(u;A))
(independent of
u
) at
u_{\rm cat}
.
Based on Theorem
1.5
, we introduce an important notion (the meaning of a caterpillar point will be clear in the next subsection).

Definition 1.6
.

For any
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, we call the regularized limits
S_{\pm}(u_{\rm cat},A):=S_{\pm}\left(u,\Phi(u;A)\right)
the Stokes matrices at the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
with respect to the connected component
U_{\rm id}
.

1.3
Regularized limit of Stokes matrices in the De Concini-Procesi space and wall-crossing formula

The results in this subsection are not necessary for the second main result of this paper, i.e., the following Application II.

On the one hand, by definition, the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,A)
are invariant under the translation action on
\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
. Thus let
\mathbb{R}
act on
\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
by translation, then for any fixed
A
,
S_{\pm}(u,A)
are parameterized by
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{R}
. Here
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
is the space of
n\times n
diagonal matrices
u={\rm diag}(u_{1},...,u_{n})
with distinct real eigenvalues and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}=0
. On the other hand, any solution
\Phi(u)
is also invariant under the translation action on
\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
. Therefore, we can actually assume the irregular data
u
is in the subspace
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})\subset\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
. In this paper, for simplicity, we use
U_{\sigma}=\{u_{\sigma(1)}<\cdots<u_{\sigma(n)}\}
for some
\sigma\in S_{n}
to denote the connected components both in
\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
according to the context.

In Section
5
we study of the regularized limit of the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,A)
of equation (
6
), as some components
u_{i}
of
u={\rm diag}(u_{1},...,u_{n})\in\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
collapse in a comparable speed. It completely describes the asymptotic behaviour of the Stokes matrices at the singularities
u_{i}=u_{j}
.
And the prescription of the regularized limits is controlled by the geometry of the De Concini-Procesi wonderful space
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
. Let us give a brief introduction. Here given a finite set of subspaces of a vector space, the De Concini-Procesi space
[
20
]
replaces the set of subspaces by a divisor with normal crossings, and leaves the complement of these subspaces unchanged. As for the root hyperplanes of type
A
Lie algebra,
the associated De Concini-Procesi space
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
contains
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
as an open part, and
roughly speaking, a point in the boundary
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})\setminus{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
is a limit point
u={\rm diag}(u_{1},...,u_{n})
, where some
u_{i}
collapse in a comparable speed. See Section
5
for more details.
In particular, the limit of
u={\rm diag}(u_{1},...,u_{n})
, as
\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\rightarrow+\infty
for all
k=2,...,n-1
and
u_{2}-u_{1}\rightarrow 0
, is a point
u_{\rm cat}
in the
0
-dimensional stratum of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
, called a caterpillar (see
[
69
]
, page 16). There are many caterpillar points, and
u_{\rm cat}
is one of them.

Note that
u
can approach to a same point in the boundary
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})\setminus{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
, for example
u_{\rm cat}
, from different connected components of
{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
. As
u
approaches to a fixed boundary point from two different components, the regularized limits can be different, see Proposition
1.8
. Therefore, when study the regularized limits of Stokes matrices at a boundary point, we should specialize the connected component from which we take the limit. For example, in this paper by saying
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
from
U_{\rm id}
we mean that
u\in U_{\rm id}
and in the meanwhile
\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\rightarrow+\infty
for all
k=2,...,n-1
, and
u_{2}-u_{1}\rightarrow 0+
.

We generalize the previous three theorems and Definition
1.6
from a caterpillar point to a general boundary point (see Section
5.7
for more precise statements with full details).

Theorem 1.7
.

Let us denote by
U_{\sigma}=\{u\in\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})~|~u_{\sigma(1)}<\cdots<u_{\sigma(n)}\}
the connected component of
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
associated to an element
\sigma\in S_{n}
in the permutation group. For any
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
,

(a).

as
u\in{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
approaches to a boundary point
u_{0}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})\setminus{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
from
U_{\sigma}
, the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,A)
of the linear system (
6
) have regularized limits, denoted by
S_{\pm}^{\sigma}(u_{0},A)
(that encodes the first order approximation of
S_{\pm}(u,A)
as
u\rightarrow u_{0}
from
U_{\sigma}
);

(b).

the regularized limits can be expressed by

\displaystyle S_{\pm}^{\sigma}(u_{0},A)=S(u,\Phi_{u_{0}}(u;A)),\ \ \text{for all }\ u\in U_{\sigma},

(10)

where
\Phi_{u_{0}}(u;A)
is the solution of isomonodromy equation (
3
) on
U_{\sigma}
with the prescribed asymptotics parameterized by
A
as
u\rightarrow u_{0}
(i.e., the prescribed boundary value
A
at
u_{0}
);

(c).

more importantly, associated to the boundary point
u_{0}
there exists a collection of linear systems of differential equations with lower ranks and simpler forms, such that the regularized limits
S^{\sigma}_{\pm}(u_{0},A)
are explicitly expressed in terms of the Stokes/connection matrices of the collection of linear systems.

When
u_{0}=u_{\rm cat}
the caterpillar point and
U_{\sigma}=U_{\rm id}
, the
(a),(b)
parts of the theorem recover Theorem
1.5
and Theorem
1.1
. And in this case, the collection of simpler linear systems given in part
(c)
are exact solvable, see Section
3.6.4
. The explicit evaluation of the regularized limits gives rise to the formula in Theorem
1.2
, see Section
3.6.5
. Thus in this special case, part
(c)
recovers Theorem
1.2
.

Like Definition
1.6
, we call the regularized limits
S^{\sigma}_{\pm}(u_{0},A)
as the Stokes matrices at a boundary point
u_{0}
with respect to the choice of
U_{\sigma}
. There are different choices of
\sigma\in S_{n}
such that
u_{0}
is in the closure of the connected component
U_{\sigma}
in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
, therefore we use the upper index
\sigma
in
S^{\sigma}_{\pm}
to stress that the limit is taken as
u
approaches to
u_{0}
from inside of
U_{\sigma}\subset\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
.

Actually, as
u
approaches to a fixed boundary point
u_{0}
from two different components
U_{\sigma}
and
U_{\sigma^{\prime}}
, the regularized limits are in general different. The wall-crossing formula of the Stokes matrices, as
u
crosses the common face of
\overline{U_{\sigma}}
and
\overline{U_{\sigma^{\prime}}}
in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
, is derived, see Section
5.6
for more details.

In particular, for each
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\tau_{i}\in S_{n}
be the permutation reversing the order of the subset
[1,...,i]
of
[1,...,n]
. Then the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
is in the intersection of all the
\overline{U_{\tau_{i}}}
in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
, and

Proposition 1.8

(Wall-crossing formula at
u_{\rm cat}
)
.

Denote by
S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)
and
S_{+}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat},A)
the regularized limits of the Stokes matrices
S_{+}(u,A)
as
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
from
U_{\rm id}
and
U_{\tau_{i}}
respectively. Then we have

\displaystyle S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}S_{i+}&B\\
0&C\end{array}\right)\rightarrow S_{+}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat},A)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}P_{i}S_{i+}^{\dagger}P_{i}^{-1}&P_{i}(S_{i+}S_{i+}^{\dagger})^{-\frac{1}{2}}S_{i+}B\\
0&C\end{array}\right).

where
S_{i+}
are the left-top
i
-th principal submatrices of
S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)
, and
S_{i+}^{\dagger}
is the complex conjugate of
S_{i+}
, and
P_{i}
is the
i\times i
permutation matrix associated to
\tau_{i}
.

The wall-crossing formula is used to realize the known cactus group action on
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystals constructed in representation theory, see Theorem
7.9
. Here the cactus group is the (
S_{n}
-equivariant) fundamental group of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
, see
[
22
]
.

Remark 1.9
.

One should compare the new wall-crossing phenomenon in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
to the known wall-crossing phenomenon in
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{C})
: Stokes matrices
S(u,A)
parameterized by
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{C})
and
A\in\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
depend on the discrete choices of initial Stokes sectors, and as
u
varies in
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{C})
, the configuration of the Stokes sectors and anti-Stokes rays vary accordingly. As
u
crosses some particular real codimensional one wall in
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{C})
, the chosen initial Stokes sector first collapses into a line and then gives birth to a new Stokes sector. This wall-crossing phenomenon amounts to the braid group actions on the space of Stokes matrices. See
[
23
, Appendix F]
and
[
14
]
.

Remark 1.10
.

Theorem
1.7
can be seen as an analytic branching rule of the linear system (
6
), as they "decouple" the linear system (
6
) into the multiple linear systems of lower ranks, according to the branches of the planar tree representing the way in which the components
u_{i}
of
u
collapse (in a comparable speed). The analytic branching rule here is related to the branching rule in representation theory, i.e., the rules decomposing the restriction of an irreducible representation into irreducible representations of the subgroup. See Sections
1.7
and
1.8
for a special case, where, in the WKB approximation, the analytic branching rule at the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
is shown to recover the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis, arsing from the branching rules from
U(k)
to
U(k-1)
for all
2\leq k\leq n
. See Conjecture
1.18
for other boundary point cases and a relation with the eigenbasis of the shift of argument subalgebras. In particular, the analytic branching rule as the two sets
u_{1},...,u_{k}
and
u_{k+1},...,u_{n}
collapse is related to the branching rule for the reduction from
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
to
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{k}\times{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n-k}
.

Remark 1.11
.

The De Concini-Procesi space was introduced to describe the asymptotic behavior of solutions of the Khniznik-Zamolodchikov (KZ) equation, see
[
20
]
. Since the isomonodromy equation (
3
) is a non-linear differential equation closely related to the KZ equation, what we get can be seen a nonlinear analog. However, the nonlinear analysis contains new phenomenon and is much harder, see
[
71
]
.

Remark 1.12
.

By the expressions in Theorem
5.22
of Section
5
, if the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,A)
take very special forms, the limits of the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,A)
exist as some components
u_{i}
of
u
collapse (independent of the relative speed) and coincide with the corresponding one on the boundary
\mathfrak{t}(\mathbb{R})\setminus\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
. It should be related to the work
[
21
]
of Cotti, Dubrovin and Guzzetti, where continuous deformation of
S_{\pm}(u,A)
from
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}
to
\mathfrak{t}
was studied.

The Stokes matrices of the system (
1
)-(
2
) or the confluent hypergeometric system (
6
) have appeared in many fields of mathematics and physics, but the difficult in practical application is that in general they don’t have explicit expression. However, since the problems, to which Stokes phenomenon applies, usually depend on the parameter
u
in an isomonodromy/isospectral way, and the structures in question are preserved under taking the regularized limit of Stokes matrices in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
, thus one can use the closed formula at the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
and then to study the problems via the isomonodromy approach. See the applications in Poisson geometry and representation theory given in the following. Therefore, Theorem
1.2
provides a framework that brings manipulable analysis tools to many seemingly disparate fields, and more importantly, helps to solve some open analysis problems for the confluent hypergeometric system (
6
) and the associated isomonodromy deformation equations. In particular, it enables us to give/understand

•

the first explicit linearization of dual Poisson Lie groups for
U(n)
, see Theorem
1.13
;

•

a realization of crystals in the representation of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
via the WKB approximation of the quantum Stokes matrices, see Theorem
1.22
, and more importantly an algebraic characterization of the WKB approximation by explicit
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystals, see Conjecture
1.18
;

•

a realization of the cactus group actions on the
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystals as a wall-crossing phenomenon of the regularized limit of Stokes matrices in the De Concini-Procesi space
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
, see Theorem
7.9
;

•

the geometry of spectral networks in the WKB approximation of equation (
6
) and its relation with cluster algebras and Cauchy interlacing inequality
[
4
]
;

•

the boundary conditions and the asymptotic expansion of generic
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
valued solutions of the nonlinear isomonodromy deformation equation, which is a generalization of Jimbo’s formula
[
45
]
from the Painlevé VI to higher rank case
[
71
]
. As applications, we construct some algebraic solutions and obtain a connection formula of the nonlinear isomonodromy deformation equation
[
77
,
71
]
.

The first three applications are contributions of this paper, and others are given in follow-up works. In the rest of the introduction, let us give more details on them, with a stress on the fact that the involved structures (either Poisson geometric nature of Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff maps, the quantum groups, or the crystals) we are interested in are preserved under taking the regularized limit of Stokes matrices in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
.

1.4
Application I: the first explicit Ginzburg-Weinstein diffeomorphism

In this subsection, we concern the Poisson geometric nature of the Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff map of the linear system (
6
) initiated by Boalch
[
11
]
. In particular, we prove that the Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff map appeared in Section
1.1
is a Poisson diffeomorphism. As a corollary, we derived the first explicit expression of the Ginzburg-Weinstein linearization.

Following the irregular Atiyah-Bott construction
[
11
,
13
]
, the theory of Stokes matrices can be placed into the context of Poisson/symplectic
geometry. For that, let us consider the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{u}}(n)
of the unitary group
{\rm U}(n)
, consisting of skew-Hermitian matrices, and identify
{\rm Herm}(n)\cong{\mathfrak{u}}(n)^{*}
via the pairing
\langle A,\xi\rangle=2{\rm Im}({\rm tr}A\xi)
. Thus
{\rm Herm}(n)
inherits a Poisson structure from the canonical linear (Kostant-Kirillov-Souriau) Poisson structure on
{\mathfrak{u}}(n)^{*}
. Furthermore, the
unitary group
{\rm U}(n)
carries a standard structure as a Poisson Lie
group (see e.g.
[
58
]
). The dual Poisson Lie group
{\rm U}(n)^{*}
, which is the group of complex
upper triangular matrices with strictly positive diagonal entries,
is identified with the space
{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)
of positive definite Hermitian
n\times n
-matrices, by
taking the upper triangular matrix
X\in{\rm U}(n)^{*}
to the positive Hermitian matrix
(X^{*}X)^{1/2}\in{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)
. The Ginzburg-Weinstein linearization theorem
[
35
]
states that the dual Poisson Lie group
{\rm U}(n)^{*}\cong{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)
is Poisson isomorphic to the dual of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}(n)^{*}\cong{\rm Herm}(n)
.
We remark that the linearization theorem works for any compact Lie group
K
with its standard Poisson structure, and there are various proofs and generalizations of Ginzburg-Weinstein diffeomorphism, from the different perspectives of Moser’s trick in symplectic geometry, Stokes phenomenon, the theory of quantum algebras and so on, see e.g.,
[
1
,
3
,
13
,
14
,
25
]
.

Although there are many proofs of the existence of Ginzburg-Weinstein diffeomorphisms, the explicit expression of such a diffeomorphism was not known before (except
n=2
case). In Section
4.1
, we prove

Theorem 1.13
.

The map

\nu(u_{\rm cat})\colon{\rm Herm}(n)\cong\mathfrak{u}(n)^{*}\rightarrow{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)\cong{\rm U}(n)^{*};\ A\mapsto S_{-}(u_{\rm cat},A)S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A),

is a Poisson isomorphism (here since the Stokes matrices satisfy
S_{-}=S_{+}^{\dagger}
, the conjugate transpose of
S_{+}
, the product
S_{-}S_{+}
is a positive definite Hermitian matrix).

As an immediate corollary of Theorem
1.13
and Theorem
1.2
, we find the first explicit Ginzburg-Weinstein diffeomorphism.

Theorem
1.13
can be understood as an extension of the following theorem of Boalch to a caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
.

Theorem 1.14
.

[
11
]

For any fixed
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, the Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff map (also known as the dual exponential map)

\displaystyle\nu(u)\colon{\rm Herm}(n)\cong\mathfrak{u}(n)^{*}\rightarrow{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)\cong{\rm U}(n)^{*};\ A\mapsto S_{-}(u,A)S_{+}(u,A),

(16)

is a Poisson isomorphism.

In Section
4.2
, we give a "linear algebra" proof of Theorem (
1.14
).
The above maps were first studied in
[
11
]
, whose surjectivity was new (i.e had not been used in the context of Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff before). Using Theorem
1.13
and the Hamiltonian formulation of the isomonodromy deformation equations, in Section
4.2
we give a new proof of the Poisson map part of Theorem
1.14
.

Remark 1.15
.

In
[
3
]
, Alekseev and Meinrenken constructed a distinguished Ginzburg-Weinstein linearization via the Gelfand-Tsetlin integrable systems. As an application of Theorem
1.1
and
1.2
, in a follow up work
[
78
]
, we clarify the relation between
\nu(u)
and the Alekseev-Meinrenken diffeomorphism. Furthermore, we find the explicit expression of the Alekseev-Meinrenken diffeomorphism. See
[
78
]
for more details.

1.5
The quantum case: representations of quantum groups arising from the quantum Stokes matrices

From now on, we switch to the quantum analog of the differential equation (
6
), with a focus on its Stokes phenomenon and WKB approximation. And in the following, we unveil the quantum analogs of all the above results. We first recall that how the representation of quantum groups naturally arises from the Stokes matrices of the quantum confluent hypergeometric equation (
17
), and then deepen the relation between the Stokes phenomenon and representation theory, by proposing a conjecture (and prove an important special case) that the Stokes phenomenon in the WKB approximation of quantum confluent hypergeometric equation is characterized by explicit crystal structures. Here on the one hand, the crystal structures in representation theory were introduced by Kashiwara
[
51
,
52
]
and Lusztig
[
59
]
in the 1990’s. Since then, they have become ubiquitous in algebra and geometry. On the other hand, the characterization of the WKB approximation, as a singular perturbation problem, is still open. So it is rather striking that the crystal structures enable us to study and understand open analysis problems. We mention that a classical analog, i.e., a geometric (instead of a representation theoretic) characterization of the WKB approximation of the (classical) confluent hypergeometric equation (
6
) in terms of integral periods over the associated spectral curves, is studied in
[
4
]
.

Let us take the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}
over the field of complex numbers, and its universal enveloping algebra
U({\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})
generated by
\{e_{ij}\}_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
subject to the relation
[e_{ij},e_{kl}]=\delta_{jk}e_{il}-\delta_{li}e_{kj}
. Let us take the
n\times n
matrix
T=(T_{ij})
with entries valued in
U({\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})

\displaystyle T_{ij}=e_{ij},\ \ \ \ \ \text{for}\ 1\leq i,j\leq n.

Given any finite-dimensional irreducible representation
L(\lambda)
of
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
with a highest weight
\lambda
, let us consider the quantum confluent hypergeometric system

\displaystyle\frac{dF_{h}}{dz}=h\left({\mathrm{i}}u+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{T}{z}\right)\cdot F_{h},

(17)

for
F_{h}(z)\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))\otimes{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
an
n\times n
matrix function with entries in
{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
. Here
{\mathrm{i}}=\sqrt{-1}
,
h
is a complex parameter,
u\in\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{C})
is seen as a
n\times n
matrix with scalar entries in
U({\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})
, and the action of the coefficient matrix on
F_{h}(z)
is given by matrix multiplication and the representation of
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
.

Let us assume
h\notin{\mathrm{i}}\mathbb{Q}
. The equation (
17
) is then nonresonant and thus has a unique formal solution
\hat{F}_{h}(z)
around

z=\infty
(note that the equation can be seen as a block linear system of rank
n\times{\rm dim}(L(\lambda))
, that is a special case of (
6
)). Again the standard theory of resummation states that there exist certain sectorial regions around
z=\infty
, such that on each of these sectors there is a unique (therefore canonical) holomorphic solution with the prescribed asymptotics
\hat{F}
. These solutions are in general different (that reflects the Stokes phenomenon), and the transition between them can be measured by a pair of Stokes matrices
S_{h\pm}(u)\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))\otimes{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
. See
[
76
]
for more details.

Theorem 1.16
.

[
76
]

For any fixed
h\notin{\mathrm{i}}\mathbb{Q}
and
u\in\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{C})
, the map (with
q=e^{h/2}
)

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{q}(u):U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})&\rightarrow{\rm End}(L(\lambda))~;\\
e_{i}&\mapsto\frac{S_{+}(u)_{i,i}^{-1}\cdot S_{+}(u)_{i,i+1}}{q^{-1}-q},\\
f_{i}&\mapsto\frac{S_{-}(u)_{i+1,i}\cdot S_{-}(u)_{i,i}^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}},\\
q^{h_{i}}&\mapsto S_{+}(u)_{i,i}\end{split}

(18)

defines a representation of the Drinfeld-Jimbo quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
on the vector space
L(\lambda)
. Here recall that
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
is a unital associative algebra with generators
q^{\pm h_{i}},e_{j},f_{j},

1\leq j\leq n-1,1\leq i\leq n
and relations:

•

for each
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq j\leq n-1
,

q^{h_{i}}q^{-h_{i}}=q^{-h_{i}}q^{h_{i}}=1,\ q^{h_{i}}e_{j}q^{-h_{i}}=q^{\delta_{ij}}q^{-\delta_{i,j+1}}e_{j},\ q^{h_{i}}f_{j}q^{-h_{i}}=q^{-\delta_{ij}}q^{\delta_{i,j+1}}f_{j};

•

for each
1\leq i,j\leq n-1
,

[e_{i},f_{j}]=\delta_{ij}\frac{q^{h_{i}-h_{i+1}}-q^{-h_{i}+h_{i+1}}}{q-q^{-1}};

•

for
|i-j|=1
,

e_{i}^{2}e_{j}-(q+q^{-1})e_{i}e_{j}e_{i}+e_{j}e_{i}^{2}=0,

f_{i}^{2}f_{j}-(q+q^{-1})f_{i}f_{j}f_{i}+f_{j}f_{i}^{2}=0,

and for
|i-j|\neq 1
,
[e_{i},e_{j}]=0=[f_{i},f_{j}]
.

The theorem associates to any representation
L(\lambda)
of
U(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
a representation
\mathcal{S}_{q}(u)
of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
on the same vector space
L(\lambda)
. In the following, we will call
S_{h\pm}(u)
the quantum Stokes matrices.
In
[
76
]
, the (formal solution and quantum Stokes matrices of) linear system (
17
) is interpreted as a quantization of the (ones of) linear system (
6
) in the framework of deformation quantization. In particular, Theorem
1.16
can be seen as a quantum analog of Theorem
1.14
.

Remark 1.17
.

The study of the quantum Stokes matrices is generalized from the second order pole case to arbitrary order pole cases: in
[
79
]
, a quantum analog of meromorphic linear systems of ODEs with pole of order
k
, as well as its quantum Stokes matrices, is introduced. As for
k=2
, it becomes the equation (
17
). The quantum Stokes matrices at pole of order
k
is then interpreted as a quantization of the space of the classical Stokes matrices.

1.6
The WKB approximation in the Stokes matrices is the crystal limit in the quantum groups

Now let us introduce the most important application of Theorem (
1.2
), i.e., a transcendental realization of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystals in representation theory. The following observation, on a correspondence between the WKB approximation in the differential equation and the crystal limit in quantum groups, is the starting point.

On the one hand, the WKB method, named after Wentzel, Kramers, and Brillouin, is for approximating solutions of a differential equation whose highest derivative is multiplied by a small parameter (other
names, including Liouville, Green, and Jeffreys are sometimes attached to this method). Accordingly, we would like to study directly the asymptotics of
S_{h\pm}(u)
as
h\rightarrow+\infty
along the positive real axis, which describe the Stokes phenomenon in the WKB approximation of (
17
). For that, we fix a lowest vector
\xi_{0}
, and an inner product on
L(\lambda)
given by the conditions

\langle\xi_{0},\xi_{0}\rangle=1
and
\langle e_{ij}v_{1},v_{2}\rangle=\langle v_{1},e_{ji}v_{2}\rangle

for any
v_{1},v_{2}\in L(\lambda)
.

On the other hand, a
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
-crystal (see Definition
7.4
) is a combinatorial object, that is a finite set along with some operators called crystal operators satisfying certain conditions, where the finite set models a weight basis for a representation of
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
, and crystal operators indicate the leading order behaviour of the simple root vectors on the basis under the crystal limit
q\rightarrow\infty
in quantum group
U_{q}({\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})
.

Note that in the realization of quantum
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
via the quantum Stokes matrices
S_{h\pm}(u)
given in Theorem
1.16
, the parameters
q
and
h
are related by
q=e^{h/2}
. Therefore, the WKB leading asymptotics as
h\rightarrow+\infty
(along the positive real axis) corresponds to the crystal limit
q\rightarrow\infty
,

\framebox{WKB approximation of the equation \eqref{introqeq}}\longleftrightarrow\framebox{crystal limit of the quantum group $U_{q}({\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})$}

In the following, let us make the above correspondence precise.

Following the standard process of WKB analysis, the leading asymptotics of solutions encodes the eigenvalues and eigenbasis of the coefficient
({\mathrm{i}}u+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{T}{z})
of equation (
17
). In our case, let us take the shift of argument subalgebra
\mathcal{A}(u)
of
U({\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})
, which is a maximal commutative subalgebras parameterized by
u\in\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{C})
. The action of
\mathcal{A}(u)
on the representation
L(\lambda)
has simple spectral for the real
u\in\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
. We denote by
E(u;\lambda)
an eigenbasis of the action of
\mathcal{A}(u)\subset U(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
on
L(\lambda)
. See e.g.,
[
30
,
39
]
for more details. In the discussion below, let us assume
h\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, and
u\in\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
. The assumption guarantees the existence of the
E(u;\lambda)
.

The action of the off-diagonal entry
S_{h+}(u)_{k,k+1}
of the quantum Stokes matrix on the eigenbasis vectors
\{v_{i}(u)\}_{i\in I}
of
E(u;\lambda)
should have the WKB type asymptotic behaviour as
h\rightarrow+\infty
,

S_{h+}(u)_{k,k+1}\cdot v_{i}(u)=\sum_{j\in I}e^{h\phi^{(k)}_{ij}(u)+{\mathrm{i}}g_{ij}^{(k)}(u,h)}\left(v_{j}(u)+\mathcal{O}(h^{-1})\right),

(19)

where
\phi^{(k)}_{ij}(u)
are real valued functions independent of
h
, and
g_{ij}^{(k)}(u,h)
are real valued functions for all
1\leq i,j\leq k\leq n-1.

An element
v_{i}(u)
of
E(u;\lambda)
is called generic if there exists only one index
j\in I
such that
\phi^{(k)}_{ij}(u)
is the biggest in the collection
\{\phi^{(k)}_{il}(u)\}_{l\in I}
of real numbers.
Thus, the WKB approximation of
S_{h+}(u)_{k,k+1}
naturally defines an operator
\widetilde{e_{k}}
on the generic elements of
E(u;\lambda)
by picking the unique leading term in (
19
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\widetilde{e_{k}}(v_{i}(u)):=v_{j}(u),\ \ \ \text{if}\ \ \ \phi^{(k)}_{ij}(u)={\rm max}\{\phi^{(k)}_{il}(u)~|~{l\in I}\}.

(20)

Similarly, by considering the WKB approximation of
S_{h-}(u)_{k+1,k}
, one defines an operator
\widetilde{f_{k}}
on (some other) generic elements of
E(u;\lambda)
. In a universal sense, the operators
\{\widetilde{e_{k}},\widetilde{f_{k}}\}_{k=1,...,n-1}
uniquely extend to the whole set
E(u;\lambda)
of eigenbasis. See Section
7.2
for more details on the extension for the case of caterpillar point.

In this heuristic spirit, the correspondence between the WKB approximation and the crystal limit predicts that the finite set
E(u;\lambda)
equipped with the operators
\{\widetilde{e_{k}}(u),\widetilde{f_{k}}(u)\}_{k=1,...,n-1}
is a
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystal, i.e., the WKB approximation of the Stokes matrices is characterized by a crystal structure. To be more precise, our conjecture states that (the conjecture is proved in an important special case, see Section
1.8
)

Conjecture 1.18
.

For any
u\in\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
and each
k=1,...,n-1
, there exists canonical operators
\widetilde{e_{k}}(u)
and
\widetilde{f_{k}}(u)
acting on the finite set
E(u;\lambda)
, and real valued functions
c_{kj}(\xi(u))
,
\theta_{kj}(h,u,\xi(u))
with
j=1,2
such that for any generic element
\xi(u)\in E(u;\lambda)
,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm lim}\limits_{h\rightarrow+\infty}\left(S_{h+}(u)_{k,k+1}\cdot e^{c_{k1}(\xi)h+{\mathrm{i}}\theta_{k1}(h,u,\xi)}\xi(u)\right)=\widetilde{e_{k}}(\xi(u)),

(21)

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm lim}\limits_{h\rightarrow+\infty}\left(S_{h-}(u)_{k+1,k}\cdot e^{c_{k2}(\xi)h+{\mathrm{i}}\theta_{k2}(h,u,\xi)}\xi(u)\right)=\widetilde{f_{k}}(\xi(u)).

(22)

Furthermore, the WKB datum
(E(u;\lambda),\widetilde{e_{k}}(u),\widetilde{f_{k}}(u))
is a
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}-
crystal.

Remark 1.19
.

The functions
c_{k1}(\xi)
and
c_{k2}(\xi)
are also determined by the representation theoretic data in the
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
-crystal. But we do not need it in this paper.

Remark 1.20
.

In the literature of KZ equations associated to a simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, see e.g.,
[
29
]
, the parameter
h
equals to
k+h^{\vee}
, where
k
is the level of the representation of the affine Lie algebra
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
and
h^{\vee}
the dual Coxeter number of
\mathfrak{g}
. Then the Stokes phenomenon in the WKB approximation of the equation (
17
) is expected to be related to the theory of representation of
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
at the critical level
k=-h^{\vee}
.

In one of our next papers, an isomonodromy deformation approach to this conjecture is proposed. It decomposes the proof of the conjecture into a problem of quantitative analysis and a problem of qualitative analysis. The quantitative analysis problem is then solved in Theorem
1.22
, and the qualitative one is a pure analysis problem.

1.7
Leading asymptotics of quantum Stokes matrices in terms of Gelfand-Tsetlin basis

This subsection gives the quantum analog of Proposition
1.4
.

Theorem 1.21
.

The leading asymptotics of the off-diagonal entries
S_{h+}(u)_{k,k+1}\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
of
S_{h\pm}(u)
, as
\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\rightarrow+\infty
for all
k=2,...,n-1
, are given by

\displaystyle S_{h+}(u)_{k,k+1}\sim 2\pi{\mathrm{i}}h^{\frac{h\small{(e_{kk}-e_{k+1,k+1}-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}e^{\frac{-h(e_{kk}+e_{k+1,k+1}+1)}{4}}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod_{l=1}^{k+1}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}-1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}-1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\cdot f^{(k)}_{hi}(\Delta_{L})^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(\frac{hT(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right).

Here the singular part

f^{(k)}_{hi}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}(u_{k}-u_{k-1})^{\frac{he_{kk}-h\zeta^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\left({u_{k+1}-u_{k}}\right)^{\frac{h\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-he_{k+1,k+1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}},&\text{if}\ \ k\geq 2,\\
(u_{2}-u_{1})^{\frac{h\zeta^{(1)}_{1}-he_{22}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}},&\text{if}\ \ k=1,\end{array}\right.

(23)

and
(\Delta_{L})^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(\frac{hT(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
and
\zeta^{(k)}_{i}\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
are the quantum analog of the minors and eigenvalues defined as in Definition
6.8
and
6.9
.

In practice, the above formula can be computed under the orthonormal Gelfand-Tsetlin basis. Denote by
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{k}
the subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
spanned by the elements
\{e_{ij}\}_{i,j=1,...,k}
, and denote by the
n
-tuples of numbers
(\lambda^{(n)}_{1},...,\lambda^{(n)}_{n})
parameterizing the highest weight
\lambda
.
Then the orthonormal Gelfand-Tsetlin basis
E_{GT}(\lambda)=\{\xi_{\Lambda}(u_{\rm cat})\}
in
L(\lambda)
, associated to the chain of subalgebras

{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n-1}\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}

is parameterized by the Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns
\Lambda
. Such a pattern
\Lambda
is a collection of numbers
\{\lambda^{(i)}_{j}(\Lambda)\}_{1\leq j\leq i\leq n}
with the fixed
\{\lambda^{(n)}_{k}\}_{k=1,...,n}
satisfying the interlacing conditions

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}^{(i)}(\Lambda)-\lambda^{(i-1)}_{j}(\Lambda)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\hskip 14.22636pt\lambda^{(i-1)}_{j}(\Lambda)-\lambda^{(i)}_{j+1}(\Lambda)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

(24)

The action of the quantum minors and the elements
\{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq k\leq n}
on the basis
{\xi_{\Lambda}}(u_{\rm cat})
of
L(\lambda)
are given in Proposition
6.11
. And we refer the reader to
[
63
]
for a general theory of Gelfand-Tsetlin basis.

Just as in the classical case, there exist regularized limits
S_{h\pm}(u_{\rm cat})
of
S_{h\pm}(u)
as
\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\rightarrow+\infty
, called the quantum Stokes matrices at
u_{\rm cat}
(with respect to the connected component
U_{\rm id}
). In Section
6.2
we give the explicit expression of
S_{h\pm}(u_{\rm cat})
at the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
(see Theorem
6.12
), as a quantum version of the formula in Theorem
1.2
. (Indeed, the formula in Theorem
1.2
is given in terms of the action-angle variables of the classical Gelfand-Tsetlin integrable systems, while the formula in Theorem
1.2
is given in terms of the quantum variables).

More generally, the quantum Stokes matrices
S_{h\pm}(u)
have canonically regularized limits as some components
u_{i}
of
u={\rm diag}(u_{1},...,u_{n})
collapse in a comparable speed, that is controlled by the geometry of the De Concini-Procesi space
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
. Furthermore, Theorem
1.16
can be generalized from
u\in\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
to the boundary points of the De Concini-Procesi space. For example, we refer to Theorem
6.20
, that gives the quantum analog of Theorem (
1.13
).

1.8
Application II: a realization of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystals via the WKB approximation in Stokes phenomenon

Theorem
1.21
enables us to compute explicitly the WKB approximation of quantum Stokes matrices in the limit
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
. In particular, a straightfoward computation verifies the expression (
19
) at the infinite point, and the result leads to a realization of the
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystal via the Stokes phenomenon.

Theorem 1.22
.

For each
k=1,...,n-1
, there exists canonical operators
\widetilde{e_{k}}(u_{\rm cat})
and
\widetilde{f_{k}}(u_{\rm cat})
acting on the finite set
E_{GT}(\lambda)
, and real valued functions
c_{ki}(\xi(u_{\rm cat}))
,
\theta_{ki}(h,u,\xi(u_{\rm cat}))
with
i=1,2
such that such that for any generic element
\xi(u_{\rm cat})\in E_{GT}(\lambda)
,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm lim}\limits_{h\rightarrow+\infty}\left(\mathop{\rm lim}\limits_{u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}\text{ from }U_{\rm id}}S_{h+}(u)_{k,k+1}\cdot e^{c_{k1}(\xi)h+{\mathrm{i}}\theta_{k1}(h,u,\xi)}\xi(u_{\rm cat})\right)=\widetilde{e_{k}}(\xi(u_{\rm cat})),

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm lim}\limits_{h\rightarrow+\infty}\left(\mathop{\rm lim}\limits_{u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}\text{ from }U_{\rm id}}S_{h-}(u)_{k+1,k}\cdot e^{c_{k2}(\xi)h+{\mathrm{i}}\theta_{k2}(h,u,\xi)}\xi(u_{\rm cat})\right)=\widetilde{f_{k}}(\xi(u_{\rm cat})).

Furthermore, the set
E_{GT}(\lambda)
equipped with the operators
\widetilde{e_{k}}(u_{\rm cat})
and
\widetilde{f_{k}}(u_{\rm cat})
is a
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
-crystal, that (under the natural bijection between semistandard Young tableaux and Geland-Testlin patterns) coincides with the known
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystal structure on semistandard Young tableaux.

The shift of argument subalgebras
\mathcal{A}(u)
of
U({\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})
extend from
u\in{\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
to the de Concini-Procesi space
u\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
. In particular, the subalgebra
\mathcal{A}(u)
at
u_{\rm cat}
becomes the Gelfand-Tsetlin subalgebra, and the eigenbasis
E(u_{\rm cat};\lambda)
at
u_{\rm cat}
becomes the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis
E_{GT}(\lambda)
(this is why we denote the basis vector in
E_{GT}(\lambda)
, corresponding to a pattern
\Lambda
, by
\xi_{\Lambda}(u_{\rm cat})
). See e.g.,
[
39
]
for more details. Therefore, Theorem
1.22
proves a limit case of Conjecture
1.18
.

The
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystals are unique, see e.g.,
[
49
, 6.4.21]
, and there are a number of ways to construct them: combinatorially using Littelmann’s path
model
[
55
]
, representation theoretically using crystal bases of a quantum group representation
[
52
]
,
and geometrically using the affine Grassmannian
[
17
]
. See also
[
43
]
for the
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystal structure on semistandard Young tableaux. As far as we know, Theorem
1.22
gives the first transcendental construction of them.

1.9
Application III: cactus group actions on
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystals arising from the wall-crossing phenomenon

Recall that the regularized limits
S_{h\pm}(u_{\rm cat})
simply encode the leading terms of
S_{h\pm}(u)
as
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
from the connected component
U_{\rm id}
(see Section
6.5
for more details). However, as
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
from a different connected component
U_{\sigma}=\{u\in\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})~|~u_{\sigma(1)}<\cdots<u_{\sigma(n)}\}
, the regularized limits are given by different
S_{h\pm}^{\sigma}(u_{\rm cat})\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))\otimes{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
. And the different regularized limits
S_{h\pm}(u_{\rm cat})
and
S_{h\pm}^{\sigma}(u_{\rm cat})
are related by an explicit wall-crossing formula (with respect to the codimension one common face of (the closure of)
U_{\rm id}
and
U_{\sigma}
in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
).

In particular, for each
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\tau_{i}\in S_{n}
be the permutation reversing the segment
[1,...,i]
. Then the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
sets in the codimension one common face of the closure
\overline{U_{\rm id}}
and
\overline{U_{\tau_{i}}}
. Similar to Theorem
1.22
, the WKB approximation of
S_{h\pm}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})
also leads to a
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystal

\left(E_{GT}(\lambda),\{\widetilde{e_{k}}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})\}_{k},\{\widetilde{f_{k}}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})\}_{k}\right),

but with a different set of crystal operators. Then a straightforward computation shows that the wall-crossing formula (relating
S_{h\pm}(u_{\rm cat})
and
S_{h\pm}^{\sigma}(u_{\rm cat})
) in the WKB approximation generate the cactus group
Cact_{n}
action on the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis, first introduced by Berenstein-Kirillov
[
8
]
, see also
[
39
]
. Here
the cactus group
Cact_{n}
appeared in the work of Davis-Januszkiewicz-Scott
[
22
]
as the
S_{n}
-equivariant fundamental group of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
. That is (see Section
7.5
)

Theorem 1.23
.

The operators
\{\rho_{i}\}_{i=1,...,n-1}
on the finite set of Gelfand-Tsetlin basis
E_{GT}(\lambda)
, uniquely determined by the identities

\displaystyle\rho_{k}\circ\widetilde{e_{k}}=\widetilde{e_{k}}^{\tau_{i}}\circ\rho_{k},\text{ for all }k=1,...,n-1,

(25)

generate the known cactus group
Cact_{n}
action.

In the theory of crystals,
the cactus group plays a role analogous to that of the braid group in representations of the quantum group. The famous Drinfeld-Kohno theorem shows that the action of braid group can
be realized as the monodromy of the Knizhnik-Zamalodchikov equation, see
[
72
]
. Theorem
1.23
can be seen as a Drinfeld-Kohno type theorem in the WKB/crystal approximation.

1.10
Witham dynamics and Halacheva-Kamnitzer-Rybnikov-Weekes covers

Although it is only a limit case, the (computation of) WKB datum at
u_{\rm cat}
, given in Theorem
1.21
and Theorem
1.22
, provides an applicable model for general
u
via the isomonodromy deformation, as explained in the following. The discussion below is based on Conjecture
1.18
.

In Section
6.6
, the system of PDEs of
S_{h\pm}(u)
with respect to
u
, controlling the variation of the irregular data
u
, is given. It is equivalent to the isomonodromic deformation equation of the linear system (
17
). Following a general principal, an isomonodromic deformation equation in the WKB approximation degenerates into the combination of a fast (isospectral deformation) dynamics and a slow (Whitham) dynamics. See e.g.,
[
70
]
and
[
53
, Section 7]
. In the classical case, the spectral data is encoded by the underlying spectral curves. And in our quantum case, the spectral data is replaced by the eigenbasis
E(u;\lambda)
.

By (
19
) the
h\rightarrow+\infty
limit of the dynamics of
S_{h\pm}(u)
with respect to
u
degenerates into a slow variation of the eigenbasis
v_{j}(u)^{\prime}s
(the Whitham dynamics that changes the spectral data) and a fast spin
{\mathrm{i}}g_{ij}^{(k)}(u,h)
on the basis
v_{j}(u)^{\prime}s
(the fast isospectral spin that preserves the spectral data). For example, at the caterpillar point, the fast spin terms are given in (
23
) in the explicit expression of the quantum Stokes matrices.

Therefore, the slow/Whitham dynamics part of the variation of
u
, in the WKB approximation of the quantum Stokes matrices, should give rise to a variation of the WKB datum
(E(u;\lambda),\widetilde{e_{k}}(u),\widetilde{f_{k}}(u))
over any connected component
U_{\sigma}
of
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
. And the discrete combinatorial structure encoded in the WKB datum should be locally independent of
u\in U_{\sigma}
, i.e., if
u_{\rm cat}\in\overline{U_{\sigma}}
the closure, then

\left(E(u;\lambda),\widetilde{e_{k}}(u),\widetilde{f_{k}}(u)\right)\cong\left(E_{GT}(\lambda),\widetilde{e_{k}}(u_{\rm cat}),\widetilde{f_{k}}(u_{\rm cat})\right).

The quantum Stokes matrices
S_{h\pm}(u)
have regularized limits as
u\in U_{\sigma}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
approaches to a boundary point in the closure
\overline{U_{\sigma}}\subset\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
, and the regularization terms are fast spin on the eigenbasis
E(u;\lambda)
. For example, following Theorem
1.21
as
u\in U_{\rm id}
approaches to the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
, the blow up terms (
23
) are just fast spin on the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis. In general, the WKB datum
(E(u;\lambda),\widetilde{e_{k}}(u),\widetilde{f_{k}}(u))
extends from any connected component
U_{\sigma}
to its closure
\overline{U_{\sigma}}
in the space
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
.

Therefore, on each connected component
U_{\sigma}
we have a cover whose fibre at
u
is given by the finite set
E(u;\lambda)
. And along the common face of (the closure of) two different connected components
U_{\sigma}
and
U_{\sigma^{\prime}}
, the finite set
E(u;\lambda)
, treated as two different extensions from either
u\in U_{\sigma}
or
u\in U_{\sigma^{\prime}}
, glue according to the wall-crossing formula of the Stokes matrices. For example, let us consider the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
setting in the codimension one common face of
U_{\rm id}
and
U_{\tau_{i}}
, where
\tau_{i}\in S_{n}
is the permutation reversing the segment
[1,...,i]
. Then by Theorem
1.23
we should have the following commutative diagram, that shows the two covers by the finite set
E(u;\lambda)\cong E_{GT}(\lambda)
over
\overline{U_{\rm id}}
and
\overline{U_{\tau_{i}}}
glue together along the common face by the operator
\rho_{i}:E_{GT}(\lambda)\rightarrow E_{GT}(\lambda)

\begin{CD}S_{h\pm}(u)@>{\text{as $u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}$ from $U_{\rm id}$}}>{}>S_{h\pm}(u_{\rm cat})\overset{\text{wall-crossing formula}}{\Longleftrightarrow}S^{\sigma}_{h\pm}(u_{\rm cat})@<{\text{as $u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}$ from $U_{\sigma}$}}<{}<S_{h\pm}(u)\\
@V{\text{WKB datum}}V{}V@V{\text{WKB datum}}V{}V@V{\text{WKB datum}}V{}V\\
(E(u;\lambda),\widetilde{e_{k}}(u))@>{\text{as $u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}$ from $U_{\rm id}$}}>{}>(E(u_{\rm cat};\lambda)),\widetilde{e_{k}}(u_{\rm cat}))\overset{\text{cactus group action}}{\Longleftrightarrow}(E(u_{\rm cat};\lambda)),\tilde{e}^{\sigma}_{k}(u_{\rm cat}))@<{\text{as $u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}$ from $U_{\sigma}$}}<{}<(E(u;\lambda),\widetilde{e_{k}}(u))\end{CD}

In this way, the Whitham dynamics, that describes the variation of the spectral data
v_{j}(u)^{\prime}s
with respect to
u
in the
h\rightarrow+\infty
/WKB approximation of the isomonodromy deformation, leads to a cover of the space
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
whose fibre at any
u
is
E(u;\lambda)
and is equipped with a
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystal structure. Then by Theorem
1.23
and Proposition
7.12
, the monodromy of the cover is given by the known cactus group action (see Section
7.5
) arising from representation theory.

In
[
39
]
, Halacheva, Kamnitzer, Rybnikov and Weekes (HKRW) constructed crystal structures on the set of eigenlines of shift of argument subalgebras
\mathcal{A}(u)
, and defined a covering of the space
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
by the eigenlines. They proved that the monodromy representation of the cover, with respect to the base point
u_{\rm cat}
, coincides with the Berenstein-Kirillov cactus group action on
E_{GT}(\lambda)
. Therefore, this subsection interprets the HKRW cover over
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
as a local system arsing from the Whitham dynamics in the WKB approximation of the differential equation (
17
).

The construction of
[
39
]
works for any simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. One can ask the similar relation between the
\mathfrak{g}
-crystals and the WKB approximation of quantum Stokes matrices associated to
\mathfrak{g}
. Furthermore, using the same idea, we expect that various wall-crossing type formula in representation theory can be interpreted via Stokes phenomenon. For example, motivated by
[
39
, Conjecture 1.14]
, we expect that the cactus group action on the Weyl group constructed by Losev
[
57
]
, via perverse equivalences coming from wall-crossing functors in category
\mathcal{O}
(a more elementary definition was given shortly
afterwards by Bonnaf
\rm\acute{e}

[
16
]
), can be realized via the stokes phenomenon of the affine KZ equations introduced by Cherednik
[
19
]
.

1.11
A continuous path connecting the canonical basis and the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis

Let
\mathbb{U}^{+}
be the subalgebra of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
generated by the elements
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1,...,n-1}
, and let
\mathbb{B}

be the canonical basis in
\mathbb{U}^{+}
. We refer the reader to
[
59
]
for the construction of
\mathbb{B}
. Upon acting on the lowest weight vector, the image of the canonical basis
\mathbb{B}
, under the map
\mathcal{S}_{q}(u)
given in Theorem
1.16
, defines a set
B_{q}(u;\lambda)
of vectors in
L(\lambda)
.

Conjecture 1.24
.

The set
B_{q}(u;\lambda)
is a basis of
L(\lambda)
for all
q\in(0,\infty)
, whose leading asymptotics as
q\rightarrow\infty
correspond to an eigenbasis
E(u;\lambda)
of the action of the shift of argument subalgebra
\mathcal{A}(u)\subset U(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
on
L(\lambda)
.

In the case
n=2
, the conjecture can be verified directly using the closed formula of the Stokes matrices. In the case
n=3
the canonical basis is given explicitly in
[
59
, Example 3.4]
. Then using the method of isomonodromy deformation, Conjecture
1.24
for
\mathfrak{gl}_{3}
can be verified using the properties of the Painlevé VI function.

On the one hand, as
q=1
,
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
becomes the undeformed
U(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
, and the representation in (
18
) coincides with the given representation of
U(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
on
L(\lambda)
. On the other hand, by specializing
q=1
and acting on
L(\lambda)
, the canonical basis
\mathbb{B}
of
\mathbb{U}^{+}
recovers the canonical basis
B(\lambda)
in
L(\lambda)
, i.e.,
B_{q=1}(u;\lambda)
coincides with
B(\lambda)
. Thus, the conjecture enables us to get a one parameter family of basis
B_{q}(u;\lambda)
connecting the canonical basis
B(\lambda)
and eigenbasis
E(u;\lambda)
in
L(\lambda)
, by varying
q=e^{\pi{\mathrm{i}}h}
from
1
to
\infty
(i.e., varying
h
from
0
to
+\infty
along the real axis). In particular, as for the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
we expect to get a
q
-continuous family of basis
B_{q}(u_{\rm cat};\lambda)
connecting the canonical basis
B(\lambda)
and the Gelfand-Testlin basis
E_{GT}(\lambda)
.

1.12
Extension to
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
valued solutions and higher rank Painlevé transcendents

The purpose of this subsection is to explain the importance of the results in Section
1.1
from the viewpoint of the theory of Painlevé transcendents. It outlines some results in the follow-up papers
[
71
,
74
,
76
]
.

Following Miwa
[
62
]
, the
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-valued solutions
\Phi(u)
of the equation (
3
) with
u_{1},...,u_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
have the strong Painlevé property. According to the original idea of Painlevé, they can be a new class of special functions. Indeed, they arise from and play important roles in various branches of mathematics and physics. In the following, let us interpret
\Phi(u)
as higher rank Painlevé transcendents, and outline how Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
can be used to unveil many basic properties of the higher rank Painlevé transcendents.

First, note that the expression of the Stokes matrices given in Theorem
1.2
is an analytic function of
\Phi_{0}
for all
\Phi_{0}\in\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
(not just
{\rm Herm}(n))
satisfying the condition

|{\rm Im}(\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(\Phi_{0})-\lambda^{(k)}_{j}(\Phi_{0}))|<2\pi,\quad\text{for every $1\leq i,j\leq k\leq n$}.

(26)

Furthermore, one checks the Riemann-Hilbert map

\nu:~\Big\{\Phi_{0}\in\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})~|~\Phi_{0}\text{ satisfies \eqref{boundcondtion}}\Big\}\rightarrow\Big\{S_{-}(u,\Phi(u;\Phi_{0}))S_{+}(u,\Phi(u;\Phi_{0}))\in{\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\Big\}

is one to one onto an open dense subset of
{\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, therefore of the space of all possible Stokes matrices. For example, it can be seen from compatibility of
the map
\nu
with the (complex) Gelfand-Tsetlin systems, i.e., (the complex version of) Proposition
4.1
.
Then by "analytical continuation", Theorem
1.1
can be generalized to
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
case: that is if
\Phi(u)
is a generic
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
valued solutions of (
3
), then its asymptotics can be parameterized by a constant boundary value
\Phi_{0}
satisfying the boundary condition (
26
). A precise such statement is proved in our follow up work
[
71
]
, by studying the complete series expansion of generic
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
valued solution
\Phi(u)
. And Theorem
1.2
is true simply by analytical continuation.

Then, let us give a brief introduction to the Painlevé transcendents. The six classical Painlevé equations were introduced at the turn of the twentieth century by Painlevé
[
66
]
and Gambier
[
33
]
, in a specific
classification problem for second order ODEs. Since then, they have appeared in the integrable nonlinear PDEs, 2D Ising models, random matrices, topological field theory and so on.
We refer the reader to the book of Fokas, Its, Kapaev and Novokshenov
[
31
]
for a thorough introduction to the history and developments of the study of Painlevé equations. In particular, the Painlevé VI equation is the nonlinear differential equation

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}y}{dx^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Big[\frac{1}{y}+\frac{1}{y-1}+\frac{1}{y-x}\Big](\frac{dy}{dx})^{2}-\Big[\frac{1}{x}+\frac{1}{x-1}+\frac{1}{y-x}\Big]\frac{dy}{dx}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{y(y-1)(y-x)}{x^{2}(x-1)^{2}}\Big[\alpha+\beta\frac{x}{y^{2}}+\gamma\frac{x-1}{(y-1)^{2}}+\delta\frac{x(x-1)}{(y-x)^{2}}\Big],\ \alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\mathbb{C}.

A solution
y(x)
has
0,1,\infty
as critical points, and can be analytically
continued to a meromorphic function on the universal covering of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
.
As stressed in
[
31
,
44
]
, the solutions of Painlevé equations (called Painlevé transcendents) are seen as nonlinear special functions, because they play the same role in nonlinear mathematical physics as that of classical special functions, like Airy functions, Bessel functions, etc., in linear physics. And it is the answers of the following questions that make Painlevé transcendents as efficient in applications as linear special functions (here we list some of them, see
[
31
,
44
]
and the references therein for more details):

(a).

The parametrization of Painlevé transcendents by their asymptotic behaviour at critical points;

(b).

The explicit expression of the monodromy of the associated linear problem via the parametrization at critical points;

(c).

The construction of the connection
formula from one critical point to another.

In particular, the problems were solved in generic case by Jimbo
[
45
]
. As shown by Harnad
[
41
]
(see also
[
60
]
,
[
15
, Section 3]
for a detailed way to do the Harnad duality), that Painlevé VI is equivalent to the equation (
3
) for
n=3
with suitable matrices
\Phi(u)
. In this way, the generic solutions
\Phi(u)
of the isomonodromy equation (
3
) can be seen as higher rank Painlevé transcendents.

Note that (the complex version of) Theorem
1.1
and
1.2
already give answers to problem
(a)
and
(b)
for the transcendents
\Phi(u)
. Actually, for
n=3
, Theorem
1.1
and
1.2
exactly recover Jimbo’s formula for Painlevé VI, see
[
74
]
for details.
And just like the expression of Stokes matrices of linear differential equations with small ranks
2
or
3
in terms of the asymptotics of the solutions of the associated nonlinear isomonodromy equations has been a major tool in the analysis of Painlevé transcendents, see e.g.,
[
15
,
60
]
and the book
[
31
]
, the Theorem
1.1
and
1.2
lay a foundation for our study of the higher rank Painlevé transcendents
\Phi(u)
. For example, we have used Theorem
1.1
and
1.2
to give partial answers to the above problem
(c)
and boundary conditions for
\Phi(u)
, see e.g,
[
71
,
77
]
.

As another example, let us outline how to use Theorem
1.2
to find algebraic solutions of the isomonodromy equation (
3
). Let us first recall the case
n=3
: based on Jimbo’s formula, the algorithm in
[
24
,
13
,
15
]
derives various algebraic solutions of the Painlevé VI. See
[
13
, Section 5]
for an important and detailed example of the algorithm. These in turn give algebraic solutions
\Phi(u)
of (
50
) in the case of rank
n=3
.

Paralelly, let us consider a general
n
case. We know from Miwa’s theorem that the matrix function
\Phi(u)
is meromorphic on the universal covering of
\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus\Delta
. Continuation along closed paths in the deformation space interchanges the branches of
\Phi(u)
. And such monodromy of the nonlinear isomonodromy equation is explicitly given in terms of the geometric terms, i.e., by an explicit braid group action on the corresponding Stokes matrices. See
[
23
,
14
]
.
In particular, if a solution
\Phi(u)
of the isomonodromy equation (
50
) with the given Stokes matrix
S_{\pm}(u,\Phi(u))
is an algebraic function with branching along the diagonals
u_{i}=u_{j}
only if
S_{\pm}
belong to a finite orbit of the action of
the pure braid group. See e.g.,
[
23
, Appendix F]
.

Therefore, we have the following systematic way to find the algebraic solutions
\Phi(u)
,

\Big\{\text{Stokes matrices $S_{\pm}(u,\Phi(u;\Phi_{0}))$}\Big\}\Longrightarrow\Big\{\text{boundary value}\ \Phi_{0}\Big\}\Longrightarrow\Big\{\text{solution}\ \Phi(u;\Phi_{0})\Big\}.

(27)

The algorithm is as follows: starting from such a pair of explicit
S_{\pm}
that belong to a finite orbit, by Theorem
1.2
we can get the leading term (boundary value
\Phi_{0}
) in the multivariable Puiseux expansion of the solution
\Phi(u;\Phi_{0})
. Following
[
71
]
, the boundary value
\Phi_{0}
determines explicitly the series expansion of the corresponding solution. Substituting back the leading term into the series expansion of the solutions of isomonodromy equation would determine, algebraically, any desired term in the multivariable Puiseux expansion, and then the solution itself. See our next work on the algebraic solutions
\Phi(u)
for more details.

In summary, given the many known applications and connections to other subjects, we believe that the higher Painlevé transcendents
\Phi(u)
have richer structures and applications remained to be found. And just like Painlevé VI case, we expect that the answers to the above problems
(a)-(c)
for
\Phi(u)
will play crucial
roles in other problems from mathematical physics.

The organization of the paper is as follows. Section
2
gives the preliminaries of Stokes data of meromorphic linear systems. Section
3
studies the boundary values/asymptotics of the solutions of the isomonodromy equation, and then derives the expression of Stokes matrices of the associated linear system via the boundary values, i.e., Theorem
1.2
. That is the first main result of this paper. Section
4
shows some applications of the analysis results in Poisson geometry, including the explicit Ginzburg-Weinstein diffeomorphism and a new proof of Theorem
1.14
. Section
5
introduces the De Concini-Procesi space, and studies the
regularized limit of Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,A)
as the irregular data
u
approaches to a boundary point of the De Concini-Procesi space. Section
6
introduces the quantum Stokes matrices of the quantum confluent hypergeometric differential equation, and obtains the expression of their regularized limits at caterpillar points, i.e., Theorem
1.21
. In the end, Section
7
gives the second main result, that is a transcendental realization of the crystals and the cactus group actions via the WKB approximation.

2
Stokes phenomenon and monodromy data

In Section
2.1
, we construct the canonical solutions of the meromorphic linear systems of differential equations (
6
), and prove a uniform property of their asymptotics. In Section
2.9
, we introduce the Stokes matrices and connection matrices of the linear systems, as well as the monodromy relation relating connection matrices to Stokes matrices.

2.1
Canonical solutions

Let
\mathfrak{h}(\mathbb{R})
(resp.
\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
) denote the set of diagonal matrices with (resp. distinct) real eigenvalues. Let us consider the meromorphic linear system

\displaystyle\frac{dF}{dz}=\left({\mathrm{i}}u-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{A}{z}\right)\cdot F,

(28)

where
F(z)
is valued in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
u\in\mathfrak{h}(\mathbb{R})
and
A\in{{\rm Herm}}(n)
. The system has an order two pole at
\infty
and (if
A\neq 0
) a first order pole at
0
.

Proposition 2.1
.

The system has a unique formal fundamental solution taking the form

\displaystyle\hat{F}(z;u,A)=({\rm Id}_{n}+\hat{H}(z^{-1};u,A))\cdot e^{{\mathrm{i}}uz}z^{-\frac{[A]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}},

(29)

where
\hat{H}(z;u,A)=\sum_{k\geq 1}H_{k}(u,A)z^{k}
is a
n\times n
matrix-valued formal power series. (Here for the convenience, we transfer the power series at
z=\infty
to the series at
z=0
after the change of variable
z\mapsto 1/z
.)

Proof.

Actually, by plugging the expression (
29
) in the equation, one checks that the coefficients
H_{k}
are determined by the recursive relation

\displaystyle[{\mathrm{i}}u,H_{k+1}]=\frac{[A]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\cdot H_{k}-H_{k}\cdot\frac{A}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}+kH_{k},\ \text{for all}\ k\geq 0,

(30)

here we assume
H_{0}={\rm Id}_{n}
. Actually, the relation (
30
) can be rewritten, in terms of the components of
A=(a_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}
and
H_{k}=(H_{k,ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}
, as: for
i\neq j

({\mathrm{i}}u_{i}-{\mathrm{i}}u_{j})H_{m+1,ij}=kH_{m,ij}+\sum_{l=1}^{n}\frac{a_{il}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}H_{m,lk}-H_{m,ij}\frac{a_{jj}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}},

(31)

and for
i=j
(replacing
k
by
k+1
in (
30
)),

\displaystyle 0=\sum_{l=1,l\neq i}^{n}\frac{a_{il}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}H_{k+1,li}+(k+1)H_{k+1,ii}.

(32)

Due to the assumption that the diagonal elements of
u
are distinct, we see that the off-diagonal part of the matrix
H_{k+1}
is uniquely determined by
H_{k}
from (
31
), and then the diagonal part of
H_{k+1}
is uniquely determined by (
32
).

Definition 2.2
.

The
Stokes sectors
of the system
are the right/left half planes
{\rm Sect}_{\pm}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}~|~\pm{\rm Re}(z)>0\}
.

For any two real numbers
a,b
, an open sector and a closed sector with opening
angle
b-a>0
are respectively denoted by

S(a,b):=\{z\in\mathbb{C}~|~a<{\rm arg}(z)<b\},\hskip 14.22636pt\bar{S}(a,b):=\{z\in\mathbb{C}~|~a\leq{\rm arg}(z)\leq b\}.

For any
d>0
, let
