\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R}):=\{u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})~|~|u_{i}-u_{j}|>d,\ \text{for all}\ i\neq j,\ i,j=1,...,n\}

denote the subset of
\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, consisting of all
u
whose components keep a fixed positive distance
d
from each other.

Let us choose the branch of
{\rm log}(z)
, which is real on the positive real axis, with a cut along the nonnegative imaginary axis
{\mathrm{i}}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Then by convention,
{\rm log}(z)
has imaginary part
-\pi
on the negative real axis in
{\rm Sect}_{-}
.

Theorem 2.3
.

For any
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, on
{\rm Sect}_{\pm}
there
is a unique fundamental solution
F_{\pm}:{\rm Sect}_{\pm}\to{\rm GL}(n,\mathbb{C})
of equation (
28
) such that
F_{+}(z;u,A)\cdot e^{-{\mathrm{i}}uz}\cdot z^{\frac{[A]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}
and
F_{-}(z;u,A)\cdot e^{-{\mathrm{i}}uz}\cdot z^{\frac{[A]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}
can be analytically continued to
S(-\pi,\pi)
and
S(-2\pi,0)
respectively, and for any small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}F_{+}(z;u,A)\cdot e^{-{\mathrm{i}}uz}\cdot z^{\frac{[A]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Id}_{n},\ \ \ \text{as}\ \ \ z\in\bar{S}(-\pi+\varepsilon_{0},\pi-\varepsilon_{0}),

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}F_{-}(z;u,A)\cdot e^{-{\mathrm{i}}uz}\cdot z^{\frac{[A]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Id}_{n},\ \ \ \text{as}\ \ \ z\in\bar{S}(-2\pi+\varepsilon_{0},-\varepsilon_{0}),

Here
{\rm Id}_{n}
is the rank n identity matrix, and
[A]
is the diagonal part of
A
.
Furthermore, the above limits are uniform for
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.
The construction of the canonical solutions via the Laplace-Borel transforms is standard, see e.g.,
[
5
,
7
,
56
,
61
]
. The new phenomenon in our case is the uniform property with respect to
u
, which relies on the fact that all components
u_{i}
of
u
lie in the same line. Since the uniform property will be used in the proof of Proposition
3.12
, in the following, we will review the construction of the canonical solutions with a stress on the uniform property with respect to
u
. We will divide the construction into three parts, i.e., the analytic property of Borel transform of formal solutions, the analytic property of the Laplace-Borel transform, then the construction of canonical solutions.

Borel transform.

Denote by
\tilde{H}=\mathcal{B}(\hat{H})
the formal Borel transform of
\hat{H}-1
(in the Borel plane with complex variable
\xi
), i.e.,

\tilde{H}(\xi;u,A):=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{H_{k}(u,A)}{\Gamma(k)}\xi^{k-1}.

In the rest of the proof, let us fix a positive
d>0
. Let us also take a matrix norm that is sub-multiplicative, i.e.,
|BC|\leq|B|\cdot|C|
for two matrices
B
and
C
. Then it follows from the formula (
30
) that

Lemma 2.4
.

There exists a constant
\tilde{K}>0
such that
|H_{k}|/\Gamma(k)\leq\tilde{K}^{k-1}
for
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.
Let us denote by
H_{k}^{d}
and
H_{k}^{od}
the diagonal and the off-diagonal part of the
n\times n
matrix
H_{k}
.
On the one hand, note that the entries of
[{\mathrm{i}}u,H_{k}]
takes the form
({\mathrm{i}}u_{i}-{\mathrm{i}}u_{j})H_{k,ij}
. Since
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R})
, we have
|{\mathrm{i}}u_{i}-{\mathrm{i}}u_{j}|>d
. Thus there exists a constant
\tilde{c}>0
such that

\displaystyle|H_{k}^{od}|\leq\tilde{c}\cdot|[u,M]|,\ \text{for all}\ u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R}).

(33)

On the other hand, assume
|A/2\pi{\mathrm{i}}|=a
, then (
32
) implies that

\displaystyle|H^{d}_{k}|\leq\frac{a}{k+1}|H^{od}_{k}|<|H^{od}_{k}|,\ \ \text{for k large enough}.

(34)

By (
33
) and (
34
) we have

|H_{k}|\leq|H_{k}^{d}|+|H_{k}^{od}|\leq 2|H_{k}^{od}|\leq 2\tilde{c}\cdot|[u,H_{k}]|,\ \text{for $k$ large and}\ u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R}).

Set
t_{k}=H_{k}/\Gamma(k)
and racall that
a=|A/2\pi{\mathrm{i}}|
, it follows from (
30
) that

t_{k}\leq 2\tilde{c}(2a+1)t_{k-1}.

Since
a
and
\tilde{c}
are independent of the choices of
k
and
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R})
, we see that taking
\tilde{K}=2\tilde{c}(2a+1)
verifies the Lemma.

Thus the Borel series
\tilde{H}(\xi)
is convergent in a small neighborhood
B_{\rho}(0):=\{\xi\in\mathbb{C}~|~|\xi|<\rho\}
of
\xi=0
. Furthermore,

Lemma 2.5
.

Given any number
\theta\in(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2})\cup(-\frac{3\pi}{2},-\frac{\pi}{2})
, there exists a sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, such that the Borel sum
\tilde{H}(\xi)
can be analytically continued to
S(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon)
and there exist constants
\alpha,\beta>0
such that

|\tilde{H}(\xi;u,A)|\leq\alpha e^{\beta|\xi|},\ \text{ for all }\ \xi\in S(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon)\ \ \text{and}\ u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R}).

Proof.
Recall that
\tilde{H}(\xi;u,A)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{H_{k}(u)}{\Gamma(k)}\xi^{k-1}
. The identities (
30
) are formally equivalent to the integral equation

\tilde{H}\cdot{\mathrm{i}}u-({\mathrm{i}}u-\xi{\rm Id}_{n})\cdot\tilde{H}=\frac{[A]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{A}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}+\int_{t=0}^{\xi}\left(\tilde{H}(t)\cdot\frac{[A]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{A}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\cdot\tilde{H}(t)\right)dt.

(35)

To study the integral equation, we employ an iteration, by beginning
\tilde{H}^{(0)}(\xi;u,A)\equiv 0
, and plugging
\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi;u,A)
into the right hand side of (
35
) and determining
\tilde{H}^{(m+1)}(\xi;u,A)
from the left hand side. The sequences so obtained
are holomorphic near
\xi=0
, and the limit of
\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi;u,A)
as
m\rightarrow\infty
coincides with the convergent Borel series
\tilde{H}(\xi)
for all
\xi\in B_{\rho}
.

Now let
\varepsilon>0
be small enough such that
S(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon)
do not overlap with the purely imaginary axis.
Since the entries of
M\cdot{\mathrm{i}}u-({\mathrm{i}}u-\xi{\rm Id}_{n})\cdot M
of any
n\times n
matrix
M
takes the form
({\mathrm{i}}u_{j}-{\mathrm{i}}u_{i}+\xi)M_{ij}
, we can choose a big enough constant
c>0
such that the off-diagonal part
M^{od}
of
M
satisfies

\displaystyle|M^{od}|\leq c\cdot|M\cdot{\mathrm{i}}u-({\mathrm{i}}u-\xi{\rm Id}_{n})\cdot M|,

(36)

for all
n\times n
matrices
M
,
\xi\in S_{>\rho^{\prime}}(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon)
, and
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R})
.
Here
c
can be chosen, because
u_{1},...,u_{n}
lie on the same line, and the differences
{\mathrm{i}}u_{j}-{\mathrm{i}}u_{i}
for all
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R})
keep a fixed positive distance from the sector
S(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon)
.

Set
a=|A/2\pi{\mathrm{i}}|
. Let
\rho^{\prime}>0
be a fixed small enough positive number such that
\rho>\rho^{\prime}>0
and
\frac{2c}{a^{2}}>\rho^{\prime}
. Let us introduce the region
S_{\geq\rho^{\prime}}(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon):=\{\xi\in S(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon)~|~|\xi|\geq\rho^{\prime}\}.

In terms of the components
\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi)_{ij}
, the integral equation (
35
) can be written as

\displaystyle({\mathrm{i}}u_{j}-{\mathrm{i}}u_{i}+\xi)\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi)_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{a_{ij}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}+\int_{t=0}^{\xi}\left(\tilde{H}^{(m-1)}(t)\cdot\frac{[A]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{A}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\cdot\tilde{H}^{(m-1)}(t)\right)_{ij}dt\ \ \text{ for }i\neq j,

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi)_{ii}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\xi}\int_{t=0}^{\xi}\sum_{l=1,l\neq i}^{n}\frac{a_{il}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\cdot\tilde{H}^{(m)}(t)_{lj}dt.

Then we prove inductively that for all
\xi
in the small ball
B_{\rho^{\prime}}(0)
and for all integer
m\geq 0
,

|\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi)^{od}|\leq\frac{ac}{1-2ca\rho^{\prime}(a+1)},\ \ \text{ and }\ \ |\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi)^{d}|\leq\frac{a^{2}c}{1-2ca\rho^{\prime}(a+1)}.

Set
\alpha={\rm Max}(\frac{a}{\rho^{\prime}},\frac{ac+a^{2}c}{1-2ca\rho^{\prime}(a+1)})
. Then we have for all
\xi\in B_{\rho^{\prime}}(0)
and
m\geq 0
,

|\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi)|\leq\alpha.

(37)

We are now ready to show that each
\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi)
has exponential growth of order
1
in
S(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon)
.

Set
c^{\prime}={\rm Max}(c,\frac{1}{\rho^{\prime}})
.
Let us introduce the sequence of positive real numbers
t^{(m)}_{k}
with
m,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
determined by the recursive relation

\displaystyle t^{(0)}_{k}

\displaystyle=0,\ \text{ for all }k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

(38)

\displaystyle t^{(m)}_{1}

\displaystyle=\alpha,\ \text{ for all }m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},

(39)

\displaystyle t^{(m+1)}_{k}

\displaystyle=2ac^{\prime}\cdot t^{(m)}_{k-1},\ \text{ for all }m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

(40)

Then let us inductively show
estimates of the form

|\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi;u,A)|\leq\sum_{k\geq 1}{t^{(m)}_{k}}|\xi|^{k-1}/\Gamma(k).

(41)

For
m=0
, since
\tilde{H}^{(0)}(\xi;u,A)\equiv 0
, the estimate
(
41
)
is true. Suppose the estimate is true for all
\tilde{H}^{(l)}
with
l\leq m
. First, the diagonal part

\tilde{H}^{(m+1)}(\xi)_{ii}=-\frac{1}{\xi}\int_{t=0}^{\xi}\sum_{l=1,l\neq i}^{n}\frac{a_{il}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\cdot\tilde{H}^{(m)}(t)_{lj}dt

Applying (
36
) to the case
M=\tilde{H}^{(m+1)}(\xi;u,A)
and using the integral equation, we get the estimate

•

for
\xi\in S_{>\rho^{\prime}}(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon)
,

\displaystyle|\tilde{H}^{(m+1)}(\xi)|

\displaystyle\leq c^{\prime}\cdot|\tilde{H}^{(m+1)}\cdot{\mathrm{i}}u-({\mathrm{i}}u-\xi{\rm Id}_{n})\cdot\tilde{H}^{(m+1)}|

\displaystyle\leq c^{\prime}\cdot\Big|\frac{[A]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{A}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\Big|+c^{\prime}\cdot\Big|\int_{t=0}^{\xi}\left(\tilde{H}^{(m)}(t)\cdot\frac{[A]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{A}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\cdot\tilde{H}^{(m)}(t)\right)dt\Big|.

\displaystyle\leq ac^{\prime}+2ac^{\prime}\int_{t=0}^{\xi}(\sum_{k\geq 1}{t^{(m)}_{k}}|t|^{k-1}/\Gamma(k))dt,

\displaystyle=ac^{\prime}+2ac^{\prime}\sum_{k\geq 1}{t^{(m)}_{k}}|\xi|^{k}/\Gamma(k+1)

\displaystyle\leq{t^{(m+1)}_{1}}+\sum_{k\geq 2}{{t^{(m+1)}_{k}}}|\xi|^{k-1}/\Gamma(k).

which proves the estimate (
41
) for
m+1
.
Here in the last inequlity, we use the identities
t^{(m+1)}_{1}=\alpha={\rm Max}(\frac{a}{\rho^{\prime}},\frac{ac+a^{2}c}{1-2ca\rho^{\prime}(a+1)})
and
t^{(m+1)}_{k}=2ac^{\prime}\cdot t^{(m)}_{k-1}
in the defining relation (
38
).

•

for
\xi\in S_{\leq\rho^{\prime}}(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon):=S(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon)\cap\overline{B_{\rho^{\prime}}(0)}
, we have

|\tilde{H}^{(m+1)}(\xi)|\leq\alpha=t^{(m+1)}_{1}\leq{t^{(m+1)}_{1}}+\sum_{k\geq 2}{{t^{(m+1)}_{k}}}|\xi|^{k-1}/\Gamma(k).

Set
\alpha=a
and
\beta=2ac
, then by (
41
) and (
38
), for
\xi\in S_{>\rho^{\prime}}(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon)
,

|\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi;u,A)|\leq\sum_{k\geq 1}{t^{(m)}_{k}}|\xi|^{k-1}/\Gamma(k)\leq\alpha\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{(\beta|\xi|)^{k-1}}{\Gamma(k)}=\alpha e^{\beta|\xi|}.

Thus, each
H^{(m)}(\xi;u,A)
is estimated by
\alpha e^{\beta|\xi|}
with suitable
\alpha,\beta
independent of
m
and
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R})
. Here we remark that the constant
c
(therefore the other constants) depends on the choice of the small
\varepsilon
.

Therefore, the proof will be completed provided that we show the convergence of
\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi;u,A)
as
m\rightarrow\infty
locally uniform for all
\xi\in S_{>\rho^{\prime}}(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon)
. For that, let us denote by
W^{(m)}(\xi):=\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi;u,A)-\tilde{H}^{(m-1)}(\xi;u,A)
the difference, then by (
35
) we have

W^{(m)}(\xi)\cdot{\mathrm{i}}u-({\mathrm{i}}u-\xi{\rm Id}_{n})\cdot W^{(m)}(\xi)=\int_{t=0}^{\xi}\left(W^{(m-1)}(t)\cdot\frac{[A]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}-\frac{A}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\cdot W^{(m-1)}(t)\right)dt.

(42)

Applying (
36
) to the case
M=W^{(m)}(\xi)
, from (
42
), we deduce that

|W^{(m)}(\xi)|\leq 2ca\cdot\int_{t=0}^{\xi}|W^{(m-1)}(t)|dt.

Similar to the estimate (
41
), by induction one proves that there exists a sequence of constants
b_{m}
such that
b_{m}=2ac\cdot b_{m-1}
and

|W^{(m)}(\xi)|\leq b_{m}\xi^{m-1}/\Gamma(m).

Therefore, there exists a large enough real number
b
such that
b_{m}\leq b^{k}
for all
m,k
. That is
|W^{(m)}(\xi)|\leq b^{m}\xi^{m-1}/\Gamma(m)
and

\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}|W^{(m)}(\xi)|\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}b^{m}\xi^{k-1}/\Gamma(k).

Since
\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}W^{(m)}(\xi)
is the partial sum of the above absolute convergence series, we get that
\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi;u,A)
converge uniformly on every compact subset of the domain
\xi\in S_{>\rho^{\prime}}(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon)
. The
{\rm lim}_{m\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{H}^{(m)}(\xi)
coincides with the Borel series
\tilde{H}(\xi)
in the common domain
B_{\rho}\cap S_{>\rho^{\prime}}(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon)
.

Laplace transform.
The Laplace transform
H_{\theta}=\mathcal{L}_{\theta}(\tilde{H})
of the function
\tilde{H}(\xi)
in the direction
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta}
is a function (in the Laplace plane of the initial
variable
z
) defined by

H_{\theta}(z;u,A)=1+\int_{\xi=0}^{\infty(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta})}e^{-\frac{\xi}{z}}\tilde{H}(\xi;u,A)d\xi.

Lemma
2.5
ensures that for any
\theta
with
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta}\in{\rm Sect}_{\pm}
, the integrand is indeed defined on the integral path, and that for any fixed
l>\beta
the integral exists for all
z\in R(\theta,l)
, where the domain

R(\theta,l):=\{z\in\mathbb{C}~|~{\rm Re}(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta}/z)>l>\beta\}.

The function

H_{\theta}=\mathcal{L}_{\theta}(\tilde{H})=\mathcal{L}_{\theta}(\mathcal{B}(\hat{H}))\hskip 8.53581pt\text{on}\ \ R(\theta,l)

is called the Laplace-Borel transform of
\hat{H}
in the direction
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta}
. The following proposition shows that the original form power series
\hat{H}(z)
is an asymptotics expansion of
H_{\theta}(z)
as
z\rightarrow 0
within
R(\theta,l)
.

Lemma 2.6
.

For any
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R})
and
\theta\in(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2})\cup(-\frac{3\pi}{2},-\frac{\pi}{2})
, there exist constants
l,C,D>0
such that

\displaystyle|H_{\theta}(z;u,A)-1-\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}H_{k}(u)z^{k}|\leq CN^{N}e^{-N}\frac{|z|^{N}}{D^{N}},\ \text{for all}\ z\in R(\theta,l),\ \ N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}.

(43)

Furthermore, the above constants
l,C,D
can be chosen independent of
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R})
.

Remark 2.7
.

For a fixed
u
, the proof of the inequality (
43
) is standard. In the following, we will go through the proof given in
[
56
, Theorem 5.3.9]
, and show that in our case the involved constants can be chosen independent of
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.
Without lose of generality, let us assume that
\theta=0
. By Lemma
2.4
and Lemma
2.5
, there exist constants
K
,
\varepsilon
,
\alpha
and
\beta>0
, such that
\tilde{H}(\xi;u,A)
is holomorphic in the union of the disk
\{\xi:|\xi|\leq 1/K\}
and the sector
S(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
, and satisfies

|\tilde{H}(\xi;u,A)|\leq\alpha e^{\beta|\xi|},\ \text{for all}\ \ \xi\in S(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\ \ \text{and}\ u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R}).

Just as in
[
56
, Theorem 5.3.9]
, let us take a point
b
with argument
\pi/4
and small enough norm
|b|<1/K
such that the path, following a straight line from
0
to
b
and
continues along a horizontal line from
b
to
+\infty
, lies in the domain
\{\xi:|\xi|\leq 1/K\}\cup S(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
of the
\xi
plane. Since the path is homotopy to
[0,+\infty)
,
by the Cauchy’s theorem, the Laplace integral
H_{\theta=0}(z;u,A)
decomposes to

H_{\theta=0}(z;u,A)=1+H^{b}(z;u,A)+Y^{b}(z;u,A)
along the path, where

H^{b}(z;u,A)=\int_{\xi=0}^{b}\tilde{H}(\xi;u,A)e^{-\frac{\xi}{z}}d\xi,\hskip 14.22636ptY^{b}(z;u,A)=\int_{\xi=b}^{+\infty}\tilde{H}(\xi;u,A)e^{-\frac{\xi}{z}}d\xi.

On the one hand, given
0<\delta<\pi/2
, following
[
56
, Lemma 1.3.2]
, we have

\displaystyle|H^{b}(z;u,A)-\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}H_{k}(u)z^{k}|\leq C^{\prime}N^{N}e^{-N}\frac{|z|^{N}}{{D^{\prime}}^{N}},\ \text{for}\ \ z\in S(-\pi/4+\delta,3\pi/4-\delta),\ \ N\in\mathbb{N}_{+},

(44)

where the constants
C^{\prime},D^{\prime}
are

\displaystyle C^{\prime}:=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{|H_{k}(u)|}{\Gamma(k)}b^{k},\hskip 14.22636ptD^{\prime}:=b\cdot{\rm sin}(\delta).

(45)

On the other hand, let us take a constant
l>\beta
, then following the proof of
[
56
, Theorem 5.3.9]
, we have

\displaystyle|Y^{b}(z;u,A)|\leq he^{-\frac{c}{z}},\ \ \text{for}\ z\in S(-\pi/4+\delta,3\pi/4-\delta)\cap R(\theta=0,l),

(46)

with the constants given by

\displaystyle h=\frac{\alpha e^{\beta|b|}}{l-\beta},\hskip 14.22636ptc=|b|{\rm cos}(\pi/2-\delta).

(47)

The estimation (
46
) further implies, see e.g,.
[
56
, Proposition 1.2.17]
, for
z\in S(-\pi/4+\delta,3\pi/4-\delta)\cap R(\theta=0,l)
,
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,

\displaystyle|Y^{b}(z;u,A)-\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}H_{k}(u)z^{k}|\leq C^{\prime\prime}N^{N}e^{-N}\frac{|z|^{N}}{{D^{\prime\prime}}^{N}},

with the constants
C^{\prime\prime}
and
D^{\prime\prime}
determined by
h
and
c
. In conclusion, if we take
C={\rm max}(C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime})
and
D={\rm max}(D^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})
, then
H_{\theta}(z;u,A)
satisfies the inequality (
43
) on the domain

S(-\pi/4+\delta,3\pi/4-\delta)\cap R(\theta=0,l).

According to
[
56
, Theorem 5.3.9]
, an argument using the symmetry with respect to the real axis, i.e., by choosing
\bar{b}
instead of
b
and the corresponding path, shows that
H_{\theta=0}(z)
satisfies the inequality (
43
) on the symmetric domain

S(-3\pi/4+\delta,\pi/4-\delta)\cap R(\theta=0,l)

with respect to the real axis. Since the union of the above two domains cover
R(\theta=0,l)
, we get
H_{\theta=0}(z)
satisfies the inequality (
43
) on
R(\theta=0,l)
.

In the end, let us check the independence of constants
C={\rm max}(C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime})
and
D={\rm max}(D^{\prime},D^{\prime\prime})
on
u_{n}
. First, following Lemma
2.4
, we have
C^{\prime}\leq\frac{Kb}{1-Kb}
. Thus we can set
C^{\prime}=\frac{Kb}{1-Kb}
, and then the constants
C,D
are determined by
\varepsilon,K,\alpha,\beta,b
and
\delta
. By Lemma
2.4
and Lemma
2.5
, as a sufficiently small
\varepsilon
fixed, those constants can be chosen independent of
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R})
. It therefore proves the proposition.

Canonical solutions on Stokes sectors.
The functions
H_{\theta}
and
H_{\theta^{\prime}}
, with
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta},e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta^{\prime}}\in{\rm Sect}_{+}
in the same Stokes sector, coincide in the overlapping of their defining domains. See e.g.,
[
56
, Proposition 5.3.7]
or
[
5
, Section 6.2]
. (While if
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
is not in the same Stokes sector,
H_{\theta}(z;u,A)
is in general not equal to
H_{\theta^{\prime}}(z;u,A)
at the points
z
where both functions are defined.) Thus the functions
H_{\theta}
for all
e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta}\in{\rm Sect}_{+}
glue together into a holomorphic function
H_{+}(z;u,A)
defined on the domain
S(-\pi,\pi)
. Furthermore, since the Laplace-Borel transform is a morphism of differential algebras from the algebra of power series to the algebra of holomorphic functions,
H_{+}(z;u,A)
satisfies the same equation obeyed by
\hat{H}(z;u,A)
. In this way, we see that the function

F_{+}(z;u,A)=({\rm Id}_{n}+H_{+}(z^{-1};u))e^{{\mathrm{i}}uz}z^{-\frac{[A]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

is a solution of (
28
) with the prescribed asymptotics at
z=\infty
within
S(-\pi,\pi)
. It follows from the Waston’s Lemma (see
[
5
, Section 4.7]
) that once an actual solution of (
28
) is obtained which has the formal fundamental solution matrix (
29
) as its asymptotic expansion as
z\rightarrow\infty
in a sector whose opening is larger than
\pi
, then such an actual solution is unique. Similarly, we can construct the unique (therefore canonical) solution
F_{-}(z;u,A)
in
S(-2\pi,0)
.

As for the uniform property with respect to
u
, we just observe that by the proof of Lemma
2.6
, the constants in the inequality (
43
) can be chosen independent of
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R})
, as long as
\theta
keeps a fixed positive distance
2\varepsilon>0
with
\pi
and
-\pi
. Therefore, in each proper closed subsector
\bar{S}(-\pi+\varepsilon_{0},\pi-\varepsilon_{0})
of
S(-\pi,\pi)
for a small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, by (
43
) the limit

\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}|H_{+}(z^{-1};u)|=\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}|H_{+}(z;u,A)|=0

is uniform for all
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R})
. The same statement is true for
H_{-}(z;u,A)
. It finishes the proof of Theorem
2.3
.

Remark 2.8
.

The above argument implies stronger results (but the present form is enough for the use in the proof of Proposition
3.12
): the limit

\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}z^{-m}(|H_{\theta}(z;u,A)-\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}H_{k}(u,A)z^{k}|)=0,\ \ \ z\in R(\theta,l)

for all
m>0
, is uniform for
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}^{d}(\mathbb{R}).

Similar argument shows that Theorem
2.3
generalizes to the case
u\in\mathfrak{h}(\mathbb{R})
, the space of
n\times n
real diagonal matrices
u={\rm diag}(u_{1},...,u_{n})
. To be more precise, let us take any partition of the set

\{1,...,n\}=I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup\cdots\cup I_{k}.

Then for any
u={\rm diag}(u_{1},...,u_{n})
, satisfying
u_{i}=u_{j}
if
i,j\in I_{s}
for some
s
;
u_{i}\neq u_{j}
otherwise, there exist canonical solutions
F_{\pm}(z;u,A)
in
{\rm Sect}_{\pm}
such that
F_{+}\cdot e^{-{\mathrm{i}}uz}\cdot z^{\frac{\delta_{u}(A)]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}
and
F_{-}\cdot e^{-{\mathrm{i}}uz}\cdot z^{\frac{\delta_{u}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}
can be analytically continued to
S(-\pi,\pi)
and
S(0,2\pi)
respectively, and for every small
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}F_{+}(z;u,A)\cdot e^{-{\mathrm{i}}uz}\cdot z^{\frac{\delta_{u}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Id}_{n},\ \ \ as\ \ \ z\in\bar{S}(-\pi+\varepsilon,\pi-\varepsilon),

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}F_{-}(z;u,A)\cdot e^{-{\mathrm{i}}uz}\cdot z^{\frac{\delta_{u}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Id}_{n},\ \ \ as\ \ \ z\in\bar{S}(-2\pi+\varepsilon,-\varepsilon),

Here
\delta_{u}(A)
is the projection of
A
on the centralizer of
u
in
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
, i.e.,

\delta_{u}(A)_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}A_{ij},&\text{if}\ \ i,\ j\in\ I_{s}\ \text{for some}\ s\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{array}\right.

Furthermore, the above limits are uniform for all
u
such that if
u_{i}
is not identically equal to
u_{j}
, then
|u_{i}-u_{j}|
is bigger than a fixed positive number
d
.

2.2
Stokes matrices and connection matrices

For any
\sigma\in S_{n}
, let us denote by
U_{\sigma}
the component
\{u_{\sigma(1)}<\cdot\cdot\cdot<u_{\sigma(n)}\}
of
\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, and denote by
P_{\sigma}\in{\rm GL}_{n}
the corresponding permutation matrix.

Definition 2.9
.

For any
u\in U_{\sigma}
, the
Stokes matrices
of the system (
28
) (with respect
to
{\rm Sect}_{+}
and the chosen branch of
{\rm log}(z)
) are the elements
S_{\pm}(u,A)\in{\rm GL}(n)
determined by

\displaystyle F_{+}(z;u,A)

\displaystyle=F_{-}(z;u,A)\cdot e^{-\frac{[A]}{2}}P_{\sigma}S_{+}(u,A)P_{\sigma}^{-1},

\displaystyle F_{-}(ze^{-2\pi{\mathrm{i}}};u,A)

\displaystyle=F_{+}(z;u,A)\cdot P_{\sigma}S_{-}(u,A)P_{\sigma}^{-1}e^{\frac{[A]}{2}},

where the first (resp. second) identity is understood to hold in
{\rm Sect}_{-}

(resp.
{\rm Sect}_{+}
) after
F_{+}
(resp.
F_{-}
)
has been analytically continued anticlockwise around
z=\infty
.

The prescribed asymptotics of
F_{\pm}(z;u,A)
at
z=\infty
, as well as the identities in Definition
2.9
, ensures that the Stokes matrices
S_{+}(u,A)
and
S_{-}(u,A)
are upper and lower triangular matrices respectively. see e.g.,
[
5
, Chapter 9.1]
. Furthermore, the following lemma follows from the fact that if
F(z;u,A)
is a solution, so is
F(\bar{z};u,A)^{\dagger}
, see
[
11
]
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
S_{+}(u,A)^{\dagger}
denote the conjugation transpose of
S_{+}(u,A)
, then
S_{-}(u,A)=S_{+}(u,A)^{\dagger}
.

Since the system (
28
) is non-resonant, i.e., no two eigenvalues of
\frac{A}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}
for
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
are differed by a positive integer, we have (see e.g
[
5
, Chapter 2]
).

Lemma 2.11
.

There is a unique holomorphic fundamental solution

F_{0}(z;u,A)\in{\rm GL}(n)
of the system (
28
) on a neighbourhood of
\infty
slit along
{\mathrm{i}}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, such that
F_{0}\cdot z^{\frac{A}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\rightarrow{\rm Id}_{n}
as
z\rightarrow 0
.

Definition 2.12
.

The
connection
matrix

C(u,A)\in{\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
of the system (
28
) (with respect to
{\rm Sect}_{+}
) is determined by

F_{0}(z;u,A)=F_{+}(z;u,A)\cdot C(u,A),

as
F_{0}(z;u,A)
is
extended to the domain of definition of
F_{+}(z;u,A)
.

The connection matrix
C(u,A)
is valued in
{\rm U}(n)
(see e.g.,
[
11
, Lemma 29]
).
Thus for any fixed
u
, by varying
A\in{{\rm Herm}}(n)
we obtain the connection map

\displaystyle C(\cdot,u)\colon{\rm Herm}(n)\rightarrow{\rm U}(n).

(48)

In a global picture, the connection matrix is related to the Stokes matrices
by the following monodromy relation, which follows from the fact that a simple negative loop (i.e., in clockwise direction) around
0
is a simple positive loop (i.e., in anticlockwise direction) around
\infty
: for any
u\in U_{\sigma}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
,

\displaystyle C(u,A)e^{A}C(u,A)^{-1}=P_{\sigma}S_{-}(u,A)S_{+}(u,A)P_{\sigma}^{-1}.

(49)

3
Expression of Stokes matrices via the boundary values of solutions of isomonodromy equations

In Section
3.1
, we recall the isomonodromy equations of the meromorphic linear systems of differential equation (
28
). In Section
3.2
and
3.3
, we study the asymptotics of the solutions of the isomonodromy equation at a critial point, and then give a proof of Theorem
1.1
. In Sections
3.4
and
3.5
, we prove an analytic branching rule of Stokes matrices with respect to the collapse of the components of the irregular parameter
u
. The branching rule enables us to express the Stokes matrices via the asymptotics of solutions of isomonodromy equation at an "infinite" point. Then in Section
3.6
, we prove our first main result, i.e, Theorem
1.2
, using the known global behavior of the solutions of confluent hypergeometric systems. In Sections
3.7
-
3.9
, we give a proof of Theorem
1.5
. In Section
3.8
, we get the explicit leading order terms of
S_{\pm}(u,A)
as
u
approaches to the "infinite" point. In the end, Section
3.11
studies the analog of the results in Section
3.2
-
3.8
for a degenerate irregular parameter
u
.

3.1
Isomonodromy deformation

In this subsection, we recall some facts about the theory of isomonodromy deformation. In general, the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,A)
of the system (
28
) will depend on the irregular term
u
.
The isomonodromy deformation (also known as monodromy preserving) problem is to find the matrix valued function
\Phi(u)
such that the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,\Phi(u))
are (locally) constant. The following definition and proposition are well known. See more detailed discussions in e.g.,
[
14
,
47
,
48
]
,
[
23
, Chapter 3]
. In particular, the equation (
50
) is the Jimbo-Miwa-Môri-Sato equation
[
47
]
with one irregular singularity and one regular singularity.

Definition 3.1
.

The isomonodromy equation is the differential equation for a matrix valued function
\Phi(u):\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow{\rm Herm}(n)

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial u_{k}}=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}[\Phi,{\rm ad}^{-1}_{u}{\rm ad}_{E_{k}}\Phi],\ \text{for all}\ k=1,...,n.

(50)

Here
E_{k}
is the
n\times n
diagonal matrix whose
(k,k)
-entry is
1
and other entries are
0
.
Note that
{\rm ad}_{E_{k}}\Phi
takes values in the space
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}^{od}
of off diagonal matrices and that
{\rm ad}_{u}

is invertible when restricted to
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}^{od}
.

Remark 3.2
.

The isomonodromy equation with respect to the derivation of
u_{j}
is generated by the time dependent quadratic Hamiltonian
H_{j}:=(-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})\sum_{k\neq j}\frac{\phi_{kj}\phi_{jk}}{u_{k}-u_{j}}
, where
\phi_{ij}
’s are the entry functions on
{\rm Herm}(n)
, see e.g.,
[
23
,
14
]
.

Set
\Phi(u)=(\phi_{ij}(u))
, then in terms of the components, the equation (
50
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{k}}\phi_{ij}(u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\left(\frac{1}{u_{k}-u_{j}}-\frac{1}{u_{k}-u_{i}}\right)\phi_{ik}(u)\phi_{kj}(u),\quad i,j\neq k,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{k}}\phi_{ik}(u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\sum_{j\neq k}\frac{\phi_{ij}(u)\phi_{jk}(u)-\delta_{ij}\phi_{kk}(u)\phi_{jk}(u)}{u_{k}-u_{j}},\quad i\neq k,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{k}}\phi_{kj}(u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\sum_{i\neq k}\frac{\delta_{ij}\phi_{kk}(u)\phi_{ki}(u)-\phi_{ki}(u)\phi_{ij}(u)}{u_{k}-u_{i}},\quad j\neq k,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial u_{k}}\phi_{kk}(u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

Proposition 3.3
.

For any solution
\Phi(u)
of the isomonodromy equation, the canonical solutions
F_{\pm}
of the system

\displaystyle\frac{dF}{dz}=\left({\mathrm{i}}u-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\Phi(u)}{z}\right)\cdot F,

(51)

satisfy (as a function of
u={\rm diag}(u_{1},...,u_{n})
)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial u_{k}}=\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{k}z-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}{\rm ad}^{-1}_{u}{\rm ad}_{E_{k}}\Phi(u)\right)\cdot F.

(52)

In particular, the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,\Phi(u))
of (
51
)
are locally constants (independent of
u
). Furthermore the isomonodromy equation (
50
) is the compatibility condition of the systems (
51
) and (
52
).

3.2
Boundary values of the solutions of the isomonodromy equation

In this subsection, we will prove Theorem
1.1
. Recall that we denote by
U_{\rm id}
the connected component
\{u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})~|~u_{1}<\cdots<u_{n}\}
of
\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, and by
\delta_{k}(A)
the matrix

\displaystyle\delta_{k}(A)_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}A_{ij},&\text{if}\ \ 1\leq i,j\leq k,\ \text{or}\ i=j\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

We first prove a stronger statement, from which Theorem
1.1
follows. We introduce the new coordinates

\displaystyle z_{0}=u_{1}+u_{2}+\cdots+u_{n},\ z_{1}=u_{2}-u_{1},\ z_{2}=\frac{u_{3}-u_{2}}{u_{2}-u_{1}},\ ......,\ z_{n-1}=\frac{u_{n}-u_{n-1}}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}}.

(56)

Then
\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}=z_{0}
, and

\displaystyle u_{2}=u_{1}+z_{1},\ u_{3}=u_{1}+z_{1}+z_{1}z_{2},\ ......,\ u_{n}=u_{1}+z_{1}+z_{1}z_{2}+z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}+...+z_{1}\cdots z_{n-1}.

(57)

In terms of the new coordinates, equation (
50
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\Big[\Phi,{\rm ad}_{u}^{-1}{\rm ad}_{\frac{\partial u}{\partial z_{j}}}\Phi\Big],\ \text{for}\ j=1,...,n-1,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z_{0}}

\displaystyle=0,

where the diagonal matrix

\frac{\partial u}{\partial z_{j}}=z_{j}^{-1}\left((u_{j+1}-u_{j})E_{j+1}+(u_{j+2}-u_{j})E_{j+2}+\cdots+(u_{n}-u_{j})E_{n}\right).

Therefore, any solution
\Phi
is independent of
z_{0}
, and is thus a function of
z_{1},...,z_{n-1}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

For any Hermitian matrix valued solution
\Phi(z_{1},...,z_{n-1})
of (
50
) on
U_{\rm id}
, there exists a chain of functions
\Phi_{k}(z_{1},...,z_{k})\in{\rm Herm}(n)
for all
k=0,...,n-1
such that
\Phi_{n-1}=\Phi
and
\Phi_{0}
is a constant, and for any
k=2,...,n-1
,

\displaystyle\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k}(z_{1},...,z_{k}))

\displaystyle=\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1}(z_{1},...,z_{k-1}))+\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}),

(58)

\displaystyle z_{k}^{\frac{-{\rm ad}\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}(\Phi_{k})

\displaystyle=\Phi_{k-1}+\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}).

(59)

as
z_{k}\rightarrow+\infty
, uniformly with respect to
z_{1},...,z_{k-1}
.
Here
z_{k}^{\frac{{\rm ad}X}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}Y:=z_{k}^{\frac{X}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}Yz_{k}^{-\frac{X}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}
for any
X,Y\in{\rm Herm}(n)
.
Furthermore, the function
\Phi_{k}
satisfy the differential equations

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi_{k}}{\partial z_{j}}=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\Big[\Phi_{k},({\rm ad}_{u}^{-1}{\rm ad}_{\frac{\partial u}{\partial z_{j}}}-z_{j}^{-1})\delta_{k+1}(\Phi_{k})\Big],\ \text{ for }\ j=1,...,k.

(60)

In the end, for
k=1
,

z_{1}^{\frac{-{\rm ad}\delta_{1}(\Phi_{0})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}(\Phi_{1})=\Phi_{0}.

(61)

We call
\Phi_{k-1}
the boundary value of
\Phi_{k}
at
z_{k}=+\infty
.

Proof.

Case I: assume that an integer
k\in\{2,...,n-1\}
, and a solution
\Phi_{k}(z_{1},...,z_{k})
of the equation (
60
), are given, let us construct a function
\Phi_{k-1}(z_{1},...,z_{k-1})
such that (
58
) and (
59
) hold. For this purpose, let us consider the differential equation of
\Phi_{k}
with respect to the parameter
z_{k}
.

Firstly, we have

Lemma 3.5
.

The solution
\Phi_{k}
satisfies

\frac{d}{dz_{j}}{\rm Tr}(\Phi_{k}^{\dagger}\Phi_{k})=0,\text{ for }j=1,...,k.

Therefore, the norm of
\Phi_{k}
is uniformly bounded for all
z_{j}
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}({\rm Tr}(\Phi_{k}^{\dagger}\Phi_{k}))={\rm Tr}(\frac{\partial\Phi_{k}^{\dagger}}{\partial z_{j}}\Phi_{k}+\Phi_{k}^{\dagger}\frac{\partial\Phi_{k}}{\partial z_{j}})=\frac{1}{\pi{\mathrm{i}}}{\rm Im}\Big[\Phi_{k},({\rm ad}_{u}^{-1}{\rm ad}_{\frac{\partial u}{\partial z_{j}}}-z_{j}^{-1})\delta_{k+1}(\Phi_{k})\Big]=0.

Here the second identity follows from
\Phi_{k}=\Phi_{k}^{\dagger}\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, and the last identity follows from the fact that
{\rm Tr}(X\cdot[X,Y]])=0
for any matrices
X,Y
.

Secondly, we have

Lemma 3.6
.

There exist functions
J_{k-1}(z_{1},...,z_{k-1})
and
\Phi_{k-1}(z_{1},...,z_{n-1})\in{\rm Herm}(n)
such that

\displaystyle\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k}(z_{1},...,z_{k}))

\displaystyle=J_{k-1}(z_{1},...,z_{k-1})+\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}),

(62)

\displaystyle z_{k}^{-\frac{{\rm ad}J_{k-1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\Phi_{k}(z_{1},...,z_{k})

\displaystyle=\Phi_{k-1}(z_{1},...,z_{k-1})+\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}),

(63)

as
z_{k}\rightarrow\infty
, uniformly with respect to
z_{1},...,z_{n-1}
.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle{\rm ad}_{u}^{-1}{\rm ad}_{\partial u/\partial z_{k}}\delta_{k+1}(\Phi_{k})=-{\rm ad}_{D^{(k)}_{k}}{\rm ad}_{E_{k+1}}\delta_{k+1}(\Phi_{k}),

(64)

where the
n\times n
diagonal matrix with
k+1
none zero elements

\displaystyle D^{(k)}_{k}={\rm diag}\left(\frac{1}{z_{k}+\frac{u_{k}-u_{1}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}},\ldots,\frac{1}{z_{k}+\frac{u_{k}-u_{k-2}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}},\frac{1}{z_{k}+1},\frac{1}{z_{k}},0,\ldots,0\right).\qquad\quad

(65)

Then we have (in terms of the block matrix form)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k})}{\partial z_{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\delta_{k}\left(\Big[\Phi_{k},(-{\rm ad}_{D^{(k)}_{k}}{\rm ad}_{E_{k+1}}-z_{k}^{-1})\delta_{k+1}(\Phi_{k})\Big]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\left(((D^{(k)}_{k})_{jj}-(D^{(k)}_{k})_{ii})(\Phi_{k})_{i,k+1}(\Phi_{k})_{k+1,j}\right)_{i,j=1}^{k}&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right).

Since
0\leq\frac{u_{k}-u_{i}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\leq 1
for all
i=1,...,k
, we have

(D^{(k)}_{k})_{jj}-(D^{(k)}_{k})_{ii}=\frac{1}{z_{k}+\frac{u_{k}-u_{j}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}}-\frac{1}{z_{k}+\frac{u_{k}-u_{i}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}}=\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-2})

as
z_{k}\rightarrow+\infty
uniformly with respect to
z_{1},...,z_{k-1}
. Furthermore, by Lemma
3.5
,

\Phi_{k}=\mathcal{O}(1)
as
z_{k}\rightarrow\infty
. We thus get

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k})}{\partial z_{k}}=\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-2}),

(67)

uniformly with respect to
z_{1},...,z_{k-1}
. Therefore, by integrating (
67
), we see that for any
z_{1},...,z_{n-1}
there exists
J_{k-1}(z_{1},...,z_{n-1})
such that (
62
) holds.

Now let us consider the function
z_{k}^{-\frac{{\rm ad}J_{k-1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\Phi_{k}:=z_{k}^{-\frac{J_{k-1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot\Phi_{k}\cdot z_{k}^{\frac{J_{k-1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}
. It satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{d(z_{k}^{-\frac{{\rm ad}J_{k-1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\Phi_{k})}{dz_{k}}=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}[z_{k}^{-\frac{{\rm ad}J_{k-1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\Phi_{k},B(z_{k})],

(68)

with

\displaystyle B(z_{k})=z_{k}^{-\frac{J_{k-1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\left((-{\rm ad}_{D^{(k)}_{k}}{\rm ad}_{E_{k+1}}-z_{k}^{-1})\delta_{k+1}(\Phi_{k})-z_{k}^{-1}(\Phi_{k}-J_{k-1})\right)z_{k}^{\frac{J_{k-1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}.

By
\Phi_{k}=\mathcal{O}(1)
and the expression (
64
) of
D^{(k)}_{k}
, we get

\displaystyle(-{\rm ad}_{D^{(k)}_{k}}{\rm ad}_{E_{k+1}}-z_{k}^{-1})\delta_{k+1}(\Phi_{k})=z_{k}^{-1}(\delta_{k+1}(\Phi_{k})-\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k}))+\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-2}).

(69)

Therefore, by Lemma
62
,

(-{\rm ad}_{D^{(k)}_{k}}{\rm ad}_{E_{k+1}}-z_{k}^{-1})\delta_{k+1}(\Phi_{k})-z_{k}^{-1}(\Phi_{k}-J_{k-1})=\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-2}).

Since
J_{k-1}/{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}
is skew-Hermitian, i.e.,
|(z_{k})^{\frac{J_{k-1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}|=1
, we get
B(z_{k})=\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-2})
and

\frac{d(z_{k}^{-\frac{{\rm ad}J_{k-1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\Phi_{k})}{dz_{k}}=\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-2}).

Integrating the above identity, we find a function
\Phi_{k-1}(z_{1},...,z_{n-1})\in{\rm Herm}(n)
such that (
63
) holds.

Thirdly, we show that
J_{k-1}
is actually equal to
\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k})
. The identity
62
implies that

\displaystyle z_{k}^{-\frac{\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}=z_{k}^{-\frac{J_{k-1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}+\mathcal{O}({\rm In}(z_{k})/z_{k}).

(70)

Here we use the fact that for any two skew-Hermitian matrices
X
and
Y
,
the inequality
|e^{X}-e^{Y}|\leq|X-Y|
holds (simply note that
e^{X}-e^{Y}=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{dt}(e^{(1-t)Y}e^{tX})dt=\int_{0}^{1}(e^{(1-t)Y}(X-Y)e^{tX})dt,

and
|e^{(1-t)Y}|=|e^{tX}|=1
).

Thus, identities (
63
) and (
70
) lead to

z_{k}^{-\frac{{\rm ad}\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\Phi_{k}(z_{1},...,z_{k})=\Phi_{k-1}(z_{1},...,z_{k-1})+\mathcal{O}({\rm In}(z_{k})/z_{k}).

(71)

Taking the operator
\delta_{k}
on both sides of (
71
) leads to

\lim_{z_{k}\rightarrow+\infty}\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k})=\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1}),

which (together with (
62
)) implies

J_{k-1}=\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1}).

Then (
62
) and (
63
) becomes (
58
) and (
59
) respectively provided replacing
J_{k-1}
by
\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1})
.

In the end, we have to prove that the new function
\Phi_{k-1}(z_{1},...,z_{k-1})
satisfies the equation (
60
) provided replacing
k
by
k-1
in (
60
). Similar to the above discussion, we verify that as
z_{k}\rightarrow+\infty
for all
j<k
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k})}{\partial z_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\Big[\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k}),({\rm ad}_{u}^{-1}{\rm ad}_{\frac{\partial u}{\partial z_{j}}}-z_{j}^{-1})\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k})\Big]+\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi_{k}}{\partial z_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\Big[\Phi_{k},({\rm ad}_{u}^{-1}{\rm ad}_{\frac{\partial u}{\partial z_{j}}}-z_{j}^{-1})\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k})\Big]+\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}),

uniformly for all
z_{j}
with
j<k
. The above two identities give rise to

\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}\left(z_{k}^{-\frac{{\rm ad}\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}(\Phi_{k})\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\Big[z_{k}^{-\frac{{\rm ad}\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}(\Phi_{k}),\ ({\rm ad}_{u}^{-1}{\rm ad}_{\frac{\partial u}{\partial z_{j}}}-z_{j}^{-1})\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k})\Big]+\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}),

(72)

as
z_{k}\rightarrow+\infty
. Based on (
71
), letting
z_{k}\rightarrow+\infty
in (
72
) leads to

\frac{\partial\Phi_{k-1}}{\partial z_{j}}=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\Big[\Phi_{k-1},\ ({\rm ad}_{u}^{-1}{\rm ad}_{\frac{\partial u}{\partial z_{j}}}-z_{j}^{-1})\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1})\Big],

which is just the equation (
60
) for
\Phi_{k-1}
(replacing
k
by
k-1
in (
60
)). By induction, it finishes the proof of the proposition for any
k=2,...,n
.

Case II:
k=1
. Then
\Phi_{1}(z_{1})
is a solution of

\frac{d\Phi_{1}}{dz_{1}}=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\Big[\Phi_{1},\ ({\rm ad}_{u}^{-1}{\rm ad}_{\frac{\partial u}{\partial z_{1}}}-z_{j}^{-1})\delta_{2}(\Phi_{1})\Big].

This equation can be simply solved explicitly, and any solution takes the form of (
61
), i.e.,

z_{1}^{\frac{-{\rm ad}\delta_{1}(\Phi_{0})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}(\Phi_{1})=\Phi_{0}.

for a unique constant
\Phi_{0}
.

Therefore, given any solution
\Phi
of (
50
), by induction it finishes the proof of existence of the chain of functions
\Phi_{n-1}=\Phi,\Phi_{n-2},...,\Phi_{1},
and the constant
\Phi_{0}
.

3.3
The proof of Theorem
1.1

The proof of Theorem
1.1
.

On the one hand, by Proposition
3.4
, we have

\displaystyle z_{k-1}^{\frac{-\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}z_{k}^{\frac{-\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}(\Phi_{k})z_{k}^{\frac{\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}z_{k-1}^{\frac{\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle z_{k-1}^{\frac{-\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\Phi_{k-1}z_{k-1}^{\frac{\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}+\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi_{k-2}+\mathcal{O}(z_{k-1}^{-1})+\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}).

(73)

On the other hand, we have

z_{k-1}^{\frac{-\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}z_{k}^{\frac{-\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}=z_{k}^{\frac{-W}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}z_{k-1}^{\frac{-\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

(74)

where

W=z_{k-1}^{\frac{-\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}(\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1}))z_{k-1}^{\frac{\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

By Proposition
3.4
, we have

W=\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-2})+\mathcal{O}(z_{k-1}^{-1})

(75)

as
z_{k-1}\rightarrow+\infty
. Furthermore, it follows from the equation of
\Phi_{k-1}
that the eigenvalues of
\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1})
are constant (independent of
z_{1},...,z_{k-1}
). In particular, the
n\times n
Hermitian matrices
W
and
\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-2})
have same eigenvalues. Therefore, for any fixed
z_{1},...,z_{k-2}
we can find a family of
n\times n
unitary matrices
P(z_{k-1})
such that

W=P\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-2})P^{-1},\ \text{ and }\ \lVert P-{\rm Id}_{n}\rVert=\mathcal{O}(z_{k-1}^{-1}).

For example, just like Remark
3.8
, the equation of
W
respect to the variable
z_{k}
is equivalent to a differential equation of
P(z_{k})
. Then the required
P(z_{k})
can be determined as a solution of the corresponding equation with the prescribed asymptotics.

Thus,

z_{k}^{\frac{-W}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}=Pz_{k}^{\frac{-\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}P^{-1}=z_{k}^{\frac{-\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}+\mathcal{O}(z_{k-1}^{-1}),

which gives rise to

z_{k}^{\frac{-W}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}z_{k-1}^{\frac{-\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}=z_{k}^{\frac{-\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}z_{k-1}^{\frac{-\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}+\mathcal{O}(z_{k-1}^{-1}).

(76)

The identities (
74
) and (
76
) give

\displaystyle z_{k-1}^{\frac{-\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}z_{k}^{\frac{-\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}(\Phi_{k})z_{k}^{\frac{\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}z_{k-1}^{\frac{\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle z_{k}^{\frac{-\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}z_{k-1}^{\frac{-\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}(\Phi_{k})z_{k-1}^{\frac{\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}z_{k}^{\frac{\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}+\mathcal{O}(z_{k-1}^{-1})+\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}).

(77)

Combining (
3.3
) and (
77
) gives rise to

z_{k}^{\frac{-\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}z_{k-1}^{\frac{-\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}(\Phi_{k})z_{k-1}^{\frac{\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}z_{k}^{\frac{\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}=\Phi_{k-2}+\mathcal{O}(z_{k-1}^{-1})+\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}).

Continuing the process step by step, we get (recall that
\Phi=\Phi_{n-1}(z_{1},...,z_{n-1})
)

\displaystyle\left(\overleftarrow{\underset{k=1,...,n-1}{\prod}}z_{k}^{\frac{-\delta_{k}(\Phi_{0})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)\cdot\Phi\cdot\left(\overleftarrow{\underset{k=1,...,n-1}{\prod}}z_{k}^{\frac{-\delta_{k}(\Phi_{0})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle z_{1}^{\frac{-\delta_{1}(\Phi_{0})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot\Phi_{1}(z_{1})\cdot z_{1}^{\frac{\delta_{1}(\Phi_{0})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}).

(78)

Here the last identity uses (
61
), and the product
\overleftarrow{\prod}
is taken with the index
i
to the right of
j
if
i>j
. Note that the identity (
78
) is stronger version of (
4
), i.e., it implies that (
4
) holds for all
z_{1}>0
, not just in the limit
z_{1}\rightarrow 0
. (We add the redundant condition
z_{1}\rightarrow 0
in Theorem
1.1
, simply because the infinite point
u=(u_{1},...,u_{n})
with
z_{1}\rightarrow 0
and
z_{k}\rightarrow+\infty
for
k=2,...,n-1
is the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
.) It proves the first part of Theorem
1.1
.

The existence of a real analytic solution
\Phi(u)
of (
50
) with the boundary value
\Phi_{0}
follows from Proposition
3.33
.

Remark 3.7
.

In a follow-up work
[
71
]
, we find the convergent series expansion of
\Phi(u)
, not just the leading expansion, in terms of
\Phi_{0}
, as
z_{k}\rightarrow\infty
for
k=2,...,n-1
.

Remark 3.8
.

There exists a
U(n)
valued function
\rho(z_{1},...,z_{n-1})
such that
\Phi(u)
is related to its boundary value
\Phi_{0}
by the conjuation of
\rho
, that is
\Phi(z_{1},...,z_{n-1})=\rho\Phi_{0}\rho^{-1}
. Actually, following from the equation and the asymptotics of
\Phi(u)
, we see that
\rho
is the solution of the equation

\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial z_{j}}=-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}{\rm ad}_{u}^{-1}{\rm ad}_{\frac{\partial u}{\partial z_{j}}}\left(\rho\Phi_{0}\rho^{-1}\right),\ \text{for}\ j=1,...,n-1,

with the prescribed asymptotics

\left(\overleftarrow{\underset{j=1,...,n-1}{\prod}}z_{j}^{\frac{-\delta_{j}(\Phi^{(11)}_{0})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)\cdot\rho(z_{1},...,z_{n-1})={\rm Id}_{n}+\sum_{j=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{j}^{-1}).

Remark 3.9
.

It follows from the equation (
60
) that if we write the
n\times n
matrix
\Phi_{k}
as a block form

\Phi_{k}(z_{1},...,z_{k})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Phi_{k}^{(11)}&\Phi_{k}^{(12)}\\
\Phi_{k}^{(21)}&\Phi_{k}^{(22)}\end{array}\right),

then the
(k+1)\times(k+1)
block
\Phi_{k}^{(11)}(z_{1},...,z_{k})
is a solution of the isomonodromy equation (
50
) of rank
k+1
(i.e., replacing
n
by
k+1
in (
50
)). Let us explain how the whole
\Phi_{k}
can be determined from the upper left submatrix
\Phi_{k}^{(11)}
. By the equation (
60
), the
(k+1)\times(n-k+1)
block
\Phi_{k}^{(12)}
and the
(n-k+1)\times(n-k+1)
block
\Phi_{k}^{(22)}
satisfy for
j=1,...,k
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi_{k}^{(12)}}{\partial z_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\Phi_{k}^{(12)}\cdot\delta_{0}(\Phi_{k}^{(22)})-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\left(({\rm ad}_{u}^{-1}{\rm ad}_{\frac{\partial u}{\partial z_{j}}}-z_{j}^{-1})\Phi_{k}^{(11)}\right)\cdot\Phi_{k}^{(12)},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi_{k}^{(21)}}{\partial z_{j}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\delta_{0}(\Phi_{k}^{(22)})\cdot\Phi_{k}^{(21)}+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\Phi_{k}^{(21)}\cdot({\rm ad}_{u}^{-1}{\rm ad}_{\frac{\partial u}{\partial z_{j}}}-z_{j}^{-1})\Phi_{k}^{(11)},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi_{k}^{(22)}}{\partial z_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\delta_{0}(\Phi_{k}^{(22)})\cdot\Phi_{k}^{(22)}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\Phi_{k}^{(22)}\cdot\delta_{0}(\Phi_{k}^{(22)}).

Here recall
\delta_{0}(\Phi_{k}^{(22)})
is the diagonal part of the matrix
\Phi_{k}^{(22)}
. Therefore, knowing the block
\Phi_{k}^{(11)}
and the boundary value
\Phi_{0}
, the other blocks can be expressed in a simple form. In particular, there exists a
U(k+1)
valued function
\rho_{k}(z_{1},...,z_{k})
such that the upper left block

\Phi^{(11)}_{k}=\rho_{k}\cdot\Phi^{(11)}_{0}\cdot\rho_{k}^{-1},

and the other blocks are then determined simply by

\Phi_{k}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\rho_{k}&0\\
0&(z_{1}\cdots z_{k})^{\frac{\delta_{0}(\Phi_{0}^{(22)})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\end{array}\right)\cdot\Phi_{0}\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\rho_{k}&0\\
0&(z_{1}\cdots z_{k})^{\frac{\delta_{0}(\Phi_{0}^{(22)})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\end{array}\right)^{-1},

where
\delta_{0}(\Phi_{0}^{(22)})
is the
(n-k+1)\times(n-k+1)
diagonal matrix with diagonal entries
(\Phi_{0})_{k+2,k+2},...,(\Phi_{0})_{n,n}
. It follows from the asymptotics of
\Phi_{k}
that the unitary matrix
\rho_{k}
satisfies

\left(\overleftarrow{\underset{j=1,...,k}{\prod}}z_{j}^{\frac{-\delta_{j}(\Phi^{(11)}_{0})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)\cdot\rho_{k}(z_{1},...,z_{k})={\rm Id}_{k+1,k+1}+\mathcal{O}(z_{j}^{-1};j=2,...,k).

A similar proof as above shows that

Proposition 3.10
.

For any solution
\Phi(u)
of the isomonodromy equation (
3
) on the connected component
U_{\rm id}
, there exists a unique constant
\Phi_{0}\in{\rm Herm}(n)
such that as
z_{k}\rightarrow+\infty
for all
k=2,...,n-1
,

\displaystyle\left(\overrightarrow{\underset{k=1,...,n-1}{\prod}}z_{k}^{\frac{-\delta_{k}(\Phi)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)\cdot\Phi\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{\underset{k=1,...,n-1}{\prod}}z_{k}^{\frac{-\delta_{k}(\Phi)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)^{-1}=\Phi_{0}+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}).

(79)

We remark that the ordered product in (
79
) is reverse to the one in (
78
), and Proposition
3.10
will be used in the proof of Theorem
1.5
in Section
3.9
.

Definition 3.11
.

We call
\Phi_{0}
the boundary value of the solution
\Phi(u)
of isomonodromy equation (
50
) at
u_{\rm cat}
. And for any
\Phi_{0}\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, we denote by
\Phi(u;\Phi_{0})
the solution of (
50
) with the boundary value
\Phi_{0}
.

The meaning of the infinite point will become clear in Section
5
. In the rest of this section, we will derive an explicit formula of the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,\Phi(u;\Phi_{0}))
via the boundary value
\Phi_{0}
, i.e., Theorem
1.2
.

3.4
A branching rule of the system (
28
) via isomonodromy deformation: one recursive step

It is more convenient to work with the following coordinates

\displaystyle\widetilde{z_{0}}=u_{1},\ z_{1}=u_{2}-u_{1},\ z_{2}=\frac{u_{3}-u_{2}}{u_{2}-u_{1}},\ ......,\ z_{n-1}=\frac{u_{n}-u_{n-1}}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}}.

In this section, we will show that as
z_{n-1}\rightarrow+\infty
, the boundary value
\Phi_{n-2}(z_{1},...,z_{n-1})
, in the sense of Proposition
3.4
, of a given solution
\Phi_{n-1}=\Phi(z_{1},...,z_{n-1})
of the isomonodromy equation can be used to "decouple" the system (
28
) of rank
n
into two lower rank systems, with rank
n-1
and rank
1
(therefore trivial) respectively. To fix some notations, for any
u\in U_{\rm id}
and
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, we denote by

•

S_{\pm}(u,A)
the Stokes matrices, and
C(u,A)
the connection matrix of
\frac{dF}{dz}=({\mathrm{i}}u-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{A}{z})F
;

•

C(E_{n},A)\subset{\rm U}(n)
, the connection matrix of
\frac{dF}{dz}=({\mathrm{i}}E_{n}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{A}{z})F
, with
E_{n}={\rm diag}(0,...,0,1)
;

•

C(u^{(n-1)},\delta_{n-1}(A))\in{\rm U}(n)
, the connection matrix of the
n\times n
system
\frac{dF}{dz}=(u^{(n-1)}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\delta_{n-1}(A)}{z})F
. Here
u^{(n-1)}:={\rm diag}(u_{1},u_{2},...,u_{n-1},0)
.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
\Phi_{n-1}(z_{n-1};\Phi_{n-2})\in{\rm Herm}(n)
denote the solution of the isomonodromy equation (
50
) with the boundary value
\Phi_{n-2}(z_{1},...,z_{n-2})
at
z_{n-1}=\infty
in the sense of Proposition
3.4
. Then we have the identity

\displaystyle C\left(u,\Phi_{n-1}(z_{n-1};\Phi_{n-2})\right)\cdot e^{\Phi_{n-1}}\cdot C\left(u,\Phi_{n-1}(z_{n-1};\Phi_{n-2})\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{-}\left(u,\Phi_{n-1}(z_{n-1};\Phi_{n-2})\right)S_{+}\left(u,\Phi_{n-1}(z_{n-1};\Phi_{n-2})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}{\left(C\left(u^{(n-1)},\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})\right)\cdot\left({u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}}\right)^{-\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot C\left(E_{n},\Phi_{n-2}\right)\right)}e^{\Phi_{n-2}}.

(80)

Proof.
The first identity simply follows from the monodromy relation (
93
). We prove the second identity by showing that the left and right hand sides compute respectively the monodromy of the linear system of equation (
3
) along two homotopy paths. Since the paths are homotopy, the monodromy are equal.

Let us assume
n>2
(for the
n=2
case the involved system can be solved exactly). Let us fix the
n-1
variables
\widetilde{z_{0}}=u_{1}
and
z_{1},...,z_{n-2}
. Thus
\Phi_{n-2}(z_{1},...,z_{n-2})\in{\rm Herm}(n)
is constant, and
u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}=z_{1}z_{2}\cdots z_{n-2}
is a constant real number. The compatible linear system of PDEs (
1
)-(
2
) reduces to the equation with respect to
z
and
z_{n-1}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial z}

\displaystyle=\left({\mathrm{i}}u-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\Phi_{n-1}(z_{n-1};\Phi_{n-2})}{z}\right)F,

(81)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial z_{n-1}}

\displaystyle=\left({\mathrm{i}}(u_{n-1}-u_{n-2})E_{n}z-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}{\rm ad}^{-1}_{u}{\rm ad}_{(u_{n-1}-u_{n-2})E_{n}}\Phi_{n-1}(z_{n-1};\Phi_{n-2})\right)F.

(82)

Let
F_{\pm}(z,z_{n-1})
be the canonical fundamental solutions of the first equation with the prescribed asymptotics

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}F_{+}(z,z_{n-1})\cdot e^{-{\mathrm{i}}uz}\cdot z^{\frac{[\Phi_{n-1}]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

\displaystyle={\rm Id}_{n},\ \ \ \text{as}\ \ \ z\in S(-\pi,\pi),

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}F_{-}(z,z_{n-1})\cdot e^{-{\mathrm{i}}uz}\cdot z^{\frac{[\Phi_{n-1}]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

\displaystyle={\rm Id}_{n},\ \ \ \text{as}\ \ \ z\in S(-2\pi,0).

Then following Proposition
3.3
, the canonical solutions
F_{\pm}(z,z_{n-1})
of the first equation also satisfy the second equation, and therefore are solutions of the compatible system (
81
)-(
82
).

By definition, for any fixed
z_{n-1}
, the monodromy of
F_{+}(z,z_{n-1})
, along a loop
\gamma_{1}(t)=(\frac{e^{-i\theta}}{\varepsilon},z_{n-1})
around
(\infty,z_{n-1})
, is just the first row in (
80
).
Another loop homotopy to
\gamma_{1}
is
\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{3}\circ\gamma_{2}^{-1}
, where
\gamma_{2}
is a simple path from
(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},z_{n-1})
to
(\varepsilon,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}})
, and
\gamma_{3}(\theta)=(e^{-i\theta}\varepsilon,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}})
a loop around
(0,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}})
. To show the monodromy along
\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{3}\circ\gamma_{2}^{-1}
coincide with the last row in (
80
), we need to compute respectively the monodromy along the loops
\gamma_{2}
and
\gamma_{3}
. This can be done by choosing certain reference solution
Y_{+}(z,z_{n-1})
around
(\varepsilon,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}})
.

To introduce
Y_{+}(z,z_{n-1})
, first note that under the change of coordinates

x=z\ \ \text{ and }\ \ y=(u_{n-1}-u_{n-2})\cdot zz_{n-1},

(here by assumption
u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}=z_{1}z_{2}\cdots z_{n-2}
is a fixed positive real number) the systems (
81
) and (
82
) become (for simplicity, we write
\Phi_{n-1}
for
\Phi_{n-1}(z_{n-1};\Phi_{n-2})
)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial x}=\left({\mathrm{i}}u^{(n-1)}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-1})}{x}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}{\rm ad}_{D_{1}}{\rm ad}_{E_{n}}\Phi_{n-1}\right)F,

(83)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}=\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{n}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}{\rm ad}_{D_{2}}{\rm ad}_{E_{n}}\Phi_{n-1}\right)F,

(84)

where

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle={\rm diag}\left(\frac{u_{1}}{y+x(u_{n-1}-u_{1})},\frac{u_{2}}{y+x(u_{n-1}-u_{2})},...,\frac{u_{n-1}}{y},0\right),

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle={\rm diag}\left(\frac{1}{y+x(u_{n-1}-u_{1})},\frac{1}{y+x(u_{n-1}-u_{2})},...,\frac{1}{y},0\right)

are two
n\times n
diagonal matrices. Here recall that
\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-1})
is defined in (
3.2
).

Then for any fixed
y\neq 0
,

•

let
G_{0}(x,y)
be the solution of equation (
83
) with the asymptotics
G_{0}\cdot x^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\sim 1
at
x=0
. The existence of such a solution can be seen by the estimation of the coefficient matrix of equation (
83
) at
x=0
: recall that
z_{n-1}=\frac{y}{(u_{n-1}-u_{n-2})x}
, by the proof of Proposition
3.4
, we have that

\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-1})-\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})=\mathcal{O}(z_{n-1}^{-1})=\mathcal{O}(x),

and that
\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}{\rm ad}_{D_{1}}{\rm ad}_{E_{n}}\Phi_{n-1}
has a limit at
x=0
.

•

Let
F^{(n)}_{\pm}(y)
be the canonical solution of
\frac{dF}{dy}=\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{n}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\Phi_{n-2}}{y}\right)F
in the two Stokes sectors (right and left half planes).

Lemma 3.13
.

The functions
Y_{\pm}(x,y):=G_{0}(x,y)\cdot(\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot F^{(n)}_{\pm}(y)
satisfy the equations (
83
) and (
84
).

Proof.
We denote the coefficients of (
83
) and (
84
) by
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
respectively. By the compatibility of the two equations, we have that
(d_{x}-A_{1})(d_{y}G_{0}-A_{2}G_{0})=0
.
Since
(d_{x}-A_{1})G_{0}=0
, we can set
d_{y}G_{0}-A_{2}G_{0}=G_{0}X(y)
for a function
X(y)
of
y
. That is
X(y)=G_{0}^{-1}d_{y}G_{0}-G_{0}^{-1}A_{2}G_{0}
. To get the expression of
X(y)
, we use the asymptotics of
G_{0}
and
A_{2}
to compute the asymptotics of
G_{0}^{-1}d_{y}G_{0}-G_{0}^{-1}A_{2}G_{0}
as
x\rightarrow 0
(while fixing
y
).

Firstly, it follows from the asymptotics of
\Phi_{n-1}
that the asymptotics of
G_{0}^{-1}A_{2}G_{0}
as
x\rightarrow 0
along the real axis is

\displaystyle G_{0}^{-1}\cdot\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{n}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}{\rm ad}_{D_{2}}{\rm ad}_{E_{n}}\Phi_{n-1}\right)\cdot G_{0}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\ x^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{n}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}{\rm ad}_{D_{2}}{\rm ad}_{E_{n}}\Phi_{n-1}\right)\cdot x^{-\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\ (\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{n}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\Phi_{n-2}-\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{y}\right)\cdot(\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{-\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}.

Here the norms
\lVert x^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\rVert=1
,
\lVert(\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\rVert=1
and in the last step, we use the asymptotics of
\Phi_{n-1}
and
D_{2}
as
x\rightarrow 0
(recall
y=({u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})xz_{n-1}
)

\displaystyle{z_{n-1}}^{\frac{-\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot\Phi_{n-1}\cdot{z_{n-1}}^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\sim\Phi_{n-2},\hskip 14.22636pt\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-1})\sim\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2}),\hskip 14.22636ptD_{2}\sim{\rm diag}\left(\frac{1}{y},...,\frac{1}{y},0\right),

to get

\displaystyle x^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot({\rm ad}_{D_{2}}{\rm ad}_{E_{n}}\Phi_{n-1})\cdot x^{-\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\ (\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot\frac{\Phi_{n-2}-\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{y}\cdot(\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{-\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}.

Secondly, the limit of
G_{0}^{-1}d_{y}G_{0}
is zero as
x\rightarrow 0
.
Therefore, we have computed the limit of
G_{0}^{-1}d_{y}G_{0}-G_{0}^{-1}A_{2}G_{0}
as
x\rightarrow 0
, and deduce that
d_{y}G_{0}-A_{2}G_{0}=G_{0}\cdot X(y)
, for

X(y)=(\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{n}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\Phi_{n-2}-\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{y}\right)\cdot(\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{-\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}.

By the explicit formula of
X(y)
and the defining equation of
F_{\pm}^{(n)}
, one verifies directly that

d_{y}\left((\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}F^{(n)}_{\pm}\right)=X(y)\cdot(\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}F^{(n)}_{\pm}.

Therefore
Y_{\pm}=G_{0}\cdot(\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}F^{(n)}_{\pm}
satisfy the equation
d_{y}Y_{\pm}=A_{2}Y_{\pm}
, i.e., the equation (
84
).

Under the coordinates transformation, the function
Y_{+}(x,y)
is viewed as solutions of (
81
) and (
82
) with prescribed asymptotics at
(z,z_{n-1})=(\varepsilon,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}})
.

Lemma 3.14
.

The monodromy of
Y_{+}
along the loop
\gamma_{3}
coincides with the monodromy of
F^{(n)}_{+}
around
y=\infty
, i.e., the monodromy
C(E_{n},\Phi_{n-2})e^{\Phi_{n-2}}C(E_{n},\Phi_{n-2})^{-1}
of
\frac{dF}{dy}=\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{n}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\Phi_{n-2}}{y}\right)F
around
y=\infty
.

Proof.
On the one hand, in the
(z,z_{n-1})
coordinates,
\gamma_{3}(\theta)=(z(\theta),z_{n-1}(\theta))=(e^{-i\theta}\varepsilon,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}})
is the loop around
(0,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}})
. On the other hand, we have the coordinates transformation
y=(u_{n-1}-u_{n-2})zz_{n-1}
and
x=z
. Thus in
(x,y)
coordinates,
\gamma_{3}
is a loop
\gamma_{3}(\theta)=(x(\theta),y(\theta))=(e^{-i\theta}\varepsilon,\frac{e^{-i\theta}}{\varepsilon})
, that is a loop around
x=0
and
y=\infty
. Analytic continuation along such a loop gives

G_{0}(e^{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}x,e^{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}y)=G_{0}(x,y)e^{-\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})},

and

\displaystyle(\frac{e^{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot F^{(n)}_{\pm}(e^{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}x,e^{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}y)

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle e^{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}(\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot F^{(n)}_{\pm}(x,y)C(E_{n},\Phi_{n-2})e^{\Phi_{n-2}}C(E_{n},\Phi_{n-2})^{-1}.

The result follows from the above two identities and the expression
Y_{+}=G_{0}\cdot(\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}F^{(n)}_{+}
.

The rest is to compare the two solutions
Y_{+}
and
F_{+}
of the same equation (
81
) (to get the monodromy along the path
\gamma_{2}
). For that, we only need to study the asymptotics of
F_{+}^{-1}Y_{+}
as
z_{n-1}\gg z\gg 0
.

Let us first study the asymptotics of
Y_{+}
. For any
x=z
, as
z_{n-1}=y/(u_{n-1}-u_{n-2})x\rightarrow+\infty
the equation (
83
) approaches to (here we use the fact
\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-1})\rightarrow\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})
as
z_{n-1}\rightarrow\infty
and
\Phi_{n-1}
is bounded, see Proposition
3.4
)

\displaystyle\frac{dF}{dx}=\left({\mathrm{i}}u^{(n-1)}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{x}\right)F,

(85)

and
G_{0}(x,y)
approaches to the fundamental solution
T_{0}(x)
of (
85
) with the asymptotics
T_{0}x^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\sim 1
at
x=0
. Let
T_{+}
be the canonical solution of (
85
) around
x=\infty
in the Stokes sector
{\rm Sect}_{+}
(right half plane), then by the definition of connection matrix, we have

T_{+}=T_{0}\cdot C(u^{(n-1)},\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})).

Then from the expression
Y_{+}(x,y)=G_{0}(x,y)\cdot(\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}F^{(n)}_{+}(y)
, the asymptotics of
F^{(n)}_{+}(y)
as
y\rightarrow+\infty
and the fact that
G_{0}
approaches to
T_{0}
, we have that for any fixed real number
x=z
, as
z_{n-1}\rightarrow+\infty
or equivalently
y\rightarrow+\infty
,

\displaystyle\lim_{y\rightarrow\infty}\left(Y_{+}(x,y)\cdot({u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot C(u^{(n-1)},\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2}))^{-1}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}ux}x^{\frac{[\Phi_{n-2}]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{y\rightarrow\infty}\Big(T_{0}(x)(\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}(1+\mathcal{O}(y^{-1}))(\frac{y}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{-\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \times C(u^{(n-1)},\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2}))^{-1}e^{{\mathrm{i}}yE_{n}}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}ux}x^{\frac{[\Phi_{n-2}]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{0}(x)C(u^{(n-1)},\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2}))^{-1}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}xu^{(n-1)}}x^{\frac{[\Phi_{n-2}]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{+}(x)e^{-{\mathrm{i}}xu^{(n-1)}}x^{\frac{[\Phi_{n-2}]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}.

(86)

Here the first identity uses the asymptotics
F^{(n)}_{+}(y)=(1+\mathcal{O}(y^{-1}))y^{-\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}yE_{n}}
, and the second identity uses the fact that the norm
|y^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}|=1
for
y\in\mathbb{R}
.
By the prescribed asymptotics of the canonical solution
T_{+}(x)
at
x=+\infty
, the right hand side of (
86
) approaches to the identity matrix as
x\rightarrow+\infty
. Thus, we get (after changing
(x,y)
to
(z,z_{n-1})
and restricting
z
to be real)

\displaystyle\lim_{z_{n-1}\gg z\gg 0}\left(Y_{+}(z,u_{n})\cdot({u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot C\left(u^{(n-1)},\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})\right)^{-1}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}uz}z^{\frac{[\Phi_{n-1}]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)={\rm Id}_{n}.

(87)

Here we use that the diagonal part
[\Phi_{n-1}]
of
\Phi_{n-1}
equals to the diagonal part
[\Phi_{n-2}]
of
\Phi_{n-2}
.

Let us now study the asymptotics of
F_{+}
. By Theorem
2.3
we have that (while fixing
u_{1},...,u_{n-1}
)

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow+\infty}\left(F_{+}(z,z_{n-1})e^{-{\mathrm{i}}uz}z^{\frac{[\Phi_{n-1}]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)={\rm Id}_{n},\hskip 8.53581pt\text{uniformly for }u_{n}>u_{n-1}+1.

(88)

Therefore, the limits (
87
) and (
88
) imply that as
z_{n-1}\gg z\gg 0
(for
z\in\mathbb{R}
)

\displaystyle e^{{\mathrm{i}}uz}z^{-\frac{[\Phi_{n-1}]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}F_{+}^{-1}Y_{+}\cdot({u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot C\left(u^{(n-1)},\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-1})\right)^{-1}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}uz}z^{\frac{[\Phi_{n}]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\rightarrow{\rm Id}_{n}.

(89)

Since
e^{{\mathrm{i}}uz}z^{-\frac{[\Phi_{n}]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}
is a diagonal matrix whose all diagonal elements have norm
1
for all
z>0
, the limit (
89
) gives rise to

Y_{+}=F_{+}\cdot C(u^{(n-1)},\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2}))\cdot({u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{-\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}.

That computes the monodromy along
\gamma_{2}
.

In summary, the monodromy
M(\gamma_{i})
along
\gamma_{i}
with respect to the reference solutions
F_{+}
and
Y_{+}
are

\displaystyle M\left({\gamma_{1}};F_{+}\mapsto F_{+}(ze^{-2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})\right)\colon

\displaystyle\ S_{-}\left(u,\Phi_{n-1}(u_{n};\Phi_{n-1})\right)S_{+}\left(u,\Phi_{n-1}(u_{n};\Phi_{n-2})\right);

\displaystyle M\left({\gamma_{2}};F_{+}\mapsto Y_{+}\right)\colon

\displaystyle\ C\left(u^{(n-1)},\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-1})\right)\cdot({u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}})^{-\frac{\delta_{n-1}(\Phi_{n-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}};

\displaystyle M\left({\gamma_{3}};Y_{+}(x,y)\mapsto Y_{+}(e^{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}x,e^{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}y)\right)\colon

\displaystyle C(E_{n},\Phi_{n-1})e^{\Phi_{n-1}}C(E_{n},\Phi_{n-1})^{-1}.

The proposition now follows from that the two loops
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{3}\circ\gamma_{2}^{-1}
are homotopy.

Similarly, we can prove a generalization of Proposition
3.12
.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
\Phi_{n-1}(z_{1},...,z_{n-1})\in{\rm Herm}(n)
be the solution of the isomonodromy equation (
50
), and
\Phi_{k}(z_{1},...,z_{k})
for
k=1,...,n-1
the corresponding chain of functions given as in Proposition
3.4
. Then for each
k
we have the identity

\displaystyle C\left(u^{(k+1)},\delta_{k+1}(\Phi_{k})\right)\cdot\left({u_{k+1}-u_{k}}\right)^{\frac{\delta_{k+1}(\Phi_{k})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot e^{\Phi_{k}}\cdot\left({u_{k+1}-u_{k}}\right)^{-\frac{\delta_{k+1}(\Phi_{k})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}C\left(u^{(k+1)},\delta_{k+1}(\Phi_{k})\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}{\left(C\left(u^{(k)},\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1})\right)\cdot\left({u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\right)^{\frac{\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot C\left(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(\Phi_{k-1})\right)\right)}e^{\Phi_{k-1}}.

(90)

Here recall that

•

C(E_{k+1},\Phi_{k-1})\subset{\rm U}(n)
, the connection matrix of
\frac{dF}{dz}=({\mathrm{i}}E_{k+1}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\Phi_{k-1}}{z})F
;

•

C(u^{(k)},\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1}))\in{\rm U}(n)
, the connection matrix of the
n\times n
system
\frac{dF}{dz}=(u^{(k)}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1})}{z})F
with
u^{(k)}:={\rm diag}(u_{1},...,u_{k},0,...0)
.

The proof is omitted due to the similarity with Proposition
3.12
. Actually Proposition
3.15
is simply reduced to the case of Proposition
3.12
, in aware of Remark
3.9
.

3.5
Recursive branching rules and expression of Stokes matrices via the boundary value

In this subsection, let us consider the recursive branching of the system (
28
) as
\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\rightarrow+\infty
(i.e.,
z_{k}\rightarrow+\infty
) for all
k=2,...,n-1
.
The recursive procedure in Section
3.4
implies that for any
A\in{{\rm Herm}}(n)
and
1\leq k\leq n
, we need to consider the system

\displaystyle\frac{dF}{dz}=\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{k}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\delta_{k}(A)}{z}\right)F,

(91)

where
E_{k}={\rm diag}(0,...,0,1,0,..,0)
with
1
at the
k
-th position.
For any
1\leq k\leq n
, we take the obvious inclusion of
U(k)
as the upper left corner of
U(n)
, extended
by
1
’s along the diagonal.
Since
U(k-1)\in U(n)
is in the centralizer of the irregular term
{\mathrm{i}}E_{k}
of the equation (
91
), the connection matrix
C(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A))
of (
91
) has the following
{U}(k-1)
-equivariance.

Lemma 3.16
.

For any
g\in{U}(k-1)\subset{U}(n)
,

C\left(E_{k},g\delta_{k}(A)g^{-1}\right)=g\cdot C\left(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A)\right)\cdot g^{-1}.

Theorem 3.17
.

For any solution
\Phi(u;\Phi_{0})
of the isomonodromy equation (
50
) on
u\in U_{\rm id}
with the boundary value
\Phi_{0}\in{\rm Herm}(n)
at
u_{\rm cat}
(as in Theorem
1.1
), we have

S_{-}(u,\Phi(u;\Phi_{0}))S_{+}(u,\Phi(u;\Phi_{0}))=\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=2,...,n}}C\left(E_{k},\delta_{k}(\Phi_{0})\right)\right)\cdot e^{\Phi_{0}}\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=2,...,n}}C\left(E_{k},\delta_{k}(\Phi_{0})\right)\right)^{-1},

where the product
\overrightarrow{\prod}
is taken with the index
i
to the right of
j
if
j<i
.

Proof.
It can be proved by recursively using Proposition
3.12
and Proposition
3.15
.
For any
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
and
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, let us denote by
R_{1}(u^{(1)},A)=A
, and for each
k=2,...,n
,

\displaystyle R_{k}(u^{(k)},A):=C(u^{(k)},\delta_{k}(A))\cdot\left({u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\right)^{\frac{\delta_{k}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}A\cdot\left({u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\right)^{-\frac{\delta_{k}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot C(u^{(k)},\delta_{k}(A))^{-1},

where
u^{(k)}={\rm diag}(u_{1},u_{2},..,u_{k},0,...,0)
.
Then Proposition
3.15
states that

\displaystyle R_{k}(u^{(k)},\Phi_{k-1})

(92)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}{\left(C\left(u^{(k-1)},\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})\right)\left({u_{k-1}-u_{k-2}}\right)^{\frac{\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}C\left(E_{k},\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-2})\right)\right)}{\Phi_{k-2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}\left({C\left(E_{k},\delta_{k}\left(R_{k-1}(u^{(k-1)},\Phi_{k-2})\right)\right)}\right)R_{k-1}(u^{(k-1)},\Phi_{k-2}).

Here in the second identity we use the
{\rm U}(k-1)
-equivariant property in Lemma
3.16
(note that
C(u^{(k-1)},\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{n-2}))\in U(k-1)\subset U(n)
)

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}{\left(C\left(u^{(k-1)},\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})\right)\left({u_{k-1}-u_{k-2}}\right)^{\frac{\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)}C\left(E_{k},\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-2})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C\left(E_{k},\ {\rm Ad}{\left(C\left(u^{(k-1)},\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})\right)\left({u_{k-1}-u_{k-2}}\right)^{\frac{\delta_{k-1}(\Phi_{k-2})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)}\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-2})\right).

Replacing
k
by
k-1
in (
92
) we get

\displaystyle R_{k-1}(u^{(k-1)},\Phi_{k-2})={\rm Ad}\left({C\left(E_{k-1},\delta_{k-1}\left(R_{k-2}(u^{(k-2)},\Phi_{k-3})\right)\right)}\right)R_{k-2}(u^{(k-2)},\Phi_{k-3}).

Then replacing
R_{k-1}
by
R_{k-2}
in the right hand side of the identity (
92
) and using
U(k-1)
- equivariant property

\displaystyle{C\left(E_{k},\ {\rm Ad}\left({C\left(E_{k-1},\delta_{k-1}\left(R_{k-2}(u^{(k-2)},\Phi_{k-3})\right)\right)}\right)\delta_{k}\left(R_{k-2}(u^{(k-2)},\Phi_{k-3})\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{{\rm Ad}\left({C\left(E_{k-1},\delta_{k-1}\left(R_{k-2}(u^{(k-2)},\Phi_{k-3})\right)\right)}\right)C\left(E_{k},\delta_{k}\left(R_{k-2}(u^{(k-2)},\Phi_{k-3})\right)\right)},

we derive

\displaystyle R_{k}(u^{(k)},\delta_{k}(\Phi_{k-1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}\left({C\left(E_{k},\delta_{k}(R_{k-1}(u^{(k-1)},\Phi_{k-2}))\right)}\right)R_{k-1}(u^{(k-1)},\Phi_{k-2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}\left({{C\left(E_{k-1},\delta_{k-1}(R_{k-2}(u^{(k-2)},\Phi_{k-3}))\right)}C\left(E_{k},\delta_{k}(R_{k-2}(u^{(k-2)},\Phi_{k-3}))\right)}\right)R_{k-2}(u^{(k-2)},\Phi_{k-3}).

Keep doing this for
k=n-1,n-2,...,2
and note that
R_{1}(u^{(1)},\Phi_{0})=\Phi_{0}
, we get

C\left(u,\Phi_{n-1}(u;\Phi_{0})\right)\cdot e^{\Phi_{n-1}}\cdot C\left(u,\Phi_{n-1}(u;\Phi_{0})\right)^{-1}=\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=2,...,n}}C\left(E_{k},\delta_{k}(\Phi_{0})\right)\right)\cdot e^{\Phi_{0}}\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=2,...,n}}C\left(E_{k},\delta_{k}(\Phi_{0})\right)\right)^{-1}.

Therefore, by the monodromy relation, we get

\displaystyle S_{-}\left(u,\Phi_{n-1}(u;\Phi_{0})\right)S_{+}\left(u,\Phi_{n-1}(u;\Phi_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=C\left(u,\Phi_{n-1}(u;\Phi_{0})\right)\cdot e^{\Phi_{n-1}}\cdot C\left(u,\Phi_{n-1}(u;\Phi_{0})\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=2,...,n}}C\left(E_{k},\delta_{k}(\Phi_{0})\right)\right)\cdot e^{\Phi_{0}}\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=2,...,n}}C\left(E_{k},\delta_{k}(\Phi_{0})\right)\right)^{-1}.

It finishes the proof.

Theorem
3.17
motivates the following definitions. Let us denote by
u_{\rm cat}
the infinite point
z_{1}\rightarrow 0+,z_{k}\rightarrow+\infty
for all
k
.

Definition 3.18
.

For any
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, the connection matrix
C(u_{\rm cat},A)
, at the infinite point
u_{\rm cat}
with respect to
U_{\rm id}
, is the pointwise ordered multiplication of all
C(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A))
’s for
k=1,...,n
. That is

C(u_{\rm cat},A):=C(E_{1},\delta_{1}(A))C(E_{2},\delta_{2}(A))\cdot\cdot\cdot C(E_{n},\delta_{n}(A)),\ \ \ \text{for any}\ A\in{\rm Herm}_{0}(n).

Definition 3.19
.

For any
A\in{{\rm Herm}}(n)
, the
Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u_{\rm cat},A)
at
u_{\rm cat}
, with respect to the connected component
U_{\rm cat}
, are respectively the upper and lower triangular matrices uniquely determined by the identity (Gauss decomposition)

\displaystyle S_{-}(u_{\rm cat},A)S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)=C(u_{\rm cat},A)e^{A}C(u_{\rm cat},A)^{-1},

(93)

and by imposing the diagonal part
[S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)]=[S_{-}(u_{\rm cat},A)]=e^{\frac{[A]}{2}}
.

It follows from Theorem
3.17
that
Definition
93
coincides with the Definition
1.6
given in the introduction. That is

Corollary 3.20
.

For any
u\in U_{\rm id}
, the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,\Phi(u;\Phi_{0}))
coincides with
S_{\pm}(u_{\rm cat},\Phi_{0})
.

3.6
Explicit expression of Stokes matrices in terms of Gelfand-Tsetlin coordinates

In this subsection, we will give an explicit formula of the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u_{\rm cat},A)
for any
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, and prove Theorem
1.2
. To this end, we only need to compute explicitly the connection matrices of the system (
91
) for all
1\leq k\leq n
. The computational procedure is better to be understood in terms of the Gelfand-Tsetlin integrable systems
[
38
]
. So before computing the Stokes matrices, let us introduce the action and angle coordinates of the Gelfand-Tsetlin systems.

3.6.1
The Gelfand-Tsetlin coordinates

Gelfand-Tsetlin maps.
For
k\leq n
let
A^{(k)}\in{\rm Herm}(k)
denote the upper left
k
-th submatrix (upper left
k\times k
corner) of a Hermitian matrix
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
,
and
\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(A)
-its ordered set of
eigenvalues,
\lambda_{1}^{(k)}(A)\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{k}^{(k)}(A)
.
The map

\lambda\colon{\rm Herm}(n)\to\mathbb{R}^{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}},

(94)

taking
A
to the collection of numbers
\lambda_{i}^{(k)}(A)
for
1\leq i\leq k\leq n
, is continuous and is called the Gelfand-Tsetlin map.
Its image
\mathcal{C}(n)
is the Gelfand-Tsetlin cone, cut out by the following inequalities,

\lambda_{i}^{(k+1)}\geq\lambda_{i}^{(k)}\geq\lambda_{i+1}^{(k+1)},\ \ 1\leq i\leq k\leq n-1.

(95)

Thimm torus actions.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{0}(n)\subset\mathcal{C}(n)
denote the subset where all of the eigenvalue inequalities
(
95
) are strict. Let
{\rm Herm}_{0}(n):=\lambda^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{0}(n))
be the corresponding dense open subset of
{\rm Herm}(n)
. The
k
-torus

T(k)\subset{\rm U}(k)
of diagonal matrices acts on

{\rm Herm}_{0}(n)
as follows,

t\bullet A=\mathrm{Ad}_{U^{-1}tU}A,\ \ \ \ t\in T(k),\ A\in{\rm Herm}_{0}(n).

(96)

Here
U\in{\rm U}(k)\subset{\rm U}(n)
is a unitary matrix such that
\mathrm{Ad}_{U}A^{(k)}
is
diagonal, with entries
\lambda_{1}^{(k)},\ldots,\lambda_{k}^{(k)}
. The action
is well-defined since
U^{-1}tU
does not depend on the choice of

U
, and preserves the Gelfand-Tsetlin map (
94
). The actions
of the various
T(k)
’s commute, hence they define an action of the Gelfand-Tsetlin torus
T(1)\times\cdots\times T(n-1)\cong{\rm U}(1)^{\frac{(n-1)n}{2}}.

Here the torus
T(n)
is excluded, since the
action (
96
) is trivial for
k=n
.

Action-angle coordinates.

If
A\in\rm Herm_{0}(n)
, then there exists a unique unitary matrix
P_{k}(A)\in{\rm U}(k)\subset{\rm U}(n)
, whose entries in the
k
-th row are positive and real, such that the upper left
k
-th submatrix of
A_{k}:=P_{k}(A)^{-1}AP_{k}(A)
is the diagonal matrix
{\rm diag}(\lambda^{(k)}_{1},...,\lambda^{(k)}_{k})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle A_{k}=P_{k}(A)^{-1}AP_{k}(A)=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda^{(k)}_{1}&&&&a^{(k)}_{1}&\cdots\\
&\ddots&&&\vdots&\cdots\\
&&\lambda^{(k)}_{k}&&a^{(k)}_{k}&\cdots\\
&&&&\\
\overline{a_{1}^{(k)}}&\cdots&\overline{a_{k}^{(k)}}&&\lambda_{k+1}^{(k)}&\cdots\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&&\cdots&\cdots\end{pmatrix}.

(97)

The
(i,k+1)
entries
a^{(k)}_{i}(A)
, for
1\leq i\leq k\leq n-1
, are seen as functions on
{\rm Herm}_{0}(n)
.
Then the functions
\{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq k\leq n}
and
\{\psi^{(k)}_{i}={\rm Arg}(a^{(k)}_{i})\}_{1\leq i\leq k\leq n-1}
on
{\rm Herm}_{0}(n)
are called the Gelfand-Tsetlin action and angle coordinates.

3.6.2
Diagonalization in stages

Now let us consider the system (
91
). By the
{\rm U}(k-1)
-equivariant property in Lemma
3.16
, to simplify the computation, we can first diagonalize the upper left
k-1
-th submatrix of
A
. According to Section
3.6.1
, there is a systematic way to do it for all
A\in{\rm Herm}_{0}(n)
via the unique unitary matrix
P_{k}(A)
in (
97
).

These unitary matrices can also be inductively defined: suppose that we already have
P_{k}(A)\in{\rm U}(k)\subset{\rm U}(n)
for
k<n
, such that
A_{{k}}=P_{k}(A)^{-1}AP_{k}(A)
is the form in (
97
). Let
L^{(k+1)}(A)\in{\rm U}(k+1)\subset{\rm U}(n)
be the matrix given by

\displaystyle L^{(k+1)}_{ij}(A):=\frac{a^{(k)}_{i}}{N_{j}^{(k+1)}(\lambda^{(k)}_{i}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j})},\ \text{for}\ i\neq k+1,\ j=1,...,k+1,

(98)

\displaystyle L^{(k+1)}_{k+1,j}(A):=\frac{1}{N_{j}^{(k+1)}},\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for}\ j=1,...,k+1,

(99)

where the normalizer

N_{j}^{(k+1)}(A):=\sqrt{1+\sum_{l=1}^{k}\frac{|a_{l}^{(k)}|^{2}}{(\lambda^{(k)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j})^{2}}}=\sqrt{\frac{\prod_{v=1,v\neq j}^{k+1}(\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{v})}{\prod_{v=1}^{k}(\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}-\lambda^{(k)}_{v})}}.

(100)

(Here the second identity follows from the combinatorial identity given by computing the character polynomial of the upper left
k+1
-th submatrix of
A_{k}
in two different ways.)
The upper left
k+1
-th submatrix of
L^{(k+1)}(A)^{-1}A_{k}L^{(k+1)}(A)
is
{\rm diag}(\lambda^{(k+1)}_{1},...,\lambda^{(k+1)}_{k+1})
and the entries in
k+1
-th row of
L^{(k+1)}(A)
is real and positive, thus we can simply define
P_{k+1}(A)
by

P_{k+1}(A):=P_{k}(A)\cdot L^{(k+1)}(A).

(101)
