Furthermore, using the Laplace expansion, we see that for any
2\leq k\leq n
, the
n\times n
matrix
P_{k}(A)
has the explicit form

\displaystyle(P_{k})_{ij}:=

\displaystyle{(-1)^{k+i}}\frac{\Delta_{1,...,\hat{i},...,k}^{1,...,k-1}\left(A-\lambda^{(k)}_{j}\right)}{\sqrt{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}(\lambda^{(k)}_{j}-\lambda^{(k)}_{l})\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}(\lambda^{(k)}_{j}-\lambda^{(k-1)}_{l})}},\ \text{if}\ 1\leq i,j\leq k

(102)

\displaystyle(P_{k})_{ii}:=

\displaystyle 1,\ \ \text{if}\ i>k,

\displaystyle(P_{k})_{ij}:=

\displaystyle 0,\ \ \text{otherwise},

and its inverse is,

\displaystyle(P_{k}^{-1})_{ij}=

\displaystyle{(-1)^{k+j}}\frac{\Delta^{1,...,\hat{j},...,k}_{1,...,k-1}\left(A-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}\right)}{\sqrt{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}(\lambda^{(k)}_{i}-\lambda^{(k)}_{l})\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}(\lambda^{(k)}_{i}-\lambda^{(k-1)}_{l})}},\ \text{if}\ 1\leq i,j\leq k

(103)

\displaystyle(P_{k}^{-1})_{ii}=

\displaystyle 1,\ \ \text{if}\ i>k,

\displaystyle(P_{k}^{-1})_{ij}=

\displaystyle 0,\ \ \text{otherwise}.

Therefore, by definition the function

a^{(k)}_{i}(A)=\sum_{v=1}^{n}(P_{k}(A)^{-1})_{iv}\cdot(A)_{v,k+1}=\frac{(-1)^{k+i}\Delta^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(A-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}\right)}{\sqrt{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}(\lambda^{(k)}_{i}-\lambda^{(k)}_{l})\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}(\lambda^{(k)}_{i}-\lambda^{(k-1)}_{l})}}.

(104)

Combing (
100
) and (
104
), we have the following relation that will be used in Section
3.6.5
.

Lemma 3.21
.

For any
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)_{0}
, we have the identity

\frac{a^{(k)}_{i}(A)}{N_{i}^{(k)}(A)}=\frac{\Delta^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(A-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}\right)}{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}(\lambda^{(k)}_{i}-\lambda^{(k)}_{l})}

Since
P_{k-1}(A)
commutes with
E_{k}
, the conjugation by
P_{k-1}
simplifies the equation (
91
)

\frac{dF}{dz}=\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{k}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\delta_{k}(A)}{z}\right)F

to

\frac{dF}{dz}=\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{k}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\delta_{k}(A_{k-1})}{z}\right)\cdot F.

In particular, if
F(z)
is a fundamental solution of the second equation,
then
{P}_{k-1}(A)F(z){P}_{k-1}(A)^{-1}
is a fundamental solution of the first equation.
Moreover, the Stokes/connection matrices of above two equations are related by

\displaystyle C(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A))

\displaystyle={P}_{k-1}(A)C(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A_{k-1}){P}_{k-1}(A)^{-1}

(105)

\displaystyle S_{\pm}(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A))

\displaystyle={P}_{k-1}(A)S_{+}(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A_{k-1})){P}_{k-1}(A)^{-1}.

(106)

3.6.3
Normalized connection matrices

Following (
105
), for any
A
, the product appearing in the Definition
3.18
is

\displaystyle C(E_{1},\delta_{1}(A))\cdots C(E_{k-1},\delta_{k-1}(A))C(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A))C(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A))\cdots C(E_{n},\delta_{n}(A))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cdots C(E_{k-1},\delta_{k-1}(A_{k-2})){P}_{k-2}^{-1}{P}_{k-1}\cdot C(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A_{k-1})){P}_{k-1}^{-1}{P}_{k}\cdots

(107)

It motivates to compute
C(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A_{k-1}){P}_{k-1}^{-1}{P}_{k}
directly. Let us introduce (recall that
L^{(k)}(A)={P}_{k-1}^{-1}{P}_{k}
was defined in (
98
))

Definition 3.22
.

For any integer
1\leq k\leq n
and any
A\in{\rm Herm}_{0}(n)
, we define the
normalized connection matrix

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}\left(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A_{k-1})\right):=C\left(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A_{k-1})\right)\cdot L^{(k)}(A).

(108)

Here recall from (
97
) that
A_{k-1}=P_{k-1}(A)^{-1}AP_{k-1}(A)
is the diagonalization of the upper left
(k-1)
-th submatrix of
A
.

Proposition 3.23
.

For any
A\in{\rm Herm}_{0}(n)
, if we define

\widetilde{C}(u_{\rm cat},A)=\widetilde{C}(E_{1},\delta_{1}(A))\widetilde{C}(E_{2},\delta_{2}(A))\cdot\cdot\cdot\widetilde{C}(E_{n},\delta_{n}(A))\in U(n),

as the pointwise multiplication, then we have

\widetilde{C}(u_{\rm cat},A)e^{A_{n}}\widetilde{C}(u_{\rm cat},A)^{-1}=C(u_{\rm cat},A)e^{A}C(u_{\rm cat},A)^{-1}.

Here recall that
A_{n}={\rm diag}(\lambda_{1}^{(n)},...,\lambda^{(n)}_{n})
.

Proof.
It follows from the identities (
107
), (
101
) and Definition (
108
) that
\widetilde{C}(u_{\rm cat},A)=C(u_{\rm cat},A)\cdot P_{n}(A)
. Then the proposition follows from the identity
P_{n}(A)e^{A_{n}}P_{n}(A)^{-1}=e^{A}
, i.e., the definition
A_{n}=P_{n}(A)^{-1}AP_{n}(A)
of
A_{n}
.

3.6.4
Stokes matrices of special confluent hypergeometric equations

For any
A\in{{\rm Herm}}_{0}(n)
, the Stokes matrix and normalized connection matrix of the system, for
k=1,...,n-1
,

\displaystyle\frac{dF}{dz}=\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{k+1}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})}{z}\right)\cdot F,

(109)

are described by the following proposition. Here recall that
A_{k}
is defined in (
97
).

Remark 3.24
.

The equation (
109
) is a very special type of confluent hypergeometric equation, and can be solved exactly via the confluent hypergeometric functions
{}_{k}F_{k}
. Thus the expression of its Stokes matrices simply follows from the well known asymptotics of
{}_{k}F_{k}
, following the line of
[
7
, Proposition 8]
for
n=2
case. The explicit expression has been derived in
[
6
]
, see Remark
3.27
, see also our paper
[
54
]
.
However, since our convention is different from theirs, and in order to illustrate how the computation can be generalized to the quantum case, we outline a proof using the asymptotics of
{}_{k}F_{k}
.

Proposition 3.25
.

(1).
The entries of the Stokes matrix
S_{+}\left(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)
of (
109
) are

\displaystyle(S_{+})_{j,k+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{j}+(A)_{k+1,k+1}}{4}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}-\lambda^{(k)}_{j}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k+1)}_{i}-\lambda^{(k)}_{j}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\cdot{a^{(k)}_{j}(A)},\ \ \ \text{for}\ j=1,...,k;

\displaystyle(S_{+})_{ii}

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{A_{ii}}{2}},\ \ \text{for}\ i=1,...,n,

\displaystyle(S_{+})_{ij}

\displaystyle=0,\ \ \text{otherwise}.

(2).
The entries of the normalized connection matrix
\widetilde{C}\left(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)
are given by

\widetilde{C}_{ij}=\frac{e^{\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}}{4}}}{(\lambda^{(k)}_{i}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j})}\frac{\prod_{v=1}^{k+1}\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k+1)}_{v}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})\prod_{v=1}^{k}\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{v}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod_{v=1,v\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{v}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})\prod_{v=1,v\neq j}^{k+1}\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k+1)}_{v}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\cdot\frac{a^{(k)}_{i}(A)}{N_{j}^{(k+1)}(A)},

for
1\leq j\leq k+1,1\leq i\leq k
, and

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{k+1,j}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\frac{\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}-(A)_{k+1,k+1}}{4}}\prod_{v=1}^{k+1}\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k+1)}_{v}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{N_{j}^{(k+1)}(A)\cdot\prod_{v=1}^{k}\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{v}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})},\ \ \ \text{for}\ 1\leq j\leq k+1.

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{ii}

\displaystyle=1,\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for}\ k+1<i\leq n,

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{ij}

\displaystyle=0,\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{otherwise}.

Proof.
Under certain generic additional assumptions one can explicitly compute a Floquet solution of the equation (
109
), using the generalized confluent hypergeometric functions. Recall that they are the functions, for any
m\geq 1
,
\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
,
\beta_{j}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,-1,-2,...\},1\leq j\leq m
, are

{}_{k}F_{k}(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{m},\beta_{1},...,\beta_{m};z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\alpha_{1})_{n}\cdots(\alpha_{m})_{n}}{(\beta_{1})_{n}\cdots(\beta_{m})_{n}}\frac{z^{n}}{n!},

where
(\alpha)_{0}=1
,
(\alpha)_{n}=\alpha\cdots(\alpha+n-1),

n\geq 1
.

Lemma 3.26
.

The equation (
109
) has a fundamental solution
F\left(z;E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)
taking the form

\displaystyle F\left(z;E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)=Y\cdot H\left(z;E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)\cdot z^{-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\delta_{k+1}(A_{k+1})},

(110)

where
Y={\rm diag}(a^{(k)}_{1},...,a^{(k)}_{k},1,...,1)
and
H\left(z;E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)
is the
n\times n
matrix given by

\displaystyle H(z)_{ij}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}\cdot{{}_{k}F_{k}}(\alpha_{ij,1},...,\alpha_{ij,k},\beta_{ij,1},...,\widehat{\beta_{ij,j}},...,\beta_{ij,k+1};{\mathrm{i}}z),\ \ \ 1\leq i\leq k,1\leq j\leq k+1,

\displaystyle H(z)_{k+1,j}={{}_{k}F_{k}}(\alpha_{k+1j,1},...,\alpha_{k+1j,k},\beta_{k+1j,1},...,\widehat{\beta_{k+1j,j}},...,\beta_{k+1j,k+1};{\mathrm{i}}z),\ \ i=k+1,\ 1\leq j\leq k+1,

\displaystyle H(z)_{ii}=1,\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for}\ k+1<i\leq n,

\displaystyle H(z)_{ij}=0,\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{otherwise},

with the variables
\{\alpha_{ij,l}\}
and
\{\beta_{ij,l}\}
given by

\displaystyle\alpha_{ij,i}=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}(\lambda^{(k)}_{i}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}),\ \ \ 1\leq i\leq k,\ 1\leq j\leq k+1,

\displaystyle\alpha_{k+1j,i}=1+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}(\lambda^{(k)}_{i}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}),\ \ \ 1\leq j\leq k+1,

\displaystyle\alpha_{ij,l}=1+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}(\lambda^{(k)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}),\ \ \ \ l\neq i,\ 1\leq l\leq k,\ 1\leq i,j\leq k+1,

\displaystyle\beta_{ij,l}=1+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}(\lambda^{(k+1)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}),\ \ \ \ l\neq j,\ 1\leq l\leq k+1,\ \ 1\leq i,j\leq k+1.

Proof.
The first
k
rows of the matrix equation (
109
) follows from the definition of the functions
H(z)_{ij}
and the special arguments
\alpha_{ij,l}
and
\beta_{ij,l}
, that the functions
H\left(z;E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)_{ij}
satisfy

\displaystyle z\frac{dH_{ij}}{dz}=\frac{{\small\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}H_{ij}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}H_{k+1,j},\ \ \ for\ 1\leq i\leq k.

For the rest of the equation, one just needs the identity
a_{i}^{(k)}b_{i}^{(k)}=-\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{k+1}(\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i})}{\prod_{j\neq i}^{k}(\lambda^{(k)}_{j}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i})}
, for any
1\leq i\leq k
, which follows from
the character polynomial of
A_{k}
.

The asymptotics expansion of
{}_{k}F_{k}
via gamma functions are (see
[
65
, Page 411]
),

\displaystyle\frac{\prod_{l=1}^{k}\Gamma(\alpha_{l})}{\prod_{l=1}^{k}\Gamma(\beta_{l})}\cdot{{}_{k}F_{k}}(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{k},\beta_{1},...,\beta_{k};z)

\displaystyle\sim\sum_{m=1}^{k}\Gamma(\alpha_{m})\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq m}^{k}\Gamma(\alpha_{l}-\alpha_{m})}{\prod_{l=1}^{k}\Gamma(\beta_{l}-\alpha_{m})}(\mp z)^{-\alpha_{m}}(1+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}))+e^{z}z^{\sum_{l=1}^{k}(\alpha_{l}-\beta_{l})}(1+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1})).

(111)

where upper or lower signs are chosen as
z
lies in the upper or lower half-plane. Using this, one can get explicitly the asymptotics of
F\left(z;E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)
as
z\rightarrow\infty
in the two different Stokes sectors, and its comparison with the unique formal solution of (
109
)

\hat{F}\left(z;E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)=({\rm Id}_{n}+\mathcal{O}(z^{-1}))e^{{\mathrm{i}}zE_{k+1}}z^{-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}[\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})]}.

In particular, we have

\displaystyle F\left(z;E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)\sim\hat{F}\left(z;E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)\cdot YU_{+},\ \ \ \text{as}\ z\rightarrow\ \infty\ \text{in}\ S(-\pi,\pi)

\displaystyle F\left(z;E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)\sim\hat{F}\left(z;E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)\cdot YU_{-},\ \ \ \text{as}\ z\rightarrow\ \infty\ \text{in}\ S(-2\pi,0),

where
Y={\rm diag}(a^{(k)}_{1},...,a^{(k)}_{k},1,...,1)

and
U_{\pm}
are certain explicit invertible matrices with entries given by gamma functions. Then by the uniqueness in Theorem
2.3
, we know that the canonical solutions

\displaystyle F\left(z;E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right))=F_{+}\left(z;E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)YU_{+}\hskip 8.53581ptin\ \ {\rm Sect}_{+},

\displaystyle F\left(z;E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)=F_{-}\left(z;E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)YU_{-}\hskip 8.53581ptin\ \ {\rm Sect}_{-}.

Then by definition, the Stokes matrices are given by
(here to derive the second formula,
the change in choice of
{\rm log}(z)
in
F(z)
is accounted for)

\displaystyle S_{+}\left(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)=YU_{-}U_{+}^{-1}Y^{-1},\ \ \ \ \ \ S_{-}\left(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)=YU_{-}e^{\delta_{k+1}(A_{k+1})}U_{+}^{-1}Y^{-1}.

(112)

The explicit computation of
U_{\pm}
using the asymptotics of
{}_{k}F_{k}
is straight-forward but
lengthy, and may be omitted here. For the explicit expression of
U_{\pm}
and more details on the computation, see our paper
[
54
]
. Similarly, one can get the expression of the connection matrix by considering the asymptotics of
F(z)
at
z=0
.

Remark 3.27
.

The proof in
[
6
]
didn’t make use of any known results on the global behaviour of
the functions
{}_{k}F_{k}
. In particular, the expression of the central connection factors
\widetilde{\Omega_{0}}
of the meromorphic linear system
\frac{dF}{dz}=(E_{k+1}+\frac{A}{z})\cdot F
are given in
[
6
, Formula 7.3-7.4]
. Following
[
6
, Formula 6.3]
(where our
C(E_{k+1},A_{k})
is denoted by
\Omega_{0}
there), the connection factor
\widetilde{\Omega_{0}}
is related to the connection matrix by

\widetilde{\Omega_{0}}=C(E_{k+1},A_{k})\cdot\widetilde{L}_{0},

where (see the definition in
[
6
, Formula 5.2-5.3]
) the matrix
\widetilde{L}_{0}=L^{(k+1)}\cdot D
with
D
given by

\displaystyle D_{jj}=\frac{\prod_{t=1}^{k}(\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}-\lambda^{(k)}_{t})}{\prod_{t=1}^{k}\Gamma(1+\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{t})},\ \ \ 1\leq j\leq k,

\displaystyle D_{k+1,k+1}=\frac{1}{\prod_{t=1}^{k}\Gamma(1+\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{t})}.

Thus the normalized connection matrix
C^{(k+1)}(A_{k})L^{(k+1)}
, what we are computing, differs from the connection factor
\widetilde{\Omega_{0}}
by
D
. One checks that multiplying the formula
[
6
, Formula 7.3-7.4]
for
\widetilde{\Omega_{0}}
by
D
gives rise to the formula in Proposition
3.25
(provided that the replacement of the matrix
A
by
-A
and the irregular term
E_{k+1}
by
{\mathrm{i}}E_{k+1}
are accounted for).

Remark 3.28
.

We note that the expression
P_{k}(A)F(z;A)P_{k}(A)^{-1}
, with
F(z;A)
the solution of the diagonalized equation (
109
) given as in Lemma
3.26
, smoothly extends from
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)_{0}
to
{\rm Herm}(n)
and gives the fundamental solution of the equation
91
for all
A
. In the meanwhile, the Stokes matrices of the equation
91
are just

S_{+}\left(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A)\right)=P_{k}(A)S_{+}\left(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)P_{k}(A)^{-1}

i.e., the
P_{k}(A)
conjugation of the ones of the diagonalized equation (
109
) given in Proposition
3.25
. The Stokes matrices
S_{+}\left(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A)\right)
are real analytic function with respect to all
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, which also follows from the definition of the Stokes matrices of the nonresonant equation.

3.6.5
Entries of Stokes matrices

Recall that for any
1\leq k\leq n-1
,
A^{(k)}\in{\rm Herm}_{0}(k)
denotes the upper left
k
-th submatrix (upper left
k\times k
corner) of a Hermitian matrix
A\in{\rm Herm}_{0}(n)
. For any
k
, we define a map
\widetilde{C}_{k}\colon{\rm Herm}_{0}(k)\rightarrow U(k)
by

\widetilde{C}_{k}(A^{(k)})=\widetilde{C}(E_{1},\delta_{1}(A^{(k)}))\cdots\widetilde{C}(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A^{(k)})),\hskip 8.53581pt\text{for}\ A^{(k)}\in{\rm Herm}_{0}(k).

Then we define upper and lower
k\times k
matrices
S_{k\pm}(A^{(k)})
via

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{k}(A^{(k)})e^{A^{(k)}_{k}}\widetilde{C}_{k}(A^{(k)})^{-1}=S_{k-}(A^{(k)})S_{k+}(A^{(k)}),

(113)

with the diagonal part
[S_{k+}(A^{(k)})]=[S_{k-}(A^{(k)})]=e^{\frac{A^{(k)}_{k}}{2}}
. Here recall from the definition (
97
) that the lower index
k
denotes the diagonalization of the upper left k-th submatrix of
A^{(k)}
, i.e.,
A^{(k)}_{k}
is the diagonal matrix
{\rm diag}(\lambda^{(k)}_{1},...,\lambda^{(k)}_{k})
with the ordered eigenvalues. By definition
S_{k\pm}(A^{(k)})
are just the
k\times k
Stokes matrices at
u_{\rm cat}
.

Recall that the
n\times n
Stokes matrix
S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)
at
u_{\rm cat}
is upper triangular. For any
1\leq k\leq n-1
, let us denote by
b_{k+1}(A)
the column vector consisting of the first
k
elements of the
k+1
-th column of
S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)
, i.e.,

b_{k+1}(A)=\left(S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)_{1,k+1},...,S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)_{k,k+1}\right)^{T}.

Lemma 3.29
.

The column vector
b_{k+1}(A)
is equal to the product of matrices

\displaystyle b_{k+1}(A)=S_{k+}(A^{(k)}_{{k-1}})\widetilde{C}_{k}(A^{(k)})e^{-\frac{A_{k}^{(k)}}{2}}b^{(k+1)}(A_{k}),

(114)

where by the definition (
97
)
A^{(k)}_{{k-1}}=P_{k-1}(A^{(k)})^{-1}A^{(k)}P_{k-1}(A^{(k)})
is the matrix from the diagonalization of the upper left
(k-1)
-th submatrix of
A^{(k)}
,
b^{(k+1)}(A_{k})
is the column vector consisting of the first
k
entries of the
k+1
-th column of the Stokes matrix
S_{+}\left(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)
of equation (
109
), i.e.,

b^{(k+1)}(A)=\left(S_{+}\left(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)_{1,k+1},...,S_{+}\left(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(A_{k})\right)_{k,k+1}\right)^{T}.

Proof.
It follows from the expression of the monodromy relation (
93
) that the upper left
k+1
submatrix of
S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)
coincides with
S_{k+1}(A^{(k+1)})
. So it is enough to prove the case
k=n-1
.
Using the monodromy relation

\widetilde{C}(E_{n},A)e^{A_{n}}\widetilde{C}(E_{n},A)^{-1}=S_{-}(E_{n},A_{n-1})S_{+}(E_{n},A_{n-1}),

and the expression

S_{-}(E_{n},A_{n-1})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{\frac{1}{2}A_{n-1}^{(n-1)}}&0\\
b^{(n)}(A_{n-1})^{\dagger}&\ast\end{array}\right),\ \ \ S_{+}(E_{n},A_{n-1})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{\frac{1}{2}A_{n-1}^{(n-1)}}&b^{(n)}(A_{n-1})\\
0&\ast\end{array}\right),

we get

\displaystyle S_{n-}(A)S_{n+}(A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\widetilde{C}_{n-1}(A^{(n-1)})&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\widetilde{C}(E_{n},A)e^{A_{n}}\widetilde{C}(E_{n},A)^{-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\widetilde{C}_{n-1}(A^{(n-1)})^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\widetilde{C}_{n-1}(A^{(n-1)})&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{\frac{1}{2}A_{n-1}^{(n-1)}}&0\\
{b^{(n)}}^{\dagger}&\ast\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{\frac{1}{2}A_{n-1}^{(n-1)}}&b^{(n)}\\
0&\ast\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\widetilde{C}_{n-1}(A^{(n-1)})^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\widetilde{C}_{n-1}(A^{(n-1)})e^{A_{n-1}^{(n-1)}}\widetilde{C}_{n-1}(A^{(n-1)})^{-1}&\widetilde{C}_{n-1}(A^{(n-1)})e^{\frac{1}{2}A_{n-1}^{(n-1)}}b^{(n)}\\
{b^{(n)}}^{\dagger}e^{\frac{1}{2}A_{n-1}^{(n-1)}}\widetilde{C}_{n-1}(A^{(n-1)})^{-1}&\ast\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}S_{n-1,-}&0\\
{b^{(n)}}^{\dagger}e^{\frac{1}{2}A_{n-1}^{(n-1)}}(S_{n-1,+}\widetilde{C}_{n-1})^{-1}&\ast\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}S_{n-1,+}&S_{n-1,+}\widetilde{C}_{n-1}e^{-\frac{1}{2}A_{n-1}^{(n-1)}}b^{(n)}\\
0&\ast\end{array}\right).

Here in the last equality, we use again the monodromy relation

\widetilde{C}_{n-1}(A^{(n-1)})e^{A_{n-1}^{(n-1)}}\widetilde{C}_{n-1}(A^{(n-1)})^{-1}=S_{n-1,-}\left(A_{n-2}^{(n-1)}\right)S_{n-1,+}\left(A_{n-2}^{(n-1)}\right).

Then the proof is finished for
k=n-1
.

3.6.6
The proof of Theorem
1.2

The proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Note that
(S_{+})_{k,k+1}
is the
k
-th entry of the column vector
b_{k+1}(A)
. Lemma
3.29
says that

b_{k+1}(A)=S_{k+}(A^{(k)}_{k-1})\widetilde{C}_{k}(A^{(k)})e^{-\frac{A_{k}^{(k)}}{2}}b^{(k+1)}(A_{k}).

On the one hand, the
k
-th row of
S_{k+}(A^{(k)}_{k-1})
is simply
(0,...,0,e^{\frac{1}{2}A_{kk}})
. Here
A_{kk}
is the
(k,k)
-entry of
A
. On the other hand, by the definition of
\widetilde{C}_{k}
for
k=1,...,n
, we have

\widetilde{C}_{k}(A^{(k)})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\widetilde{C}_{k-1}(A^{(k-1)})&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\cdot\widetilde{C}(E_{k},A^{(k)}).

Thus the
k
-th row of
S_{k+}(A^{(k)}_{k-1})\widetilde{C}_{k}(A^{(k)})
is just the scalar multiplication of
e^{\frac{1}{2}A_{kk}}
with the
k
-th row of the normalized connection matrix
\widetilde{C}(E_{k},A^{(k)})
.

Therefore, the
k
-th entry of the column vector
b_{k+1}(A)
is given by multiplying the
k
-th row of (the scalar product with matrix)
e^{\frac{1}{2}A_{kk}}\cdot\widetilde{C}(E_{k},A^{(k)})
with the column vector
e^{\frac{-1}{2}A_{k}^{(k)}}b^{(k+1)}(A_{k})
. By Proposition
3.25
, that is

(S_{+})_{k,k+1}=e^{\frac{(A)_{k,k}+(A)_{k+1,k+1}}{4}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod_{l=1}^{k+1}\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k+1)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k-1)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\right)\cdot\frac{a^{(k)}_{i}({A})}{N_{i}^{(k)}({A})}.

Then the expression of
(S_{+})_{k,k+1}
in the theorem follows immediately from the identity given in Lemma
3.21

\frac{a^{(k)}_{i}({A})}{N_{i}^{(k)}({A})}=2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\frac{\Delta^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(\frac{A-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)}{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}(\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}-\lambda^{(k)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}

and the fact

\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})=\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{l}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})\cdot\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}(\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}-\lambda^{(k)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}).

The expression of
(S_{-})_{k+1,k}
follows in a similar way.

Remark 3.30
.

Since each
C(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A))
smoothly depend on
A
, the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u_{\rm cat},A)
are smoothly defined on
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
. However, in Definition
3.22
we introduce the normalized connection matrix
\widetilde{C}(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A))
, associated to a systematically defined chain of matrices
P_{k}(A)
diagonalizing
A^{(k)}
. The matrix
\widetilde{C}(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A))
is only defined for
A\in{\rm Herm}_{0}(n)
, simply because
P_{k}
is only defined there (a family of matrices
P(t)
, which diagonalizes a family of
A(t)
, can be singular when
t
approaches to the point where eigenvalues of
A(t)
coincides). In this way, we only derive the explicit expression of the Stokes matrices on
{\rm Herm}_{0}(n)\subset{\rm Herm}(n)
. But one checks that the expression smoothly (actually real analytic) extends to
{\rm Herm}(n)
. See also remark
3.28
. Thus in the above computation, we can do the diagonalization in stages completely formally, i.e., ignore the issue if
P_{k}(A)
is invertible.

We also remark that it is interesting to study the behaviour of the expression in Theorem
1.2
under the toric degeneration of Gelfand-Tsetlin systems, see e.g.,
[
64
]
.

Remark 3.31
.

Apart from Poncaré rank 1 case, similar results as in Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
for other linear differential equations have been studied in the literature of Painlev
\rm{\acute{e}}
transcendents (see
[
31
]
for the history), the expression of Stokes matrices of some linear differential equations in terms of the asymptotics of the solutions of the associated nonlinear isomonodromy equations has been a major tool in the analysis of Painlev
\rm{\acute{e}}
transcendents, see
[
31
, Part II and Part III]
for the case
of Painlev
\rm{\acute{e}}
II and III. Also, in
[
37
]
, the expressions of Stokes matrices of certain differential equations with two irregular singularities were given via the asymptotics of the solutions of the associated
isomonodromy equations (which is in this case the
tt^{*}

equations of Cecotti and Vafa).

3.7
The diffeomorphism
\Phi_{u_{\rm cat}}(u)
and the existence part of Theorem
1.1

For any fixed
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, the Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff map (also known as the dual exponential map) is

\displaystyle\nu(u)\colon{\rm Herm}(n)\cong\mathfrak{u}(n)^{*}\rightarrow{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)\cong{\rm U}(n)^{*};\ A\mapsto S_{-}(u,A)S_{+}(u,A).

(115)

It follows from
[
11
, Theorem 2]
that
\nu(u)
is a diffeomorphism (a real analytic map). Similarly,

Definition 3.32
.

The Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff map at
u_{\rm cat}
is

\displaystyle\nu(u_{\rm cat})\colon{\rm Herm}(n)\rightarrow{\rm Herm}^{+}(n);\ A\mapsto S_{-}(u_{\rm cat},A)S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A).

(116)

Recall that Theorem
1.13
states that the map
\nu(u_{\rm cat})
is a Poisson diffeomorphism.
We leave a proof of Theorem
1.13
to the end of Section
4.1
. As a corollary, we get the existence part of Theorem
1.1
.

Proposition 3.33
.

Given any
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, there exists a unique solution
\Phi(u;A)
of the isomonodromy equation (
50
) on
U_{\rm id}
with the boundary value
A
at
u_{\rm cat}
.

Proof.
Since
\nu(u)
and
\nu(u_{\rm cat})
are diffeomorphisms, for any fixed
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, there exists a unique function
Y(u)\in{\rm Herm}(n)
of
u\in U_{\rm id}
such that
S_{\pm}(u,Y(u))=S_{\pm}(u_{\rm cat},A)
. Because
S_{\pm}(u_{\rm cat},A)
is independent of
u
, the function
Y(u)
is a solution of isomonodromy equation (
50
) on
U_{\rm id}
. By Theorem
1.1
and Corollary
3.20
, there exists a constant
A^{\prime}\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, as the boundary value of the solution
Y(u)
at
u_{\rm cat}
(i.e., as
\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\rightarrow+\infty
and
u_{2}-u_{1}\rightarrow 0
), such that

S_{\pm}(u_{\rm cat},A)=S_{\pm}(u,Y(u))=S_{\pm}(u_{\rm cat},A^{\prime}).

Since
\nu(u_{\rm cat})
is a diffeomorphism,
it follows that
A=A^{\prime}
, i.e., the boundary value of the solution
Y(u)
at
u_{\rm cat}
is
A
.

Definition 3.34
.

We introduce the map

\Phi_{u_{\rm cat}}(u):{\rm Herm}(n)\rightarrow{\rm Herm}(n)~;~A\mapsto\Phi(u;A),

where
\Phi(u;A)
is the solution of (
50
) with the boundary value
A
at
u_{\rm cat}
. Then by Corollary
3.20
it is just the diffeomorphism such that

\nu(u)\circ\Phi_{u_{\rm cat}}(u)=\nu(u_{\rm cat}).

3.8
The leading terms of Stokes matrices via the Gelfand-Tsetlin systems

This subsection is a proof of Proposition
1.4
. For any
B\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, denote by the unitary matrix

g(u;B):=\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=1,...,n-1}}(z_{k})^{\frac{\delta_{k}(B)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}.

(117)

Here recall that
z_{k}
are the coordinates

\displaystyle z_{0}=u_{1}+\cdots+u_{n},\ z_{1}=u_{2}-u_{1},\ z_{2}=\frac{u_{3}-u_{2}}{u_{2}-u_{1}},\ ......,\ z_{n-1}=\frac{u_{n}-u_{n-1}}{u_{n-1}-u_{n-2}}.

Since the Stokes matrices do not depend on
z_{0}
, we can assume
z_{0}=0
and thus
u\in\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R}).

Proposition 3.35
.

For any fixed
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, we have that as
u\in U_{\rm id}
and
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
, i.e.,
z_{1}\rightarrow 0+,z_{k}\rightarrow+\infty
,

\displaystyle S_{\pm}(u,A)=S_{\pm}\left(u_{\rm cat},\hskip 5.69054ptg(u;-A)\cdot A\cdot g(u;-A)^{-1}+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1})\right),

(118)

Proof.
Suppose we are given the solution
\Phi(u;\Phi_{0})
of the isomonodromy equation (
50
) on
U_{\rm id}
with the boundary value
\Phi_{0}\in{\rm Herm}(n)
. Then it follows from the definition of Stokes matrices at
u_{\rm cat}
that

\displaystyle S_{\pm}(u_{\rm cat},\Phi_{0})=S_{\pm}(u,\Phi(u;\Phi_{0})).

(119)

If we set

A:=\Phi(u;\Phi_{0})=g(u;\Phi_{0})\cdot\Phi_{0}\cdot g(u;\Phi_{0})^{-1}+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1})

for any
u\in U_{\rm id}
close to
u_{\rm cat}
, then following Proposition
3.10

\Phi_{0}=g(u;-A)\cdot A\cdot g(u;-A)^{-1}+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}).

(120)

Therefore, setting
A=\Phi(u;\Phi_{0})
and applying (
120
) to the identity (
119
) leading to (
118
).

Proof of Proposition
1.4

: On the one hand, following Theorem
1.2
we have explicit expression of
S_{\pm}(u_{\rm cat},\Phi_{0})
for any
\Phi_{0}\in{\rm Herm}(n)
. On the other hand, following Proposition
3.35
, we have

\displaystyle S_{\pm}\left(u,~A\right)

\displaystyle=S_{\pm}\left(u_{\rm cat},~g(u;-A)\cdot A\cdot g(u;-A)^{-1}+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1})\right)

\displaystyle=S_{\pm}\left(u_{\rm cat},~g(u;-A)\cdot A\cdot g(u;-A)^{-1}\right)+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}).

(121)

Here the second identity uses the explicit expression of the Stokes matrices at
u_{\rm cat}
.
Therefore, we only need to find the expression of the eignvalues
\lambda^{(k)}_{i}
and minors
\Delta^{1,...,k-1,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}
of
g(u;-A)\cdot A\cdot g(u;-A)^{-1}
in terms of those of
A
. The conjugation action by
g(u;-A)
on
A
is better to be understood in terms of the Thimm action.

First, under the Gelfand-Tsetlin action and angle coordinates, the Thimm action (see Section
3.6.1
) of an element

\theta^{(k)}={\rm diag}(e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta^{(k)}_{1}},...,e^{{\mathrm{i}}\theta^{(k)}_{k}})\in T(k),\text{ for }k=1,....,n-1

on
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
is described by

\displaystyle\lambda^{(i)}_{j}(\theta^{(k)}\bullet A)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{(i)}_{j}(A),\ \ 1\leq j\leq i\leq n,

(122)

\displaystyle\psi^{(i)}_{j}(\theta^{(k)}\bullet A)

\displaystyle=\psi^{(i)}_{j}(A)+\delta_{ki}\theta^{(k)}_{j},\ \ 1\leq j\leq i\leq n-1.

(123)

Second, the conjugation by a diagonal element

d={\rm diag}(d_{1},...,d_{n})\in T(n)\subset U(n)

is described by

\lambda^{(i)}_{j}({\rm Ad}_{d}A)=\lambda^{(i)}_{j}(A),\hskip 14.22636pt\alpha^{(i)}_{j}({\rm Ad}_{d}A)=d_{i}\cdot\alpha^{(i)}_{j}\cdot d_{i+1}^{-1}.

(124)

For any
A
and
u
, let us introduce an element in the product of torus

(u_{2}-u_{1})^{\frac{-\lambda^{(1)}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\times\overrightarrow{\underset{k=2,...,n-1}{\prod}}\left(\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\right)^{\frac{-\lambda^{(k)}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\in T(1)\times\cdots\times T(n-1)

(125)

where
\lambda^{(k)}(A):={\rm diag}(\lambda^{(k)}_{1},...,\lambda^{(k)}_{k})
. Let us introduce the diagonal matrix

D(u;A)={\rm diag}(1,(u_{2}-u_{1})^{-{A_{22}}/{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}},...,(u_{n}-u_{n-1})^{-{A_{nn}}/{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}})\in T(n).

(126)

Then one checks

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}_{g(u;-A)}A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}_{D(u;A)}\left((u_{2}-u_{1})^{\frac{-\lambda^{(1)}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\times\overrightarrow{\underset{k=2,...,n-1}{\prod}}\left(\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\right)^{\frac{-\lambda^{(k)}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\bullet A\right).

Here
\bullet
denotes the Thimm action of the element in (
125
) on
A
. Therefore, by (
124
) and (
123
) we have

\displaystyle\lambda^{(k)}_{i}({\rm Ad}_{g(u;-A)}A)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(A),

\displaystyle a^{(k)}_{i}({\rm Ad}_{g(u;-A)}A)

\displaystyle=a^{(k)}_{i}(A)\cdot(u_{k}-u_{k-1})^{\frac{-A_{kk}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}(u_{k+1}-u_{k})^{\frac{A_{k+1,k+1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\left(\frac{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}\right)^{\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

\displaystyle=a^{(k)}_{i}(A)\cdot(u_{k}-u_{k-1})^{\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(A)-A_{kk}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}(u_{k}-u_{k-1})^{\frac{A_{k+1,k+1}-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}},

where the
(u_{k}-u_{k-1})^{\frac{-A_{kk}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}(u_{k+1}-u_{k})^{\frac{A_{k+1,k+1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}
term in the second identity comes from the conjugation action of the diagonal matrix
D(u;A)
.

In the end, the proposition follows from the real analyticity and the explicit expression of
S_{\pm}(u_{\rm cat},\Phi_{0})
in Theorem
1.2
(provided replacing the Gelfand-Tsetlin coordinates of
\Phi_{0}={\rm Ad}_{g(u;-A)}A
by those of
A
).

One can prove similar results for the other entries of
S_{\pm}(u,A)
. Proposition
1.4
gives the expression of the leading terms of Stokes matrices as
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
from
U_{\rm id}
, in terms of the Gelfand-Tsetlin action and angle coordinates. Furthermore, we see that the leading terms include a fast spin on the corresponding Liouville torus of the Gelfand-Tsetlin integrable systems, which is cancelled out as far as the regularized limit is considered.

3.9
Proof of Theorem
1.5
: regularized limits of Stokes matrices at a caterpillar point

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

A manipulation of Gauss decomposition and monodromy relation (
149
) shows the following equivariant property of Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff map
\nu(u_{\rm cat}):{\rm Herm}(n)\rightarrow{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)
at
u_{\rm cat}

(see Proposition
4.1
for a stronger equivariant property of
\nu(u_{\rm cat})
with respect to the Thimm action),

\displaystyle\nu\left(u_{\rm cat},g(u;-A)\cdot A\cdot g(u;-A)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D(u;A)\cdot\left((u_{2}-u_{1})^{\frac{-\lambda^{(k)}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\times\overrightarrow{\underset{k=2,...,n-1}{\prod}}\left(\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\right)^{\frac{-\lambda^{(k)}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\bullet\nu(u_{\rm cat},A)\right)\cdot D(u;A)^{-1}.

Here we take the notation from (
125
) and (
126
) and in particular
\bullet
denotes the Thimm action. It also follows from Proposition
4.1
that

\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(A)={\rm log}\left(\lambda^{(k)}_{i}\left(\nu(u_{\rm cat},A)\right)\right),\text{ for }1\leq i\leq k\leq n,

here recall that the eigenvalues are ordered. Therefore, we get

\nu\left(u_{\rm cat},g(u;-A)Ag(u;-A)^{-1}\right)=G\left(u;-\nu(u_{\rm cat},A)\right)\cdot\nu(u_{\rm cat},A)\cdot G\left(u;-\nu(u_{\rm cat},A)\right)^{-1},

(127)

where for any positive definite Hermitian matrix
B\in{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)
, if
B=B_{+}^{\dagger}B_{+}
for an upper triangular matrix, then

G(u;B):=\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=1,...,n-1}}(z_{k})^{\frac{{\rm log}(\delta_{k}(B_{-})\delta_{k}(B_{+}))}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}.

Note that

\displaystyle S_{\pm}\left(u,~A\right)=

\displaystyle S_{\pm}\left(u_{\rm cat},~g(u;-A)\cdot A\cdot g(u;-A)^{-1}+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{\pm}\left(u_{\rm cat},~g(u;-A)\cdot A\cdot g(u;-A)^{-1}\right)+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}).

Therefore, the identities (
121
) and (
127
) give

\displaystyle\nu(u,A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{-}(u,~A)S_{+}(u,~A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{-}\left(u_{\rm cat},~g(u;-A)\cdot A\cdot g(u;-A)^{-1}\right)S_{+}\left(u_{\rm cat},~g(u;-A)\cdot A\cdot g(u;-A)^{-1}\right)+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu\left(u_{\rm cat},g(u;-A)\cdot A\cdot g(u;-A)^{-1}\right)+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G\left(u;-\nu(u_{\rm cat},A)\right)\cdot\nu(u_{\rm cat},A)\cdot G\left(u;-\nu(u_{\rm cat},A)\right)^{-1}+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}).

Following the definition of
G(u;B)
, the above identity implies

G(u;\nu(u,A))\cdot\nu\left(u,A\right)\cdot G(u;\nu(u,A))^{-1}=\nu(u_{\rm cat},A)+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1}),

which proves the theorem provided writing the Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff maps
\nu(u,A)
,
\nu(u_{\rm cat},A)
and the gauge transform
G(u;\nu(u,A))
by the Stokes matrices via the definitions.

Example 3.36
.

[2 by 2 cases]
Let us consider the rank two case, that is

\frac{dF}{dz}=\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\mathrm{i}}u_{1}&0\\
0&{\mathrm{i}}u_{2}\end{array}\right)-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}z}{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}t_{1}&a\\
\bar{a}&t_{2}\end{array}\right)}\right)\cdot F.\quad

Following [
[
7
]
Proposition 8], the Stokes matrices (with respect to the chosen branch of
{\rm log}(z)
) are

S_{-}(u,A)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{\frac{t_{1}}{2}}&0\\
\frac{\bar{a}\cdot e^{\frac{t_{1}-t_{2}}{4}}({u_{2}-u_{1}})^{\frac{t_{1}-t_{2}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\lambda_{1}-t_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})\Gamma(1-\frac{\lambda_{2}-t_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}&e^{\frac{t_{2}}{2}}\end{array}\right),\ \ S_{+}(u,A)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{\frac{t_{1}}{2}}&\frac{a\cdot e^{\frac{t_{1}+t_{2}}{4}}({u_{2}-u_{1}})^{\frac{t_{2}-t_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}}{\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda_{1}-t_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda_{2}+t_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\\
0&e^{\frac{t_{2}}{2}}\end{array}\right).

Here
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
are eigenvalues of
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}t_{1}&a\\
\bar{a}&t_{2}\end{array}\right)
. By the definition of
\delta_{k}
given in (
3.2
), we have that
\delta_{1}(S_{\pm}(u,A))
is the diagonal part of
S_{\pm}(u,A)
. Then
{\frac{{\rm log}(\delta_{1}(S_{-})\delta_{1}(S_{+}))}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}={{\rm diag}(\frac{t_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}},\frac{t_{2}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}
, and
z_{1}=u_{2}-u_{1}
, thus

\displaystyle{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(u_{2}-u_{1})^{\frac{t_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}&0\\
0&(u_{2}-u_{1})^{\frac{t_{2}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\end{array}\right)}S_{-}(u,A)S_{+}(u,A)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(u_{2}-u_{1})^{\frac{-t_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}&0\\
0&(u_{2}-u_{1})^{\frac{-t_{2}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\end{array}\right)

approaches to
S_{-}(u_{\rm cat},A)S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)
, as
u_{2}-u_{1}\rightarrow 0
, where

S_{-}(u_{\rm cat},A)^{\dagger}=S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{\frac{t_{1}}{2}}&\frac{a\cdot e^{\frac{t_{1}+t_{2}}{4}}}{\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda_{1}-t_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})\Gamma(1+\frac{\lambda_{2}-t_{1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\\
0&e^{\frac{t_{2}}{2}}\end{array}\right).

Actually, as we have seen in the above computation or in the proof of Proposition (
3.4
), the expression (
3.36
) equals to
S_{-}(u_{\rm cat},A)S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)
(not necessary to take the limit as
z_{1}=u_{2}-u_{1}\rightarrow 0
).

3.10
The explicit expression of the other entries of Stokes matrices

Following Theorem
1.13
(whose proof is given in Section
4.1
), the Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff map
\nu(u_{\rm cat})
at the caterpillar point is a Poisson map. That is, in terms of the r-matrix formulation of the Poisson brackets on the dual Poisson Lie group, Theorem
1.13
can be rewritten via the classical RLL formulation (see
[
2
, Formula (235)]
):

Corollary 3.37
.

The matrix valued function
\nu(u_{\rm cat})=S_{-}(u_{\rm cat},A)S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)\in{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)
satisfies

\{\nu^{1}\otimes\nu^{2}\}=r_{+}\nu^{1}\nu^{2}+\nu^{1}\nu^{2}r_{-}-\nu^{1}r_{+}\nu^{2}-\nu^{2}r_{-}\nu^{1}.

(132)

Here

\displaystyle r_{+}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{ii}\otimes E_{ii}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}E_{ij}\otimes E_{ji}\in\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})\otimes\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{C}^{n}),

\displaystyle r_{-}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E_{ii}\otimes E_{ii}-\sum_{1\leq j<i\leq n}E_{ij}\otimes E_{ji}\in\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})\otimes\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{C}^{n}),

and the tensor notation is used: both sides are
\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})\otimes\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
valued functions on

A\in\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{n}
,
\nu^{1}:=\nu(u_{\rm cat})\otimes 1
,
\nu^{2}:=1\otimes\nu(u_{\rm cat})
, and the
ij,kl
coefficient of the matrix
\{\nu^{1},\nu^{2}\}
is
defined as the Poisson bracket
\{\nu_{ij},\nu_{kl}\}
of the functions on the
canonical linear Poisson space
\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{n}^{\ast}\cong\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{n}
.

The other entries of
S_{\pm}(u_{\rm cat},A)
are uniquely determined by the sub-diagonal ones through the identity (
132
), and one can actually find their explicit expressions, as well as their leading terms via the Gelfand-Tsetlin systems like in Section
3.8
. Since the explicit expressions of the other entries are not used in this paper, we omit the computation.

Remark 3.38
.

The quantum analog of Corollary
3.37
, or equivalently Theorem
1.14
, is just Theorem
1.16
. In particular, the quantum Stokes matrices of quantum confluent hypergeometric equation (
17
) satisfy the quantum RLL relation in the Faddeev-Reshetikhin-Takhtajan
[
67
]
realization of quantum groups, and then Corollary and Theorem
1.14
follows by taking a semiclassical limit
[
73
]
.

3.11
Regularized limits of Stokes matrices with degenerate irregular terms

Although the results in the previous sections are obtained under the assumption that
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, their generalization to the degenerate case, i.e.,
u\in\mathfrak{h}(\mathbb{R})\setminus\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, is direct and is given in this subsection.

Given any fixed partition
\underline{d}
of
n
, i.e., a set of integers
\{d_{j}\}_{j=1,...,m}
such that
n=d_{1}+\cdots+d_{m}
, let us consider the subspace of
\mathfrak{h}(\mathbb{R})

U^{\underline{d}}_{\rm id}=\{u={\rm diag}(\underbrace{u_{1},...,u_{1}}_{d_{1}},\underbrace{u_{2},...,u_{2}}_{d_{2}},....,\underbrace{u_{m},...,u_{m}}_{d_{m}})\in\mathfrak{h}(\mathbb{R})~|~u_{i}<u_{j}\ \ \text{if}\ \ i<j\}.

Any
n\times n
matrix
A
can be seen as a block matrix
A=(A_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq m}
according to the tuple
\underline{d}
, where each
A_{ij}
denotes the
d_{i}\times d_{j}
block/submatrix formed by the corresponding
d_{i}
rows and
d_{j}
columns of
A
. Then for each
k=0,...,m-1
, we denote by
\delta_{k}^{\underline{d}}(A)
the blocked matrix

\delta_{k}^{\underline{d}}(A)_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}A_{ij},&if\ \ 1\leq i,j\leq k,\ \text{or}\ i=j\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

(133)

Remark 3.39
.

The identity (
133
) depends on the partition
d_{1},...,d_{m}
, which generalizes the definition of
\delta_{k}(A)
given in (
3.2
) corresponding to the case
d_{1}=\cdots=d_{n}=1
. Although this more general notation can lead to confusion with (
3.2
), the context typically eliminates any ambiguity.

Accordingly, we consider the
n\times n
system of partial differential equations for a function
F(z,u)\in GL(n)
of
(z,u)\in\mathbb{C}\times U^{\underline{d}}_{\rm id}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial z}

\displaystyle=\left({\mathrm{i}}u-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\Phi(u)}{z}\right)\cdot F,

(134)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial u_{k}}

\displaystyle=\left({\mathrm{i}}E(\underline{d})_{k}z-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}{\rm ad}^{-1}_{u}{\rm ad}_{E(\underline{d})_{k}}\Phi(u)\right)\cdot F,\ \text{for all}\ k=1,...,m,

(135)

where the residue
\Phi(u):U^{\underline{d}}_{\rm id}\rightarrow{\rm Herm}(n)
is a solution of the blocked isomonodromy deformation equation

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial u_{k}}=\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}[\Phi,{\rm ad}^{-1}_{u}{\rm ad}_{E(\underline{d})_{k}}\Phi],\ \text{for all}\ k=1,...,m.

(136)

Here

E(\underline{d})_{k}={\rm diag}(0,...,0,{\rm Id}_{d_{k}},0,...,0)

(137)

is the block diagonal matrix whose
k-
th block diagonal entry is the rank
d_{k}
identity matrix
{\rm Id}_{d_{k}}
. Note that
{\rm ad}_{E(\underline{d})_{k}}\Phi
takes values in the space
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}^{od}(\underline{d})
of block off diagonal matrices with respect to the tuple
(\underline{d})
, and that
{\rm ad}_{u}

is invertible when restricted to the subspace
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}^{od}(\underline{d})\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
. Note that (
136
) is the compatibility condition of the systems (
134
) and (
135
).

For any fixed
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, since
\Phi(u)/2\pi{\mathrm{i}}
is skew-Hermitian matrix, the ordinary differential equation (
1
) is nonresonant, and thus has a unique formal solution
\hat{F}
around

z=\infty
. Similar to Theorem
2.3
, the Borel-Laplace transform of the unique formal solution
\hat{F}(z)
produces actual fundamental solutions
F_{+}(z),F_{-}(z)
of equation (
1
) with prescribed asymptotics within the sectors
S(-\pi,\pi)
and
S(-2\pi,0)
respectively. Then the transition matrices between the two actual solutions are a pair of Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,\Phi(u))\in{\rm GL}(n)
. Similar to Section
2.2
, one also defines the connection matrix
C(u,\Phi(u))
of equation (
1
).

Furthermore, for any solution
\Phi(u)
of the equation (
136
), the canonical solutions
F_{\pm}(z;u,A)
of (
1
) automatically satisfy the equation (
2
). Thus the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(\Phi(u),u)
of (
134
)
are locally constants (independent of
u\in U^{\underline{d}}_{\rm id}
).

Now in a similar way, we can prove results analog to Proposition
3.4
, and an analog of Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 3.40
.

For any solution
\Phi(u)
of the equation (
136
) on
U^{\underline{d}}_{\rm id}
, there exists a unique constant
\Phi_{0}\in{\rm Herm}(n)
such that as the real numbers
\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\rightarrow+\infty
for all
k=2,...,m-1
and
u_{2}-u_{1}\rightarrow 0
,

\Phi(u)={\rm Ad}{\left((u_{2}-u_{1})^{\frac{\delta^{\underline{d}}_{1}(\Phi_{0})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\overrightarrow{\underset{k=2,...,m-1}{\prod}}\left(\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\right)^{\frac{\delta^{\underline{d}}_{k}(\Phi_{0})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)}\Phi_{0}+\sum_{k=2}^{m-1}\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\right)^{-1}\right).

Furthermore, given any
\Phi_{0}\in{\rm Herm}(n)
there exists a unique real analytic solution
\Phi(u)
of (
3
) with the prescribed asymptotics (
4
).

Similarly, we can prove a result analog to Theorem
3.17
for the systems (
134
) and (
135
). Then the same argument as in Section
3.9
leads to the following analog of Theorem
1.5
for the degenerate
u
case.

Theorem 3.41
.

For any fixed
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, the limit of the matrix valued function of
u\in U^{\underline{d}}_{\rm id}

{\rm Ad}{\left((\frac{1}{u_{2}-u_{1}})^{\frac{{\rm log}(\delta^{\underline{d}}_{1}(S_{-})\delta^{\underline{d}}_{1}(S_{+}))}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot\overrightarrow{\underset{k=2,...,m-1}{\prod}}(\frac{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}{u_{k+1}-u_{k}})^{\frac{{\rm log}(\delta^{\underline{d}}_{k}(S_{-})\delta^{\underline{d}}_{k}(S_{+}))}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)}\left(S_{-}(u,A)S_{+}(u,A)\right),

(138)

as
\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\rightarrow+\infty
for all
k=2,...,m-1
and
u_{2}-u_{1}\rightarrow 0
, equals to

\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=1,...,m}}C\left(E(\underline{d})_{k},\delta^{\underline{d}}_{k}(A)\right)\right)\cdot e^{A}\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=1,...,m}}C\left(E(\underline{d})_{k},\delta^{\underline{d}}_{k}(A)\right)\right)^{-1}.

(139)

Here each
C\left(E(\underline{d})_{k},\delta^{\underline{d}}_{k}(A)\right)
denotes the connection matrix of the system

\frac{dF}{dz}=\left({\mathrm{i}}E(\underline{d})_{k}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\delta^{\underline{d}}_{k}(A)}{z}\right)F,

(140)

where recall
\delta^{\underline{d}}_{k}(A)
and
E(\underline{d})_{k}
are defined in (
133
) and (
137
).

This theorem will be used to derive the expression of quantum Stokes matrices at a caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
, see Section
6.2
.

4
Applications in Poisson geometry

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.13
, and give a new proof of Theorem
1.14
. Section
4.1
shows that the Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff maps at caterpillar points intertwine Gelfand-Tsetlin systems and their multiplicative analogs. Then Section
4.2
shows the Poisson geometric nature of taking closure of Stokes matrices, and provides a new proof to Theorem
1.14
.

4.1
Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff map at the caterpillar point is compatible with Gelfand-Tsetlin

Recall that in Section
3.6.1
, we have introduced the Gelfand-Tsetlin maps and Thimm torus actions. In this subsection, we first introduce their multiplicative analogs
[
27
]
on
{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)
, and then prove that the map
\nu(u_{\rm cat})
is compatible with them.

Logarithmic Gelfand-Tsetlin maps.

Let
{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)\subset{\rm Herm}(n)
denote the subset of positive definite
Hermitian matrices, and define a logarithmic Gelfand-Tsetlin map

\mu\colon{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)\to\mathbb{R}^{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}},

(141)

taking
A
to the collection of numbers

\mu^{(k)}_{i}(A)=\log(\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(A))
. Here recall that
\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(A)^{\prime}s
are the ordered set of
eigenvalues of the upper left
k
-th submatrix
A^{(k)}
of
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
. Then
\mu
is a continuous
map from
{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)
onto the Gelfand-Tsetlin cone
\mathcal{C}(n)
.

Thimm torus actions.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{0}(n)\subset\mathcal{C}(n)
denote the subset where all of the eigenvalue inequalities
(
95
) are strict. Let
{\rm Herm}_{0}^{+}(n)
denote the intersection of
{\rm Herm}_{0}(n)
and
{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)
, i.e.,
{\rm Herm}_{0}^{+}(n)=\mu^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{0}(n))
. Then the actions of Thimm torus on

{\rm Herm}_{0}(n)
defined in (
96
) restrict to a torus action on
{\rm Herm}_{0}^{+}(n)
. The action preserves the logarithmic Gelfand-Tsetlin map
\mu
.

Recall from Definition
3.32
that the Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff map at
u_{\rm cat}
is

\nu(u_{\rm cat})\colon{\rm Herm}(n)\rightarrow{\rm Herm}^{+}(n);\ A\mapsto S_{-}(u_{\rm cat},A)S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A).

Proposition 4.1
.

The Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff map

\nu(u_{\rm cat}):{\rm Herm}(n)\rightarrow{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)

is a diffeomorphism compatible with the Gelfand-Tsetlin systems. That is

(a).

\nu(u_{\rm cat})
intertwines the
Gelfand-Tsetlin maps:
\mu\circ\nu(u_{\rm cat})=\lambda
;

(b).

\nu(u_{\rm cat})
intertwines the Thimm’s torus actions on
{\rm Herm}_{0}(n)
and
{\rm Herm}_{0}^{+}(n)
.

The proof of the proposition relies on the following linear algebra fact.

Lemma 4.2
.

For each
0<k\leq n
, let
\Psi^{(k)}:{\rm Herm}(k)\rightarrow{\rm U}(k)
be a smooth map satisfying the conditions

(1).

\Psi^{(k)}
is a
{\rm U}(k-1)
-equivariant map, i.e.,
\Psi^{(k)}(gAg^{-1})={\rm Ad}_{g}\Psi^{(k)}(A)
, for any
g\in{\rm U}(k-1)\subset U(k)
;

(2).

for any
A\in{\rm Herm}(k)
, there is a block decomposition of
\Psi^{(k)}(A)e^{A}\Psi^{(k)}(A)^{-1}
taking the form

\Psi^{(k)}(A)e^{A}\Psi^{(k)}(A)^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\rm Id_{k-1}}&0\\
{B^{(k)}}^{\dagger}&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{A^{(k-1)}}&0\\
0&\star\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\rm Id_{k-1}}&B^{(k)}\\
0&1\end{array}\right),

where
B^{(k)}
is a column with
k-1
elements, and
{B_{+}^{(k)}}^{\dagger}
the conjugate transpose.

Let us extend
\Psi^{(k)}:{\rm Herm}(k)\rightarrow{\rm U}(k)
as a map from
{\rm Herm}(n)
to
{\rm U}(n)
, using the projection of
{\rm Herm}(n)
onto
{\rm Herm}(k)
and the natural inclusion
{\rm U}(k)\subset{\rm U}(n)
.
Let
\Psi:=\Psi^{(1)}\cdot\cdot\cdot\Psi^{(n)}
be the map from
{\rm Herm}(n)
to
{\rm U}(n)
given by the pointwise multiplication, then the map

\Gamma_{\Psi}:={\rm Ad}_{\Psi}\circ{\rm exp}\colon{\rm Herm}(n)\to{\rm Herm}^{+}(n);\ A\mapsto\Psi(A)e^{A}\Psi(A)^{-1}

is a diffeomorphism compatible with the Gelfand-Tsetlin systems.

Proof.
We will prove this theorem inductively on
n
. When
n=1
, the result is obvious.
For the inductive step
n>1
, we first assume that the map
\Psi_{n-1}:=\Psi^{(1)}\cdot\cdot\cdot\Psi^{(n-1)}\colon{\rm Herm}(n-1)\rightarrow{\rm Herm}^{+}(n-1)
is such that
{\rm Ad}_{\Psi_{n-1}}\circ{\rm exp}
satisfies the conditions
(a)
and
(b)
(provided replacing
\nu(u_{\rm cat})
by
\Gamma_{\Psi}
).

Now we try to prove that the map
{\rm Ad}_{\Psi_{n-1}\Psi^{(n)}}\circ{\rm exp}
also satisfy
(a)
and
(b)
. First, using the identity in the assumption
(2)

\Psi^{(n)}(A)e^{A}\Psi^{(n)}(A)^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\rm Id}_{n-1}&0\\
{B^{(n)}}^{\dagger}&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{A^{(n-1)}}&0\\
0&\star\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\rm Id}_{n-1}&B^{(n)}\\
0&1\end{array}\right),

we have

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}_{\Psi_{n-1}(A)\Psi^{(n)}(A)}e^{A}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Psi_{n-1}e^{A^{(n-1)}}\Psi_{n-1}^{-1}&\Psi_{n-1}e^{A^{(n-1)}}B^{(n)}\\
{B^{(n)}}^{\dagger}e^{A^{(n-1)}}\Psi_{n-1}^{-1}&\star\end{array}\right).

Hence by the assumption about the map
{\rm Ad}_{\Psi_{n-1}}\circ{\rm exp}
, we observe that
{\rm Ad}_{\Psi_{n-1}\Psi^{(n)}}\circ{\rm exp}
intertwines the
Gelfand-Tsetlin maps.

For the Thimm’s torus action, by the assumption for
n-1
, the map
{\rm Ad}_{\Psi_{n-1}}\circ{\rm exp}

intertwines the
T(k)\subset U(k)
actions on

{\rm Herm}_{0}(n-1)
and
{\rm Herm}^{+}_{0}(n-1)
for
1\leq k<n-1
. That is

\Psi_{n-1}(t\bullet A)e^{t\bullet A}\Psi_{n-1}(t\bullet A)^{-1}=t\bullet\left(\Psi_{n-1}(A)e^{A}\Psi_{n-1}(A)^{-1}\right).

(142)

Here recall that the
k
-torus

T(k)\subset{\rm U}(k)\subset U(n)
acts on

{\rm Herm}_{0}(n)
as in (
96
).
Furthermore, using the assumption
(1)
on the equivariance of
\Psi^{(n)}
, we have

{\rm Ad}_{\Psi_{n-1}(A)\Psi^{(n)}(A)}e^{A}={\rm Ad}_{\Psi^{(n)}(\Psi_{n-1}(A)A\Psi_{n-1}(A)^{-1})}e^{\Psi_{n-1}(A)A\Psi_{n-1}(A)^{-1}}.

Together with (
142
) and the definition of the torus action, we obtain

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\Psi}(t\bullet A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}_{\Psi_{n-1}(t\bullet A)\Psi^{(n)}(t\bullet A)}e^{t\bullet A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}_{\Psi^{(n)}(t\bullet(\Psi_{n-1}(A)A\Psi_{n-1}(A)^{-1}))}e^{t\bullet(\Psi_{n-1}(A)A\Psi_{n-1}(A)^{-1})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t\bullet\left({\rm Ad}_{\Psi^{(n)}(\Psi_{n-1}(A)A\Psi_{n-1}(A)^{-1})}e^{\Psi_{n-1}(A)A\Psi_{n-1}(A)^{-1}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t\bullet\Gamma_{\Psi}(A),

for any
t\in T(k)
and
1\leq k\leq n
. It finishes the proof.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.
By the definition of
\nu(u_{\rm cat})
, we only need to prove that the connection map

C^{(k)}:{\rm Herm}(k)\rightarrow{\rm U}(k)~;~B\mapsto C(E_{k},B)

of the
k\times k
system

\frac{dF}{dz}=\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{k}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{B}{z}\right)F,

(143)

(or equivalently the system (
91
)) satisfies the assumption
(1)
and
(2)
in Lemma
4.2
. To see that, let
S^{(k)}_{\pm}(B)\in{\rm GL}(k)
denote the two Stokes matrices of (
91
). By Definition
2.9
and the asymptotics of the canonical solutions
F_{\pm}
of (
143
), we have (here to derive the second formula,
the change in choice of
{\rm log}(z)
is accounted for)

\displaystyle F_{+}(z)F_{-}^{-1}(z)=e^{{\mathrm{i}}E_{k}z}z^{-\frac{\delta_{k-1}(B)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}e^{\frac{-\delta_{k-1}(B)}{2}}S^{(k)}_{+}z^{\frac{\delta_{k-1}(B)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}E_{k}z}\sim{\rm Id}_{k},\ \ \text{as}\ z\rightarrow\infty\ \text{ in }{\rm Sect}_{-},

\displaystyle F_{-}(ze^{-2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})F_{+}^{-1}(z)=e^{{\mathrm{i}}E_{k}z}z^{-\frac{\delta_{k-1}(B)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}S^{(k)}_{-}e^{\frac{-\delta_{k-1}(B)}{2}}z^{\frac{\delta_{k-1}(B)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}e^{-{\mathrm{i}}E_{k}z}\sim{\rm Id}_{k},\ \ \text{as}\ z\rightarrow\infty\ \text{ in }{\rm Sect}_{+},

where
\delta_{k-1}(B)
is the projection of
B
to the centralizer of
E_{k}
in
{{\rm Herm}}(k)
. It follows that the Stokes matrices take the form

\displaystyle S^{(k)}_{-}(B)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{\frac{B^{(k-1)}}{2}}&0\\
{b_{-}^{(k)}}&\ast\end{array}\right),\ \ \ S^{(k)}_{+}(B)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{\frac{B^{(k-1)}}{2}}&b^{(k)}\\
0&\ast\end{array}\right),

where
b^{(k)}
is a column vector. Furthermore, the monodromy relation (
49
) gives rise to

C^{(k)}(B)e^{B}C^{(k)}(B)^{-1}=S_{-}^{(k)}(B)S_{+}^{(k)}(B),\hskip 8.53581ptfor\ B\in{\rm Herm}(k).

(149)

It follows from (
4.1
) and (
149
) that the connection map
C^{(k)}:{\rm Herm}(k)\rightarrow{\rm U}(k)
of the system (
143
) satisfies the assumption
(1)
and
(2)
in the beginning of this subsection. Since (
143
) is seen as a subsystem of (
91
), the proof follows from Lemma
4.2
and the definition of
\nu(u_{\rm cat})
.

Remark 4.3
.

Now we see that the chosen minus sign in the coefficients of linear systems (
143
) or (
91
) is to ensure the monodromy relation (
149
), which is further to ensure the compatibility with the chosen Gelfand-Tsetlin chain
\mathfrak{u}(1)\subset\cdots\subset\mathfrak{u}(n-1)\subset\mathfrak{u}(n)
as the upper left corners embeddings.

Remark 4.4
.

There exists a family of integrable systems
\mathcal{F}(u)
on
\mathfrak{u}(n)\cong{\rm Herm}(n)
defined by Mishchenko and Fomenko, parametrized by
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
, such that
\mathcal{F}(u_{\rm cat})
coincides with the Gelfand-Tsetlin system. More generally, we expect that there exists a family of mutiplicative analog
m\mathcal{F}(u)
of
\mathcal{F}(u)
on
U(n)^{*}\cong{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)
such that the Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff map
\nu(u)
intertwines
\mathcal{F}(u)
and
m\mathcal{F}(u)
for the same
u\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof of Theorem
1.13
.
It follows from
[
3
]
that any smooth map from
{\rm Herm}_{0}(n)\subset{\rm Herm}(n)
to
{\rm Herm}_{0}^{+}(n)
, intertwining the Gelfand-Tsetlin systems, uniquely extends to a Poisson diffeomorphism from
{\rm Herm}(n)
to
{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)
. Following Proposition
4.1
, the map
\nu(u_{\rm cat})
intertwines the Gelfand-Tsetlin system on the open dense subset
{\rm Herm}_{0}(n)
. Since
\nu(u_{\rm cat})
is already real analytic map defined on
{\rm Herm}(n)
, it is thus a Poisson diffeomorphism.

4.2
Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff maps are Poisson

Proposition 4.5
.

For any
u\in U_{\rm id}
, the diffeomorphism given in Definition
3.34

\Phi_{u_{\rm cat}}(u):{\rm Herm}(n)\cong{\mathfrak{u}}(n)^{*}\rightarrow{\rm Herm}(n)\cong{\mathfrak{u}}(n)^{*};A\mapsto\Phi(u;A)

is a Poisson isomorphism.

Proof.
On the one hand, due to the Hamiltonian description of the isomonodromy equation, the solution
\Phi(u;A)
can be seen as a Hamiltonian flow. Thus for any fixed
u,u^{\prime}\in U_{\rm id}
, the map

\Phi_{u_{\rm cat}}(u^{\prime})\circ\Phi_{u_{\rm cat}}(u)^{-1}:{\rm Herm}(n)\rightarrow{\rm Herm}(n);\Phi(u;A)\mapsto\Phi(u^{\prime};A)

is Poisson. On the other hand, for any
u\in U_{\rm id}
the map

G(u):{\rm Herm}(n)\rightarrow{\rm Herm}(n);A\mapsto g(u;-A)\cdot A\cdot g(u;-A)^{-1},

corresponding to a time
u
flow of the Gelfand-Tsetlin system, is a Poisson isomorphism. Here
g(u;-A)
is defined in (
117
). It follows that the map

G(u^{\prime})\circ\Phi_{u_{\rm cat}}(u^{\prime})\circ\Phi_{u_{\rm cat}}(u)^{-1}:{\rm Herm}(n)\rightarrow{\rm Herm}(n)

is a Poisson isomorphism.

Now by Theorem
1.1
and Definition
3.11
, we see that

(G(u^{\prime})\circ\Phi_{u_{\rm cat}}(u^{\prime}))(A)=G(u^{\prime})(\Phi(u^{\prime};A))\rightarrow A,\ \ \text{as}\ u^{\prime}\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}.

Therefore, for fixed
u
the Poisson maps
G(u^{\prime})\circ\Phi_{u_{\rm cat}}(u^{\prime})\circ\Phi_{u_{\rm cat}}(u)^{-1}
has the inverse map
\Phi^{-1}_{u_{\rm cat}}(u)
of
\Phi_{u_{\rm cat}}(u)
as a limit when
u^{\prime}\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
. It finishes the proof.

Given the relation
\nu(u_{\rm cat})=\nu(u)\circ\Phi_{u_{\rm cat}}(u)
(see Definition
3.34
), this proposition states that the closure of Stokes matrices preserves the Poisson geometry nature. For example, it follows that the map
\nu(u_{\rm cat})
at the boundary point
u_{\rm cat}
is Poisson.

A new proof of Theorem
1.14
:
we have seen the relation
\nu(u_{\rm cat})=\nu(u)\circ\Phi_{u_{\rm cat}}(u)
(see Definition
3.34
). Following from Theorem
1.13
and Proposition
4.5
, the diffeomorphisms
\nu(u_{\rm cat})
and
\Phi_{u_{\rm cat}}(u)
are both Poisson. Therefore, the Riemann-Hilbert map
\nu(u)
is Poisson.

5
Regularized limit of Stokes matrices in the De Concini-Procesi space

The infinite point
u_{\rm cat}
is actually a special boundary point in the De Concini-Procesi space
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
. In this section, we generalize the result in Section
3
from the special boundary point
u_{\rm cat}
to arbitrary boundary points on
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
. First, in Section
5.1
, we recall the definition of the space
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
. In Sections
5.2
and
5.3
, we recall its stratification and coordinate charts, and introduce a parameterization of a boundary point by planar rooted coloring tree. Then in Section
5.4
, we introduce the boundary value of solutions of the isomonodromy equation at an arbitrary boundary point of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
. Later in Section
5.5
, we introduce the (regularized limits of) connection and Stokes matrices at the boundary point, which depend on some discrete choices. In Section
5.6
, we show that how the connection and Stokes matrices explicitly depend on the discrete choices, and introduce the wall-crossing formula. After these preparations, in Section
5.7
, we generalize the results in Section
3
from the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
to an arbitrary boundary point.

5.1
The De Concini-Procesi space

Let us take simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
with the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{t}
, set of roots
\Pi\subset\mathfrak{t}^{*}
, positive roots
\Pi_{+}
, and simple roots
\{\alpha_{i}\}
.

We let
{\mathcal{G}}
denote the
minimal building set
associated to the set of roots. To define
{\mathcal{G}}
, let
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
denote the set of all non-zero subspaces of
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
which are spanned by a subset of
\Pi
. Let
V\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
. We say that
V=V_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus V_{k}
is a
decomposition
of
V
if
V_{1},\dots,V_{k}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
, and if whenever
\alpha\in\Pi
and
\alpha\in V
, then
\alpha\in V_{i}
for some
i
. From Section 2.1 of
[
20
]
, every element of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
admits a unique decomposition. Then we define
{\mathcal{G}}
as the set of indecomposable elements of
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
. The set
{\mathcal{G}}
has the following description. There is an action of the Weyl group
W
on
\mathfrak{t}
that preserves
\Pi
. Thus, we get actions of
W
on
{\mathcal{G}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
. If
J\subseteq I
is a non-empty, connected subset of the Dynkin diagram
I
of
\mathfrak{g}
, we can form
V_{J}={\rm span}(\alpha_{j}:j\in J)
. Then
V_{J}\in{\mathcal{G}}
. In fact, every
V\in{\mathcal{G}}
is of the form
w(V_{J})
for some
w\in W
and
J
as above.

Let
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}=\{\chi\in\mathfrak{t}:\alpha(\chi)\neq 0,\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Pi\}
. For any
V\in{\mathcal{G}}
, we have a map
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{g}/V^{\perp})
.

Definition 5.1
.

The De Concini-Procesi space
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}\subset\mathfrak{t}\times\prod_{V\in{\mathcal{G}}}\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{t}/V^{\perp})
is the closure of the image of the map
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}\rightarrow\mathfrak{t}\times\prod_{V\in{\mathcal{G}}}\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{t}/V^{\perp})
.

Since the root system
\Pi
is defined over
\mathbb{R}
, the variety is defined over
\mathbb{R}

and so it make sense to consider the real points
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
. The space
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
is called the De Concini-Procesi space of
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, i.e., the space of
n\times n
diagonal matrices
u={\rm diag}(u_{1},...,u_{n})
with distinct real eigenvalues and such that
\sum_{k=1}^{n}u_{k}=0
.

Remark 5.2
.

As explained in the introduction, by definition, the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(u,A)
are invariant under the translation action on
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
. Thus let
\mathbb{R}
act on
\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
by translation, then for any fixed
A
,
S_{\pm}(u,A)
are parameterized by
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{R}
. In this section, we assume
u\in\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
.

5.2
Coordinate charts on
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})

The space
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
has a stratification, with the strata indexed by rooted trees with
n
colored leaves.
Let
RT
be such a tree, then the corresponding
stratum
\mathcal{M}_{RT}
is the product of
\mathcal{M}_{0,d(I)}
over all internal vertices
I
of
RT
with
d(I)
the index
of
I
. In particular,
0
-dimensional strata correspond to binary rooted trees with
n
ordered leaves, while
1
-dimensional strata correspond to almost binary trees (with exactly
one
4
-valent internal vertex). In the following, let us introduce coordinates chart on
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
, and explain more on the stratification.

We denote by
RT(u)
a planar rooted tree
RT
with
n
leaves colored by the components
u_{1},...,u_{n}
of
u
. We say that
RT
is compatible with
\sigma\in S_{n}
if all internal vertices of the tree are in the lower half plane, all leaves are on the horizontal line
y=0
and are colored by
u_{\sigma(1)},...,u_{\sigma(n)}
from left to right.

To any planar binary rooted tree
BRT
compatible with
\sigma
, one can assign a set of
n-1
coordinates
z_{I}
, indexed by internal vertices
I
of
BRT
, in an appropriate neighborhood
U_{BRT_{\sigma}}
of the corresponding 0-dimensional stratum. The coordinate ring of the open
chart
U_{BRT_{\sigma}}\subset\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
is generated by the following set of coordinate functions
z_{I}
on
U_{BRT_{\sigma}}
indexed by inner vertices
I

of the tree
BRT
,

\displaystyle z_{I}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}u_{r(I)}-u_{l(I)},&\text{ if $I$ is the root vertex},\\
\frac{u_{r(I)}-u_{l(I)}}{u_{r(I^{\prime})}-u_{l(I^{\prime})}},&\text{ if $I$ is any other vertex},\end{array}\right.

where
I^{\prime}
is the preceding vertex of
I
in
BRT
, i.e.
I^{\prime}:={\rm max}\{J\in BRT~|~J<I\}
in the partial ordering
<
of the vertices of
BRT_{\sigma}
with the root being the minimal element, and for any vertex
I
,
l(I)\in[1,...,n]
is such that

\sigma(l(I))
is the maximal index of the
u_{i}\textquoteright s
in the left branch at
I
, and analogously,
r(I)\in[1,...,n]
is such that
\sigma(r(I))
the minimal
index of the
u_{i}^{\prime}s
in the right branch at
I
.

Then any evaluation of
z_{I}^{\prime}s
represents a point in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
. In particular, the point
z_{I}=0
for all inner vertices
I
is the corresponding
0
-dimensional stratum (origin point of the chart
U_{BRT_{\sigma}}
). And a point, with
k
of the
n-1
coordinates
z_{I}
are zero, is in the
n-1-k
dimensional stratum.
Actually, the stratum corresponding to a tree
RT
(not necessary binary) lies in the closure of the one corresponding to another
tree
RT^{\prime}
if and only if
RT^{\prime}
is obtained from
RT
by contracting some edges. In the coordinate charts, the stratum
\mathcal{M}_{RT^{\prime}}
corresponding to a rooted tree
RT^{\prime}
in the local
coordinates determined by a binary rooted tree
BRT
can be described as follows.

Proposition 5.3
.

The stratum
\mathcal{M}_{RT^{\prime}}
has a nonempty intersection with the coordinate chart
U_{{BRT_{\sigma}}}
if and only if
RT^{\prime}
is
obtained from
BRT
by contracting some edges. In the latter case,
\mathcal{M}_{RT^{\prime}}
is a subset of
U_{BRT_{\sigma}}
defined as follows:
z_{I}\neq 0
if the (unique) edge of
BRT
which ends at
I
is contracted in
RT^{\prime}
, and
z_{I}=0
otherwise.

5.3
Parameterization of boundary points of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})

From Section
5.2
, we have seen a cover of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
by coordinate charts
U_{BRT_{\sigma}}
associated to planar binary rooted trees. And boundary points of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})\setminus\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
in
U_{BRT_{\sigma}}
are parameterized by coordinates
z_{I}^{\prime}s
with some of them being zero.
In the following, let us introduce another way to parameterize the boundary points, that is particularly easy to work with for our purpose.

Definition 5.4
.

We denote by
RT_{\sigma}(u)
a planar rooted tree
RT
(not necessary binary) with
n
leaves colored by
u
and compatible with
\sigma\in S_{n}
.

u_{3}
u_{5}
u_{6}
u_{2}
u_{1}
u_{4}

Figure 1:
A planar binary rooted tree with
6
leaves colored by
u_{1},...,u_{6}
.

Then
RT_{\sigma}(u)
represents a point in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
as follows. If the planar rooted tree
RT_{\sigma}(u)
can be obtained from a binary tree
BRT
(compatible with
\sigma
) by contracting some edges, the coloring
u=(u_{1},...,u_{n})
of the leaves of
RT_{\sigma}(u)
determines a point
u_{0}
in the chart
U_{BRT_{\sigma}}\subset\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
: in the coordinates
\{z_{I}\}
associated to
BRT
and
\sigma
, the point
u_{0}
has the coordinates

\displaystyle z_{I}

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{r(I)}-u_{l(I)}}{u_{r(I^{\prime})}-u_{l(I^{\prime})}},\ \text{ if $I$ and $I^{\prime}$ are contracted in $RT_{\sigma}(u)$},

(152)

\displaystyle z_{I}

\displaystyle=0,\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{otherwise}.

(153)

where
I^{\prime}
is the preceding vertex of
I
in the binary tree
BRT
. We call
RT_{\sigma}(u)
a representative of
u_{0}
.

For any
\sigma\in S_{n}
, we take the connected component
U_{\sigma}
of
\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, and denote by
\overline{U_{\sigma}}
its closure in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
. Note that
\overline{U_{\sigma}}
is consisting of the points which can be represented by certain planar rooted tree
RT_{\sigma}(u)
compatible with
\sigma
.
From the expression (
152
), we see that on the one hand, for different coloring
u
,
RT_{\sigma}(u)
can represent a same point. In particular, if we have a binary tree
RT_{\sigma}
, then for all
u\in U_{\sigma}
,
RT_{\sigma}(u)
represents a same point. On the other hand, if the ordering
\sigma
is obtained from
\sigma^{\prime}
by reversing the order
of the descendants of any internal vertex of
RT_{\sigma}(u)
, then
RT_{\sigma}(u)
and
RT_{\sigma^{\prime}}(u)
represent a same point.

Example 5.5
.

Let us take the following planar binary tree
BRT_{\sigma}
with coloring

u_{\sigma(n)}
u_{\sigma(1)}
u_{\sigma(2)}
u_{\sigma(3)}
\cdots
u_{\sigma(n-1)}

Figure 2:
A planar rooted tree with coloring

Let us denote the vertices of the above tree
BRT_{\sigma}
in the partial ordering by
I^{(1)}>I^{(2)}>\cdots>I^{(n)}
. And let

z_{I^{(1)}},...,z_{I^{(n)}}
be the corresponding coordinates, then (following the definition in
[
69
, Page 16]
) the point
u^{\sigma}_{\rm cat}\in U_{BRT_{\sigma}}
with coordinates
z_{I^{(k)}}=0
for all
k=1,...,n
is called a caterpillar point. For
\sigma={\rm id}\in S_{n}
, we simply denote
u^{\rm id}_{\rm cat}
by
u_{\rm cat}
.

Now associated to a planar labelled rooted tree
RT_{\sigma}(u)
, let us introduce another set of coordinates on the connected component
U_{\sigma}
.

Definition 5.6
.

For any vertex
I\in RT_{\sigma}(u)
with
E(I)
outgoing edges,
we denote its branches by
I_{1},...,I_{E(I)}
(counting from left to right). We define
r_{I}
and
\{t_{I_{j}}\}_{j=1,...,E(I)}
as the following collection of
1+E(I)
variables assigned to the vertex
I
and the
E(I)
branches of
I

•

r_{I}=u_{min(I_{E(I)})}-u_{max(I_{1})}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
;

•

for
I
an internal vertex,

t_{I_{1}}=0,\ t_{I_{E(I)}}=1\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581ptt_{I_{j}}=\frac{u_{min(I_{j})}-u_{max(I_{1})}}{u_{min(I_{E(I)})}-u_{max(I_{1})}},\ \text{for}\ 1<j<E(I),

(154)

here
max(I_{1})\in[1,...,n]
is such that

\sigma(max(I_{1}))
is the maximal index of the
u_{i}\textquoteright s
in the first branch
I_{1}
of
I
, and analogously,
min(I_{j})\in[1,...,n]
is such that
\sigma(min(I_{j}))
the minimal
index of the
u_{i}^{\prime}s
in the
j
-th branch
I_{j}
of
I
;

•

for
I
the root vertex,

t_{I_{1}}=0,\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581ptt_{I_{j}}={u_{r(I_{j})}-u_{l(I_{1})}},\ \text{ for }\ 1<j\leq E(I).

(155)

Note that the collection of variables
\{t_{I_{j}}\}_{j=1,...,E(I)},\{r_{I}\}
, for all vertices
I
of
RT_{\sigma}(u)
, define a new coordinate system on
U_{\sigma}
. And the change of coordinates from the system
\{t_{I_{j}},r_{I}\}
to the coordinate system
\{u_{i}\}
of
U_{\sigma}
is

u_{min(I_{j})}-u_{max(I_{1})}=r_{I}\cdot t_{I_{j}}\text{ for }1\leq j\leq E(I).

(156)

If the planar labelled rooted tree
RT_{\sigma}(u)
with coloring has
k
internal vertex, then it represents a point
u_{0}\in\overline{U_{\sigma}}
in the codimension
k
strata. The numbers of
\{r_{I}\}
and
\{t_{I_{j}}\}
variables are exactly
k
and
n-k-1
.
Actually, by (
152
)-(
153
) and (
154
), the variables
\{t_{I_{j}}\}
for all
I
already specify the point
u_{0}
.

To be more precise, under the coordinate transform (
156
), the points
u(\{t_{I_{j}}\},\{r_{I}\})\in U_{\sigma}
are functions of the variables
\{r_{I}\}
and
\{t_{I_{j}}\}
. Although the components
u_{1},...,u_{n}
of
u
depend on the variables
\{r_{I}\}
and
\{t_{I_{j}}\}
, while for fixed
\{t_{I_{j}}\}
, by (
152
)-(
153
) and (
154
), the tree
RT_{\sigma}(u)
with coloring
u(\{t_{I_{j}}\},\{r_{I}\})
represents the same point
u_{0}
for all positive real numbers
\{r_{I}\}
.
It is useful to think of
u_{0}
as the limit of the point
u(\{t_{I_{j}}\},\{r_{I}\})\in U_{\sigma}
as
{r_{I}}/{r_{I^{\prime}}}\rightarrow 0+
for all
I
and its preceding vertex
I^{\prime}
in
RT_{\sigma}(u)
. And the tree
RT_{\sigma}(u)
with coloring
u
encodes the way to take limit of the components
u_{1},...,u_{n}
of
u\in U_{\sigma}
.

Example 5.7
.

Let us take the following binary tree with coloring, that represents the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
. Denote its vertices in the partial ordering by
I^{(1)}>I^{(2)}>\cdots>I^{(n)}
. Its internal vertex
I^{(k)}
for
k=1,...,n-1
has two branches
I^{(k)}_{1},I^{(k)}_{2}
. By Definition
5.6
the numbers associated to the two branches are just
t_{I^{(k)}_{1}}=0
and
t_{I^{(k)}_{2}}=1
, and the number
r_{I^{(k)}}
associated to the vertex
I^{(k)}
is
u_{k+1}-u_{k}
. And the point
u_{\rm cat}
is then the limit
\frac{r_{I^{(k-1)}}}{r_{I^{(k)}}}=\frac{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}\rightarrow 0+
for all
k
.

u_{n}

u_{3}
\cdots
u_{n-1}

.

Let us consider planar tree with coloring in the following figure. Denote its three internal vertices in the partial ordering by

) the limit

\frac{r_{I^{(l)}}}{r_{I}}=\frac{u_{k}-u_{1}}{u_{n}-u_{1}}\rightarrow 0+,\ \ \frac{r_{I^{(r)}}}{r_{I}}=\frac{u_{n}-u_{k+1}}{u_{n}-u_{1}}\rightarrow 0+,\ \ r_{I}=u_{n}-u_{1}\rightarrow 0+,

with the given fixed ratios
\frac{u_{i}-u_{1}}{u_{k}-u_{1}}
and

for

and

.

5.4
Boundary value of solutions of the isomonodromy equation at an arbitrary boundary point

Definition 5.9
.

Suppose
u_{0}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})\setminus{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
is a boundary point represented by a planar rooted colored tree
RT_{\sigma}(u)
. For any
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
and any internal vertex
I
of
RT_{\sigma}(u)
, we denote by
\delta_{I}(A)\in{\rm Herm}(n)
the matrix whose
(i,j)
entry is

\delta_{I}(A)_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}A_{ij},&\text{if }i,j\in Branch(I),\ \text{or}\ i=j\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{array}\right.

(157)

where
Branch(I)
is the subset of
\{1,...,n\}
formed by all index of the coloring
u_{k}^{\prime}s
of leaves at
I
.

Then the following theorem describes the asymptotics of solutions of the isomonodromy equations as
u\rightarrow u_{0}
, that is an analog of Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 5.10
.

For any point
u_{0}
represented by a planar rooted tree
RT_{\sigma}(u)
and any solution
\Phi(u)
of the isomonodromy equation (
50
) on
U_{\sigma}
, there exists a constant
A(u_{0})\in{\rm Herm}(n)
such that as
r_{I}/r_{I^{\prime}}\rightarrow 0+
for all
I
and its preceding vertex
I^{\prime}
in
RT_{\sigma}(u)
,

\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{I\in RT_{\sigma}(u)}}\left(\frac{r_{I}}{r_{I^{\prime}}}\right)^{\frac{\delta_{I}(\Phi(u))}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)\cdot\Phi(u)\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{I\in RT_{\sigma}(u)}}\left(\frac{r_{I}}{r_{I^{\prime}}}\right)^{\frac{\delta_{I}(\Phi(u))}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)^{-1}\rightarrow A(u_{0}),

(158)

where the product
\overrightarrow{\prod}
is taken over all internal vertices
I
of
RT_{\sigma}(u)
, and is taken with
I
to the right of
{J}
if
I<J
in the partial order. Here
\Phi(u)^{I}
is defined as (
157
) associated to the vertex
I
. Furthermore, given any
A(u_{0})\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, there exists a unique real analytic solution
\Phi(u)
of (
3
) on
U_{\sigma}
such that the limit (
158
) holds.

Definition 5.11
.

We call
\mathcal{A}(u_{0})
the boundary value of
\Phi(u)
at
u_{0}\in\overline{U_{\sigma}}
, and denote by
\Phi^{\sigma}(u;A(u_{0}))
the solution on
U_{\sigma}
with the given boundary value
A(u_{0})
at
u_{0}
.

In the following, let us study how to express the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}\left((u,\Phi^{\sigma}(u;\Phi(u_{0}))\right)
via the boundary value
A(u_{0})
.

5.5
Connection matrices and Stokes matrices parameterized by
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})\setminus\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})

Suppose
u_{0}\in\overline{U_{\sigma}}
is a boundary point represented by a planar rooted colored tree
RT_{\sigma}(u)
. Recall that for any vertex
I\in T_{\sigma}(u)
with
E(I)
outgoing edges, we have assigned a collection of numbers
\{t_{I_{j}}\}_{j=1,...,E(I)}
by (
154
) and (
155
). Then with respect to the permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
and associated to each
I
, let us introduce an
n\times n
diagonal matrix
t_{\sigma}^{I}={\rm diag}((t_{\sigma}^{I})_{1},...,(t_{\sigma}^{I})_{n})
with

\displaystyle(t_{\sigma}^{I})_{k}

\displaystyle=t_{I_{j}},\ \ \ \text{if the coloring}\ u_{\sigma(k)}\text{ is in the $j$-th branch $I_{j}$ of $I$}\ \ \text{for certain}\ 1\leq j\leq E(I),

(159)

\displaystyle(t_{\sigma}^{I})_{k}

\displaystyle=0,\ \ \ \ \text{otherwise},

(160)

Besides, for any
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
we define
\delta_{I}(A)\in{\rm Herm}(n)
as in (
157
).

Now associated to
RT_{\sigma}(u)
and any
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, there is a finite set of
n\times n
linear systems labelled by the vertices of
RT_{\sigma}(u)
: for any vertex
I\in RT_{\sigma}(u)
, the linear system is

\displaystyle\frac{dF}{dz}=\left({\mathrm{i}}t_{\sigma}^{I}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\delta_{I}(A)}{z}\right)F.

(161)

The system has only two anti-Stokes directions, the two halves of the imaginary axis. We will choose the right half plane
{\rm Sect}_{+}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}~|~{\rm Re}(z)>0\}
as the initial Stokes sector, and take the branch of
{\rm log}(z)
which is real on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Let us denote by
C(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))
and
S_{\pm}(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))
the associated connection and Stokes matrices.
Let
U(n)^{I}\subset U(n)
be the stabilizer subgroup of
t_{\sigma}^{I}\subset{\rm Herm}(n)
under the conjugation action of
U(n)
on
{\rm Herm}(n)
. Then the connection map

C(t_{\sigma}^{I}):{\rm Herm}(n)\rightarrow U(n);~A\mapsto C(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))

is equivariant with respect to the group
U(n)^{I}
, and (similar to the
u_{\rm cat}
case given in (
4.1
)) the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))
are blocked according to the index set of the coloring
u_{i}^{\prime}s
in the branches
I_{1},...,I_{E(I)}
at the vertex
I
. Furthermore, we have the monodromy relation

\displaystyle C(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))e^{\delta_{I}(A)}C(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))^{-1}=S_{-}(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))S_{+}(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A)).

(162)

Definition 5.12
.

For any
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, the
connection matrix

C^{\sigma}(u_{0},A)
at a boundary point
u_{0}\in\overline{U_{\sigma}}
(with respect to the connected component
U_{\sigma}
) is

\displaystyle C^{\sigma}(u_{0},A):=\overrightarrow{\prod_{I\in RT_{\sigma}(u)}}C(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A)),

(163)

where
RT_{\sigma}(u)
is any representative of
u_{0}
, the product is taken over all vertices
I
of
RT_{\sigma}(u)
, and is taken with
C(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))
to the right of
C(t_{\sigma}^{I^{\prime}},\delta_{I^{\prime}}(A))
if
I^{\prime}<I
in the partial ordering.

Let us check the definition is independent of the different choices of the representatives
RT_{\sigma}(u)
of
u_{0}
. By comparing identities (
152
) and (
153
) with (
154
), we see that the irregular term
t_{\sigma}^{I}
in the equation (
161
) only depends on the variables
\{t_{I_{j}}\}
. Since the
\{t_{I_{j}}\}
variables for all
I
specify the point
u_{0}
, the right hand side of (
163
) only depends on the point
u_{0}
and the chosen compatible permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
. That is the right hand side of (
163
)
is independent of the different choices of the coloring
u
, as long as
RT_{\sigma}(u)
is compatible with
\sigma
and represents the same point
u_{0}
. Recall that by Definition (
5.6
) we think of the boundary point
u_{0}
as the limit of the point
u(\{t_{I_{j}}\},\{r_{I}\})\in U_{\sigma}
as
{r_{I}}/{r_{I^{\prime}}}\rightarrow 0+
for all
I
and its preceding vertex
I^{\prime}
in
RT_{\sigma}(u)
. Then as we will see, the connection matrix
C^{\sigma}(u_{0},A)
is the regularized limit of the (ordinary) connection matrices
C(u(\{t_{I_{j}}\},\{r_{I}\}),A)
as
{r_{I}}/{r_{I^{\prime}}}\rightarrow 0+
.

In particular, if
u_{0}
is in the
0
-dimensional strata, then any coloring
u\in U_{\sigma}
of the binary tree
RT_{\sigma}(u)
will define the same
C^{\sigma}(u_{0},A).
The definition of
C^{\sigma}(u_{0},A)
also depends on the choice of
\sigma
. That is for
u_{0}\in\overline{U_{\sigma}}\cap\overline{U_{\sigma^{\prime}}}
, i.e., there exists different
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in S_{n}
such that
u_{0}
can be represented by certain
RT_{\sigma}(u)
and
RT_{\sigma^{\prime}}(u)
, the connection matrices
C^{\sigma}(u_{0},A)
and
C^{\sigma^{\prime}}(u_{0},A)
will be in general different, see Section
5.6
.

Now since each factor
C(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))
in the product is unitary, we have

Proposition 5.13
.

The connection matrix
C^{\sigma}(u_{0},A)
is unitary.

Given any
\sigma\in S_{n}
, denote by
P_{\sigma}\in{\rm GL}_{n}
the corresponding permutation matrix.

Definition 5.14
.
