For any
u_{0}\in\overline{U_{\sigma}}
and
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, the
Stokes matrices

S^{\sigma}_{\pm}(u_{0},A)
at
u_{0}
with respect to
U_{\sigma}
are the unique triangular matrices, with
{P_{\sigma}}^{-1}e^{\frac{[A]}{2}}P_{\sigma}
as diagonal part, determined by the identity (Gauss decomposition)

\displaystyle{C^{\sigma}(u_{0},A)}e^{A}{C^{\sigma}(u_{0},A)}^{-1}=P_{\sigma}S_{+}^{\sigma}(u_{0},A)S_{-}^{\sigma}(u_{0},A){P_{\sigma}}^{-1}.

(164)

Here
[A]
is the diagonal part of
A
.

Example 5.15
.

If we take
u_{0}=u_{\rm cat}
and the compatible
\sigma={\rm id}\in S_{n}
, then Definitions
5.12
and
5.14
coincide with the definition of connection and Stokes matrices at
u_{\rm cat}
introduced in Section
3.5
.

We stress that just like the connection matrices, the Stokes matrices, at a boundary point
u_{0}
, not only depend on the boundary point itself, but also depend on the discrete choices of the
\sigma\subset S_{n}
such that
u_{0}
is in the closure of the connected component
U_{\sigma}
.

5.6
Different choices of planar embeddings and wall-crossing formula

In this subsection, we study how connection and Stokes matrices associated to planar trees depend on the choices of planar embeddings.

Suppose that a given point
u_{0}
lies at the intersection of
\overline{U_{\sigma}}
and
\overline{U_{\sigma^{\prime}}}
, for different
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in S_{n}
. Then there are two equivalent representatives of
u_{0}
, i.e., planar embeddings
RT_{\sigma}(u)
and
RT_{\sigma^{\prime}}(u)
represent the same rooted tree coloring
u
compatible with
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
respectively. Here equivalence means that
RT_{\sigma^{\prime}}(u)
is obtained from
RT_{\sigma}(u)
by reversing the order of the descendants of some internal vertex of
RT_{\sigma}(u)
. Let us assume that
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
differ by reversing only one cycle. Then
RT_{\sigma^{\prime}}(u)
is given by reversing the order of the descendants of some vertex
I
of
RT_{\sigma}(u)
.

Assume that
C(t_{\sigma}^{J},A^{J})
and
C(t_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{J},A^{J})
are the connection matrices associated to the vertex
J
of
RT_{\sigma}(u)
and
RT_{\sigma^{\prime}}(u)
respectively. Then by the assumption and the definition of the connection matrices, we have

\displaystyle C(t_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{J},A^{J})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}C(t_{\sigma}^{J},A^{J}),&if\ J\neq I,\\
C_{-}(t_{\sigma}^{J},A^{J}),&J=I,\end{array}\right.

where
C_{-}(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))
is the connection matrix of the system (
161
) but with respect to the Stokes sector
{\rm Sect}_{-}
. To be more precise, similar to Definition
2.12
, the connection
matrix
C_{-}(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))
of the system (
28
) (with
(u,A)
replacing by
(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))
is determined by

F_{0}(z)=F_{-}(z)\cdot C_{-}(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))
, where
F_{-}(z)
is the canonical solution on
{\rm Sect}_{-}
. Furthermore, since a negative half cycle (i.e., in clockwise direction) around
0
is a positive half cycle around
\infty
, we get the monodromy relation

C_{-}(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))=\left(S_{+}(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))S_{-}(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}S_{+}(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))C(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A)),

where
S_{\pm}(t_{\sigma}^{I},\delta_{I}(A))
are the Stokes matrices of the system (
161
). In summary, we have the following wall-crossing formula of the connections matrices as
u
crosses the common face of
U_{\sigma}\cup U_{\sigma^{\prime}}
.

Proposition 5.16
.

Given any point
u_{0}
in
\overline{U_{\sigma}}\cap\overline{U_{\sigma^{\prime}}}
, where
\sigma
differs with
\sigma^{\prime}
by reversing some segments, let
RT_{\sigma}(u)
and
RT_{\sigma^{\prime}}(u)
be its two representatives. Then for any
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
,
C^{\sigma^{\prime}}(u_{0},A)
equals to
C^{\sigma}(u_{0},A)=\overrightarrow{\prod}_{J\in RT_{\sigma}(u)}C(t_{\sigma}^{J},A)
after replacing the corresponding factors
C(t_{\sigma}^{J},A)
by
C_{-}(t_{\sigma}^{J},A)
in the product.

Following Definition
5.14
, we also get a wall-crossing formula of the Stokes matrices as
u
crosses the common face of
U_{\sigma}\cup U_{\sigma^{\prime}}
.

Example 5.17

(Caterpillar points)
.

For each
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\tau_{i}\in S_{n}
be the permutation reversing the segment
[1,...,i]
. Then associated to
\tau_{i}
, there is a representative
RT_{\tau_{i}}(u)
of the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
as in the following figure. Note that for all
\tau_{i}
,
i=1,...,n
, the trees
RT_{\tau_{i}}(u)
represent the same point.

u_{n}
u_{i}
\cdots
u_{1}
u_{i+1}
\cdots

u_{n-1}

Figure 5:
A caterpillar point with a planar embedding given by
\tau_{i}

For any
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, we denote by
S_{+}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat},A)
the Stokes matrix associated to the planar tree. For
\tau_{1}={\rm id}\in S_{n}
, it is just the Stokes matrix
S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)
. For any other
\tau_{i}
,
S_{+}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat},A)
is the Stokes matrix at the caterpillar point but with respect to a different choice of planar embedding. Thus we can use Proposition
5.16
to express
S_{+}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat},A)
by
S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)
.

Proposition 5.18

(Wall-crossing formula at
u_{\rm cat}
)
.

Under the change of the planar embedding, the associated Stokes matrices at
u_{\rm cat}
change as

\displaystyle S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}S_{i+}&B\\
0&C\end{array}\right)\rightarrow S_{+}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat},A)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}P_{i}S_{i+}^{\dagger}P_{i}^{-1}&P_{i}(S_{i+}S_{i+}^{\dagger})^{-\frac{1}{2}}S_{i+}B\\
0&C\end{array}\right),

where
S_{i+}
are the left-top
i
-th principal submatrices of
S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)
, and
S_{i+}^{\dagger}
is the complex conjugate of
S_{i+}
, and
P_{i}
is the
i\times i
permutation matrix associated to
\tau_{i}
.

Proof.
By Definition
5.14
, the Stokes matrix
S^{\tau_{i}}_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)
associated to
\tau_{i}
, is given by

\displaystyle{C^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat},A)}e^{A}{C^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat},A)}^{-1}=P_{i}S_{-}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat},A)S_{+}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat},A){P_{i}}^{-1},

(171)

where

C^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat},A)=C_{-}(E_{1},\delta_{1}(A))\cdot\cdot\cdot C_{-}(E_{i},\delta_{1}(A_{i}))C(E_{i+1},\delta_{i+1}(A))\cdot\cdot\cdot C^{(n)}.

Here for
1\leq k\leq i
,
C_{-}(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A))
is the connection matrix of

\frac{dF}{dz}=\left(-{\mathrm{i}}E_{k}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\delta_{k}(A)}{z}\right)F,
and recall that

C(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A))
,
S_{\pm}(E_{k},\delta_{k}(A))
are the connection and Stokes matrices of
\frac{dF}{dz}=\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{k}-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\delta_{k}(A)}{z}\right)F.

Lemma 5.19
.

For any
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
, we have

\displaystyle C_{-}(E_{1},\delta_{1}(A))\cdot\cdot\cdot C_{-}(E_{i},\delta_{1}(A_{i}))=(S_{i+}S_{i-})^{-\frac{1}{2}}S_{i+}C(E_{1},\delta_{1}(A))\cdot\cdot\cdot C(E_{i},\delta_{1}(A_{i})).

(172)

Proof.
Consider a rank
i
linear system
\frac{dF(z)}{dz}=\left({\mathrm{i}}u-\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\Phi(u)}{z}\right)\cdot F(z)
. Since a negative half cycle (i.e., in clockwise direction) around
0
is a positive half cycle around
\infty
, we get the monodromy relation

\displaystyle C_{-}(u,\Phi(u))=(S_{+}(u,\Phi(u))S_{-}(u,\Phi(u)))^{-\frac{1}{2}}S_{+}(u,\Phi(u))C(u,\Phi(u)).

If we take
\Phi(u)
the solution of the corresponding rank
i
isomonodromy equation with the boundary value
A
at
u_{\rm cat}
, then the above relation implies the identity (
172
). It finishes the proof of Proposition
5.18
.

The lemma implies

C^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat},A)=(S_{i+}S_{i-})^{-\frac{1}{2}}S_{i+}\cdot C(u_{\rm cat},A).

Then by a direct blocked matrix manipulation, the proposition follows from the defining relations (
93
) and (
171
) of
S_{+}(u_{\rm cat},A)
and
S_{+}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat},A)
, and the relation
S_{i-}(u_{\rm cat},A)=S_{i+}(u_{\rm cat},A)^{\dagger}
.

Remark 5.20
.

Proposition
5.18
can be used to interpret the cactus group actions on the Gelfand-Tsetlin cones
[
8
]
from the perspective of WKB approximation. See Section
7
.

5.7
Regularized limit of Stokes matrices

In this subsection, we generalize the results in Section 3, particularly the Theorem
3.17
, from the caterpillar points to any boundary points.

Theorem 5.21

(The expression of Stokes matrices at the boundary via the isomonodromy deformation)
.

Let us denote by
\Phi(u;A(u_{0}))
the solution of (
50
) with the boundary value
A(u_{0})
at
u_{0}\in\overline{U_{\sigma}}
as in Theorem
5.10
, then we have

\displaystyle S_{\pm}(u,\Phi^{\sigma}(u;A(u_{0}))=S_{\pm}^{\sigma}(u_{0},A(u_{0})).

For
u_{0}=u_{\rm cat}
and
\sigma={\rm id}
(i.e.,
U_{\sigma}=U_{\rm id}
), they recover Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
3.17
. For a general boundary point
u_{0}
, in order to prove them, we can repeat the recursive procedures in Sections
3.2
–
3.5
. The only difference is that now we need to block the system (
6
) in a different way, i.e., study the set of intermediate linear systems determined by
u_{0}
and the associated isomonodromy equations restricted to each variable
r_{I}
(instead of the variable
u_{i}
as in the caterpillar point case). Since the proofs are rather same, the complete proofs will be omitted here.

And the analog of Theorem
1.5
is

Theorem 5.22

(Regularized limits of Stokes matrices at the boundary)
.

For any
A\in{\rm Herm}(n)
and a boundary point
u_{0}\in\overline{U_{\sigma}}
represented by a planar rooted colored tree
RT_{\sigma}(u)
, we have

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{I\in RT_{\sigma}(u)}}\left(\frac{r_{I^{\prime}}}{r_{I}}\right)^{\frac{{\rm log}(\delta_{I}(S_{-})\delta_{I}(S_{+}))}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)(S_{-}(u,A)S_{+}(u,A))\rightarrow S_{-}^{\sigma}(u_{0},A)S_{+}^{\sigma}(u_{0},A),

as
r_{I}/r_{I^{\prime}}\rightarrow 0+
for all vertex
I
and its preceding vertex
I^{\prime}
in
RT_{\sigma}(u)
.
Here
\delta_{I}(S_{-}(u,A))\delta_{I}(S_{+}(u,A))
is a positive definition Hermitian matrix, and
{\rm log}
takes its logarithm.

It can be proved in the same way to the proof of Theorem
1.5
given in Section
3.9
, except that we now need the following equivariance, generalizing the identity
127
at
u_{\rm cat}
,

\displaystyle S_{-}^{\sigma}\left(u_{0},{\rm Ad}\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{I\in RT_{\sigma}(u)}}\left(\frac{r_{I}}{r_{I^{\prime}}}\right)^{\frac{\delta_{I}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)A\right)\cdot S_{+}^{\sigma}\left(u_{0},{\rm Ad}\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{I\in RT_{\sigma}(u)}}\left(\frac{r_{I}}{r_{I^{\prime}}}\right)^{\frac{\delta_{I}(A)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)A\right)

\displaystyle={\rm Ad}\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{I\in RT_{\sigma}(u)}}\left(\frac{r_{I}}{r_{I^{\prime}}}\right)^{\frac{{\rm log}(\delta_{I}(S_{-})\delta_{I}(S_{+}))}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)(S^{\sigma}_{-}(u_{0},A)S^{\sigma}_{+}(u_{0},A)).

The above identity follows from a manipulation of the equivariance of the connection maps
C(t_{\sigma}^{I})
with respect to
U(n)^{I}
for each vertex
I
, and the (blocked Gauss decomposition) monodromy relation (
162
).

Remark 5.23
.

Theorem
5.21
can be understood as a branching rule of the Stokes matrices
S(u,A)
as
u
degenerates to
u_{0}
according to the associated planar tree
T_{\sigma}(u)
. It relates the Stokes matrices at
u\in{\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
to the analytic data of a set of linear systems of lower ranks via the solutions of isomonodromy equation with prescribed asymptotics, as some of components
u_{i}
of
u
collapse (in a comparable speed).

As can be seen in this paper, the application of the explicit expression, of Stokes matrices at a caterpillar point, relies on the fact that various involved structures we are interested in are preserved under the closure of Stokes matrices. For example, as for the Poisson structures, let us introduce

Definition 5.24
.

The Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff map at a boundary point
u_{0}\in\overline{U_{\sigma}}
(with respect to the connected component
U_{\sigma}
) is

\displaystyle\nu^{\sigma}(u_{0}):{\rm Herm}(n)\rightarrow{\rm Herm}^{+}(n);\ A\mapsto P_{\sigma}S_{-}^{\sigma}(u_{0},A){P_{\sigma}}^{-1}e^{[A]}P_{\sigma}S_{+}^{\sigma}(u_{0},A){P_{\sigma}}^{-1}.

(173)

Then, we can similarly generalize the discussion for
u_{\rm cat}
to any other boundary point (with a chosen planar embedding), and then prove that the map
\nu^{\sigma}(u_{0})
is a Poisson isomorphism. In this way, we prove that the Poisson geometric nature of the Stokes matrices, i.e., Theorem
1.14
, is preserved under taking the regularized limits from
\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
to any boundary point in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
. That is

Theorem 5.25
.

The Riemann-Hilbert-Birkhoff map
\nu^{\sigma}(u_{0}):{\rm Herm}(n)\rightarrow{\rm Herm}^{+}(n)
is a Poisson Diffeomorphism.

6
Quantum Stokes matrices and their explicit expression at caterpillar points

This section gives the quantum analog of the results in Section
3
, i.e., the expression of the regularized limits, as well as the leading terms, of quantum Stokes matrices as
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
, in terms of the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis. In Section
6.1
, we introduce the quantum Stokes matrices of the linear system (
6
). In Sections
6.2
, we recall the regularized limits of quantum Stokes matrices at the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
. In Section
6.3
, we introduce the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis and quantum minors. In Sections
6.4
and
6.5
, we obtain the explicit expressions and the leading terms of quantum Stokes matrices as
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
in terms of the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis. In Section
6.6
we interpret the regularized limit of quantum Stokes matrices via the viewpoint of isomonodromy deformation. In the end, in Section
6.7
, we prove that the quantum Stokes matrices at
u_{\rm cat}
give rise to representation of quantum groups.

6.1
Quantum Stokes matrices

In this subsection, we recall the quantum Stokes matrices of the linear system (
17
) associated to a representation
L(\lambda)

\frac{dF_{h}}{dz}=h\left({\mathrm{i}}u+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{T}{z}\right)\cdot F_{h}.

(174)

First, since
h
is a real number, the system is nonresonant and has a unique formal fundamental solution, see
[
76
]
for more details.

Proposition 6.1
.

For any nonzero real number
h
and
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, the ordinary differential equation (
174
) has a unique formal fundamental solution taking the form

\displaystyle\widehat{F_{h}}(z)=\widehat{H_{h}}(z)e^{{h{\mathrm{i}}uz}}z^{h[T]},\ \ \ {\it for}\ \widehat{H_{h}}=1+H_{1}(h)z^{-1}+H_{2}(h)z^{-2}+\cdot\cdot\cdot,

(175)

where each coefficient
H_{m}(h)\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))\otimes{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
, and
[T]
denotes the diagonal part of
T
, i.e.,
\delta T=\sum_{k}e_{kk}\otimes E_{kk}.

Proof.

Plugging (
175
) into the equation (
28
) gives rise to the equation for
\widehat{H_{h}},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h}\frac{d\widehat{H_{h}}}{dz}+\hat{H_{h}}\cdot\Big({\mathrm{i}}u+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{[T]}{z}\Big)=\Big({\mathrm{i}}u+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{T}{z}\Big)\cdot\widehat{H_{h}}.

(176)

Comparing the coefficients of
z^{-m-1}
, we see that
H_{m}
satisfies

\displaystyle[2\pi u,H_{m+1}]=(\frac{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}m}{h}+T)\cdot H_{m}-H_{m}\cdot\delta T.

(177)

Set
\{E_{kl}\}_{1\leq k,l\leq n}
the standard basis of
{\rm End}(\mathbb{C})
. Then

T=\sum_{k,l}e_{kl}\otimes E_{kl},\ \text{ and }u=\sum_{i}1\otimes u_{i}E_{ii}.

Plugging
H_{m}(h)=\sum_{k,l}H_{m,kl}(h)\otimes E_{kl}
, with
H_{m,kl}(h)\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
, into the equation (
177
) gives rise to

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}(u_{k}-u_{l})H_{m+1,kl}(h)\otimes E_{kl}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}\frac{2\pi m}{h}H_{m,kl}(h)\otimes E_{kl}+\sum_{k,l,j}e_{kj}H_{m,jl}(h)\otimes E_{kl}-\sum_{k,l}H_{m,kl}(h)e_{ll}\otimes E_{kl}.

(178)

Here
e_{kl}^{\prime}s
are understood as elements in
{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
via the given representation. That is for
k\neq l

(u_{k}-u_{l})H_{m+1,kl}=\frac{2\pi m}{h}H_{m,kl}(h)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}e_{kj}H_{m,jl}(h)-H_{m,kl}(h)e_{ll}\ \in{\rm End}(L(\lambda)),

(179)

and for
k=l
(replacing
m
by
m+1
in (
178
)),

\displaystyle 0=\sum_{j\neq k}e_{kj}H_{m+1,jk}(h)+\frac{2\pi(m+1)}{h}H_{m+1,kk}(h)+[e_{kk},H_{m+1,kk}(h)]\ \in{\rm End}(L(\lambda)).

(180)

Suppose
H_{m}(h)
is given, let us check that the above recursive relation have a unique solution
H_{m+1}(h)
. First note that, since
u_{k}\neq u_{l}
for
k\neq l
, the identity (
179
) uniquely defines the "off-diangonal" part
H_{m+1,kl}(h)
(
k\neq l
) of
H_{m+1}(h)
from
H_{m}(h)
. Furthermore, since
h
is real, we have
\frac{2\pi(m+1)}{2}{\rm Id}+{\rm ad}_{e_{kk}}
is invertible on
{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
for any integer
m+1
. Thus, the condition (
180
) uniquely defines the "diagonal" part
H_{m+1,kk}(h)
of
H_{m+1}(h)
from the off diagonal part.

Note that under a choice of basis in
L(\lambda)
, the system (
174
) becomes a special case of the equation (
28
) with rank
n\times{\rm dim}(L(\lambda))
. Then one can follow the standard resummation procedure as in Section
2
to study the Stokes phenomenon. In particular, the Borel-Laplace transforms of the formal solutions
\widehat{F_{h}}
given in Proposition
6.1
produce actual solutions with the prescribed asymptotics in the corresponding sectoral regions. And again, let us choose the branch of
{\rm log}(z)
, which is real on the positive real axis, with a cut along
i\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Analog to Theorem
2.3
, we have that

Theorem 6.2
.

[
76
]

For any
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, on
{\rm Sect}_{\pm}
there
is a unique (therefore canonical) holomorphic fundamental solution
F_{h\pm}(z;u,A)\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))\otimes{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
of (
174
) such that
F_{h\pm}\cdot e^{-{\mathrm{i}}huz}z^{\frac{-h[T]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}
can be analytically continued to
S(-\pi,\pi)
and
S(-2\pi,0)
respectively, and

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}F_{h+}(z;u,A)\cdot e^{-{\mathrm{i}}huz}\cdot z^{\frac{-h[T]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1,\ \ \ as\ \ \ z\in{S}(-\pi,\pi),

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}F_{h-}(z;u,A)\cdot e^{-{\mathrm{i}}huz}\cdot z^{\frac{-h[T]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1,\ \ \ as\ \ \ z\in{S}(-2\pi,0),

where
[T]={\rm diag}(E_{11},...,E_{nn})\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))\otimes{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
.

Definition 6.3
.

[
76
]

The
quantum Stokes matrices
of (
174
) (with respect
to
{\rm Sect}_{+}
and the chosen branch of
{\rm log}(z)
) are the elements
S_{h\pm}(u)\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))\otimes{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
determined by

\displaystyle F_{h+}(z)=F_{h-}(z)\cdot e^{\frac{h[T]}{2}}S_{h+}(u),\ \ \ \ \ F_{h-}(ze^{-2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})=F_{h+}(z)\cdot S_{h-}(u)e^{\frac{-h[T]}{2}},

(181)

where the first (resp. second) identity is understood to hold in
{\rm Sect}_{-}

(resp.
{\rm Sect}_{+}
) after
F_{h+}
(resp.
F_{h-}
) has been analytically continued anticlockwise around
z=\infty
.

Let us assume
u\in U_{\rm id}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, then just like the classical case, the asymptotics in Theorem
6.2
ensures that
S_{h+}
is a upper triangular matrix, and
S_{h-}
is lower triangular with entries in
{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
, see e.g.,
[
76
]
.

Remark 6.4
.

Following
[
76
]
, the (formal solution and quantum Stokes matrices of) linear system (
174
) is interpreted as a quantization of the (ones of) linear system (
6
) in the framework of deformation quantization. The study of the quantum Stokes matrices is generalized to arbitrary order pole cases: in
[
79
]
, a quantum analog of meromorphic linear systems of ODEs with pole of order
k
, as well as its quantum Stokes matrices, is introduced. As for
k=2
, it becomes the equation (
174
). The quantum Stokes matrices at pole of order
k
is then interpreted as a quantization of the space of the classical Stokes matrices.

6.2
Quantum Stokes matrices at a caterpillar point

Since (
174
) can be seen as a special form of the general system (
28
), we have

S_{h\pm}(u)=S_{\pm}(hu,-hT),

where the right hand side is understood as the (classical) Stokes matrices of the equation (
28
) with
u
and
A
replaced by
hu
and
-hT
. Here the irregular term
{\mathrm{i}}hu={\rm diag}({\mathrm{i}}hu_{1},...,{\mathrm{i}}hu_{n})\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))\otimes{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
is degenerate, i.e., has repeated eigenvalues.
One can still study the regularized limits of
S_{h\pm}(u)
as the components
u_{i}
of
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
collapse in a comparable speed, and introduce the quantum Stokes matrices for any boundary point in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})\setminus{\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}}(\mathbb{R})
. In particular, the construction in Section
3.11
enables us to define the quantum Stokes matrices at
u_{\rm cat}
as follows.

First, the system (
174
) has rank
n\times{\rm dim}(L(\lambda))
. In terms of the notation in Section
3.11
, let us take the partition
\underline{d}
of
n\times{\rm dim}(L(\lambda))
with
d_{1}=\cdots=d_{n}={\rm dim}(L(\lambda))
, and assume that the irregular term
u
in the Stokes matrices
S_{\pm}(hu,-hT)
of (
174
) lives in
U^{\underline{d}}_{\rm id}
. Following Theorem
3.41
, the matrix
S_{-}(hu,-hT)\cdot S_{+}(hu,-hT)
has a regularized limit as
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
from
u\in U^{\underline{d}}_{\rm id}
.

Definition 6.5
.

The quantum Stokes matrices at
u_{\rm cat}
, with respect to the choice of
U^{\underline{d}}_{\rm id}
, are the upper and lower
n\times n
triangular matrices
S_{h\pm}(u_{\rm cat})
(having the same diagonal part), with entries valued in
{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
, such that (the blocked Gauss decomposition)

S_{h-}(u_{\rm cat})S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})

equals to the regularized limit of the function
S_{-}(hu,-hT)\cdot S_{+}(hu,-hT)
on
U^{\underline{d}}_{\rm id}
, i.e., the limit of

{\rm Ad}{\left((\frac{1}{u_{2}-u_{1}})^{\frac{{\rm log}(\delta^{\underline{d}}_{1}(S_{h-})\delta^{\underline{d}}_{1}(S_{h+}))}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\overrightarrow{\underset{k=2,...,m-1}{\prod}}(\frac{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}{u_{k+1}-u_{k}})^{\frac{{\rm log}(\delta^{\underline{d}}_{k}(S_{h-})\delta^{\underline{d}}_{k}(S_{h+}))}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\right)}\left(S_{h-}(u)S_{h+}(u)\right),

(182)

as
\frac{u_{k+1}-u_{k}}{u_{k}-u_{k-1}}\rightarrow+\infty
for all
k=2,...,m-1
and
u_{2}-u_{1}\rightarrow 0
,
where
\delta^{\underline{d}}_{k}(S_{h\pm}(u))
takes the
{\rm End}(\lambda)
valued entries of the matrices
S_{h\pm}(u)
as in (
133
).

6.3
Quantum minors and Gelfand-Tsetlin basis

To write down the explicit expression of
S_{h\pm}(u_{\rm cat})
, as a non-commutative version of the formula in Theorem
1.2
, let us introduce

Definition 6.6
.

The matrix
T(x)=T-x\operatorname{Id}
is the characteristic matrix of
T
, where
x
is an indeterminate commuting with all generators
e_{ij}
, for
1\leq i,j\leq n
.

Definition 6.7
.

For
1\leq m\leq n
, given two sequences,
a=\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}\}
and
b=\{b_{1},\cdots,b_{m}\}
, with elements in
\{1,2,\cdots,n-1,n\}
, the corresponding quantum
minor of the matrix
T(x)
is defined by

\displaystyle\Delta^{a_{1},...,a_{m}}_{b_{1},...,b_{m}}(T(x)):=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{m}}(-1)^{\sigma}T(x_{1})_{a_{\sigma(1)},b_{1}}\cdots T(x_{m})_{a_{\sigma(m)},b_{m}}\in U({\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})[x],

where
x_{k}:=x+k-1
for
k=1,...,m
, and
(-1)^{\sigma}
means the signature of the permutation
\sigma
in the symmetry group
S_{m}
of
m
elements.

Since
\zeta^{(k)}_{i}
does not commute with all
e_{ab}
, for
1\leq a,b\leq n
. So
\Delta^{a_{1},...,a_{m}}_{b_{1},...,b_{m}}\big(T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\big)
is not well defined. However,

Definition 6.8
.

Suppose that
\Delta^{a_{1},...,a_{m}}_{b_{1},...,b_{m}}\left(T\left(x\right)\right)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}r_{i}x^{i}
with coefficient
r_{i}\in U\left({\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}\right)
. For any element
\zeta
(that may not commute with
e_{ij}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
), we define
\left(\Delta_{L}\right)^{a_{1},...,a_{m}}_{b_{1},...,b_{m}}\big(T(\zeta)\big)
and
\left(\Delta_{R}\right)^{a_{1},...,a_{m}}_{b_{1},...,b_{m}}\big(T(\zeta)\big)
in the following way

\displaystyle\left(\Delta_{L}\right)^{a_{1},...,a_{m}}_{b_{1},...,b_{m}}\big(T(\zeta)\big)=\zeta^{n-1}r_{n-1}+\zeta^{n-2}r_{n-2}+\cdots+\zeta r_{1}+r_{0},

\displaystyle\left(\Delta_{R}\right)^{a_{1},...,a_{m}}_{b_{1},...,b_{m}}\big(T(\zeta)\big)=r_{n-1}\zeta^{n-1}+r_{n-2}\zeta^{n-2}+\cdots+r_{1}\zeta+r_{0}.

For any
1\leq k\leq n
, let

\displaystyle M_{k}(\zeta):=\Delta^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k}\left(T(\zeta)\right)

(183)

be the upper left
k\times k
quantum-minor of
T
. It is known that the subalgebra, generated in
U({\mathfrak{gl}}_{n})
by the coefficients in all
M_{k}(\zeta)
for all
1\leq k\leq n
, is a maximal commutative subalgebra and is called the Gelfand-Tsetlin subalgebra.

Definition 6.9
.

Let
\{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq k\leq n}
denote the roots of
M_{k}(\zeta)=0
for all
k=1,...,n
(in an appropriate splitting extension).

The action of the Gelfand-Tsetlin subalgebra on a highest weight representation has simple spectrum and the corresponding orthonomral eigenbasis is called a Gelfand-Testlin basis. The action of the quantum minors and the roots
\{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq k\leq n}
on the representation can be explicitly expressed under the basis, see Proposition
6.11
. Before giving the explicit action, let us introduce the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis in a more conventional way.

Recall that
\{e_{ij}\}_{i,j=1,...,n}
is the standard basis of the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
. Denote by
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n-1}
the subalgebra spanned by the elements
\{e_{ij}\}_{i,j=1,...,n-1}
. Finite dimensional irreducible representations of
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
are parameterized by the highest weight, i.e.,
n
-tuples of numbers
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{n})
with

\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i+1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},\hskip 14.22636pt,\ \forall\ i=1,...,n-1.

We denote by
L(\lambda)
the corresponding representation. It has a lowest vector
\xi_{0}
such that
E_{ii}\xi=\lambda_{i}\xi
for
i=1,...,n
, and
E_{ij}\xi=0
for
1\leq j<i\leq n
. Then the simple branching rule for the reduction from
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
to
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n-1}
states that the restriction of
L(\lambda)
to the subalgebra
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n-1}

is isomorphic to the
direct sum of pairwise inequivalent irreducible representations

L(\lambda)|_{{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n-1}}\cong\underset{\lambda^{(n-1)}}{\bigoplus}L^{\prime}(\lambda^{(n-1)}),

where the summation is over the highest weights
\lambda^{(n-1)}
of
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n-1}
satisfying the interlacing conditions

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}^{(n)}-\lambda^{(n-1)}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\hskip 14.22636pt\lambda^{(n-1)}_{i}-\lambda^{(n)}_{i+1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\hskip 14.22636pt\forall\ i=1,...,n-1.

(184)

Thus a chain of subalgebras

{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n-1}\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}

produces a decomposition of
L(\lambda)
into one dimensional subspaces, and the one dimensional subspaces are parameterized by the Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns. Such a pattern
\Lambda
(for fixed
\lambda^{(n)}:=\lambda
) is a collection of numbers
\{\lambda^{(i)}_{j}(\Lambda)\}_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n-1}
satisfying the interlacing conditions

\lambda_{j}^{(i)}(\Lambda)-\lambda^{(i-1)}_{j}(\Lambda)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\hskip 14.22636pt\lambda^{(i-1)}_{j}(\Lambda)-\lambda^{(i)}_{j+1}(\Lambda)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\hskip 14.22636pt\forall\ i=1,...,n-1.

(185)

Definition 6.10
.

We denote by
P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})
the set of Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns in
L(\lambda)
, seen as the set of integer points in the real Gelfand-Tsetlin polytope
P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{R})
.

Given the decomposition of
L(\lambda)
into the one dimensional subspaces, one gets a basis by choosing a nonzero vector from each subspace. In particular, there exists a basis
\xi_{\Lambda}
of
L(\lambda)
, called a Gelfand-Tsetlin basis, parameterized by all patterns
\Lambda\in P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})
. The basis is denoted by
\xi_{\Lambda}(u_{\rm cat})
in the introduction, for simplicity let us drop the symbol
u_{\rm cat}
. The structure of the basis obtained in this way is summarized in the following proposition (see e.g.,
[
63
]
).

Proposition 6.11
.

There exists an orthonormal basis
\xi_{\Lambda}
of
L(\lambda)
, called the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis, parameterized by all patterns
\Lambda\in P_{GZ}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})
, such that for any
1\leq i\leq k\leq n
, the actions of
e_{kk}
,
\zeta^{(k)}_{i}
and
\alpha^{(k)}_{i}
on the basis
{\xi_{\Lambda}}
of
L(\lambda)
are given by

\displaystyle e_{kk}\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(\Lambda)-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\lambda^{(k-1)}_{i}(\Lambda)\right)\xi_{\Lambda},

(186)

\displaystyle\zeta^{(k)}_{i}\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(\Lambda)-i+1\right)\xi_{\Lambda},

(187)

and

\displaystyle(\Delta_{L})^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right)\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{k+i}\sqrt{-\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l})\prod_{l=1}^{k+1}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}-1)\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l})}{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}-1)}}\cdot\xi_{\Lambda+\delta^{k}_{i}}

(188)

where the pattern
\Lambda+\delta^{(k)}_{i}
is obtained from
\Lambda
by replacing
\lambda^{(k)}_{i}
by
\lambda^{(k)}_{i}+1
. It is supposed that
\xi_{\Lambda}
is zero if
\Lambda
is not a pattern.

Proof.
We refer to
[
63
, Sectioin 2]
for the existence of the orthogonal Gelfand-Tsetlin basis
\{\eta_{\Lambda}\}
, and the norms
|\eta_{\Lambda}|
of the elements in the basis
[
63
, Proposition 2.4]
(we remark that the basis
\{\xi_{\Lambda}\}
is the orthonormal basis in the original work of Gelfand and Tsetlin
[
34
]
, and is different from the orthogonal one
\{\eta_{\Lambda}\}
from
[
63
]
). We then perform the explicit normalization to get the orthonormal basis
\{\xi_{\Lambda}\}=\eta_{\Lambda}/|\eta_{\Lambda}|
from
\{\eta_{\Lambda}\}
. Then the actions of
e_{kk}
and
\zeta^{(k)}_{i}
on the orthogonal basis
\{\eta_{\Lambda}\}
given in
[
63
, Theorem 2.3]
imply the identities (
186
) and (
187
). Furthermore, the identity (
188
) follows from the action of
(\Delta_{L})^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}
on the orthogonal basis
\{\eta_{\Lambda}\}
given in
[
63
, Section 2.5]
, provided the normalization, from the orthogonal to the orthonormal basis, is accounted for. We also refer the reader to
[
54
]
for a detailed proof of (
188
).

6.4
The explicit expression of quantum Stokes matrices at a caterpillar point

Theorem 6.12
.

For any
1\leq k\leq n-1
, the
(k,k+1)
-entry of
S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})
, as an element in
{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
is given by

\displaystyle(S_{h+})_{k,k+1}

\displaystyle=({\mathrm{i}}h)^{\frac{h\small{(e_{kk}-e_{k+1,k+1}-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}e^{-\frac{he_{kk}}{2}}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod_{l=1}^{k+1}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}-1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}-1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\right)\cdot(\Delta_{L})^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(\frac{h}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right),

and the
(k+1,k)
-entry of
S_{h-}(u_{\rm cat})
is given by

\displaystyle(S_{h-})_{k+1,k}

\displaystyle=(\Delta_{R})_{1,...,k}^{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(\frac{-h}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(-h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod_{l=1}^{k+1}\Gamma(1-h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}+1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(1-h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}-1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}\Gamma(1-h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\right)(-{\mathrm{i}}h)^{\frac{-h\small{(e_{kk}-e_{k+1,k+1}-1})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}e^{\frac{-he_{kk}}{2}}.

Here for any constant
c
,
\Delta^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(cT(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right):=c^{k}\Delta^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right)
.

Proof.
The above explicit expressions can be derived in the same way as the ones in Theorem
1.2
, except that in the non-commutative setting we should use Lemma
6.13
to exchange the orders in the product of the
{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
valued functions
\zeta^{(k)}_{i}
and
\alpha^{(k)}_{i}
.

First, following Definition
6.5
and Theorem
3.41
, we have

\displaystyle S_{h-}(u_{\rm cat})S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})=\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=2,...,n}}C_{h}(E_{k},\delta_{k}(T))\right)\cdot e^{hT}\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=2,...,n}}C_{h}(E_{k},\delta_{k}(T))\right)^{-1},

(189)

where each
C_{h}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T))
denotes the connection matrix of

\displaystyle\frac{dF}{dz}=h\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{k+1}+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\delta_{k+1}(T)}{z}\right)\cdot F.

(190)

Here (by abuse of notation)
E_{k+1}\in{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})\otimes{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
denotes the matrix whose
(k+1,k+1)
entry is
1\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
and other entries are zero. Therefore, to get the explicit expressions of
S_{h\pm}(u_{\rm cat})
, we need to compute each
C_{h}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T))
.

Now the key observation is that, just like the classical case, the equation (
190
) has a fundamental solution given by confluent hypergeometric function
{}_{k}F_{k}
.

Diagonalization in stages.
Just as in the classical case, to simplify the equation (
190
), let us diagonalize the upper left
k
-th submatrix of its coefficient matrix.
Recall that in the classical case, the diagonalization in stages are only taken on the open dense subset
{\rm Herm}(n)_{0}
of
{\rm Herm}(n)
. In the quantum case, the analog of the space
{\rm Herm}(n)_{0}
is a subspace of the representation
L(\lambda)
spanned by the basis elements
\xi_{\Lambda}
such that the inequalities in (
185
) are strict, i.e.,

L(\lambda)_{0}:={\rm span}\{\xi_{\Lambda}~|~\lambda_{j}^{(i)}(\Lambda)-\lambda^{(i-1)}_{j}(\Lambda)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\ \lambda^{(i-1)}_{j}-\lambda^{(i)}_{j+1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},\ \forall\ i,j\}\subset L(\lambda).

However, just as in the classical setting, see Remark
3.28
and
3.30
, the Stokes and connection matrices of (
190
) have no singularities. It is only the diagonalization method of computation (the matrix
P_{k}
) that introduce the singularities. Therefore, to compute the Stokes and connection matrices of the equation (
190
), it is enough to do the diagonalization in stages formally. Thus for simplicity, in below we do the computation in a formal setting, i.e., we formally introduce
\mathcal{P}_{k}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{k}
as below ignoring the pole issue in the denominator.

Recall that
\zeta^{(k)}_{1},...,\zeta^{(k)}_{k}
of
M_{k}(\zeta)
denote the roots of the quantum minor
\Delta^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k}\left(T(\zeta-\frac{h}{2}(k-1))\right)
, which act diagonally on the Gelfand-Zeitlin basis in
L(\lambda)
.
For any
2\leq k\leq n
, let us take the
n\times n
matrix
\mathcal{Q}_{k}
,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Q}_{k})_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k+j}}{\sqrt{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l})\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l})}}(\Delta_{L})^{1,...,\hat{j},...,k}_{1,...,k-1}\left(T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right),\ \text{if}\ 1\leq i,j\leq k

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Q}_{k})_{ii}

\displaystyle=1,\ \ \text{if}\ i>k,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Q}_{k})_{ij}

\displaystyle=0,\ \ \text{otherwise},

and the
n\times n
matrix
\mathcal{P}_{k}
with entries in
{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}_{k})_{ij}

\displaystyle=(\Delta_{R})_{1,...,\hat{i},...,k}^{1,...,k-1}\left(T(\zeta^{(k)}_{j})\right)\cdot\frac{(-1)^{k+i}}{\sqrt{\prod_{l=1,l\neq j}^{k}(\zeta^{(k)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l})\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}(\zeta^{(k)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l})}},\ \text{if}\ 1\leq i,j\leq k

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}_{k})_{ii}

\displaystyle=1,\ \ \text{if}\ i>k,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}_{k})_{ij}

\displaystyle=0,\ \ \text{otherwise}.

The following lemmas (the analog of the classical linear algebra facts in Section
3.6.2
) follow from the Laplace expansion of quantum minors, see e.g.,
[
54
]
.

Lemma 6.13
.

We have

\mathcal{P}_{k}\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{k}=\mathcal{Q}_{k}\cdot\mathcal{P}_{k}={\rm Id}_{n},

and
the matrix
\delta_{k+1}(T_{k}):=\mathcal{Q}_{k}\cdot\delta_{k+1}(T)\cdot\mathcal{P}_{k}
takes the form

\delta_{k+1}(T_{k})=\begin{pmatrix}\zeta^{(k)}_{1}+(k-1)&&&&\alpha^{(k)}_{1}&\star\\
&\ddots&&&\vdots&\star\\
&&\zeta^{(k)}_{k}+(k-1)&&\alpha^{(k)}_{k}&\star\\
&&&&\\
\beta_{1}^{(k)}&\cdots&\beta_{k}^{(k)}&&E_{k+1}&0\\
\star&\star&\star&&\star&{\rm diag}(E_{k+2,k+2},...,E_{nn})\par\end{pmatrix}.

Here

\displaystyle\alpha^{(k)}_{j}(T)=\frac{(-1)^{k+j}}{\sqrt{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l})\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l})}}(\Delta_{L})^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right),

(191)

and for any
1\leq k\leq n-1
, and
1\leq i,j\leq k
, we have the commutators

\displaystyle[\zeta^{(k)}_{i},\alpha^{(k)}_{j}]=\delta_{ij}\alpha^{(k)}_{j}.

(192)

Lemma 6.14
.

For any
1\leq k\leq n-1
, we have

\mathcal{P}_{k}=\mathcal{L}^{(k+1)}\mathcal{P}_{k+1}^{-1}

(193)

where the
n\times n
matrix
\mathcal{L}^{(k+1)}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(k+1)}_{ij}=\alpha^{(k)}_{i}\cdot\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}_{j}^{(k+1)}(\zeta^{(k+1)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k)}_{i})},\ \ \ 1\leq i\leq k,\ \ 1\leq j\leq k+1,

(194)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(k+1)}_{k+1,j}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}_{j}^{(k+1)}},\ \ \ \ \ 1\leq j\leq k+1.

(195)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(k+1)}_{ii}=1,\ \ if\ i>k+1,\ \ \ \mathcal{L}^{(k+1)}_{ij}=0,\ otherwise.

Here the normalizer is

\mathcal{N}_{j}^{(k+1)}(T)=\sqrt{\frac{\prod_{v=1,v\neq j}^{k+1}(\zeta^{(k+1)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{v})}{\prod_{v=1}^{k}(\zeta^{(k+1)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k)}_{v})}}.

(196)

Confluent hypergeometric functions in representation spaces.
Using Lemma
6.13
to "formally" diagonalize the upper left
k
-th submatrix of the coefficient of (
190
), we are left with the equation

\displaystyle\frac{dF}{dz}=h\left({\mathrm{i}}E_{k+1}+\frac{1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\frac{\delta_{k+1}(T_{k})}{z}\right)\cdot F.

(197)

A solution of this equation, as a function valued in
{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})\otimes{\rm End}(L(\lambda)_{0})
, is described using the generalized confluent hypergeometric functions as follows. First recall that the confluent hypergeometric functions associated to any
\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
and
\beta_{j}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,-1,-2,...\},1\leq j\leq m
, are

{}_{k}F_{k}(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{k},\beta_{1},...,\beta_{k};z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\alpha_{1})_{n}\cdots(\alpha_{k})_{n}}{(\beta_{1})_{n}\cdots(\beta_{k})_{n}}\frac{z^{n}}{n!},

(198)

where
(\alpha)_{0}=1
,
(\alpha)_{n}=\alpha\cdots(\alpha+n-1),

n\geq 1
. Since
\{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq k\leq n}
are commutative elements in
{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
, we can introduce the following confluent hypergeometric functions valued in
{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
,

\displaystyle H(z)_{ij}=\scriptsize{\frac{1}{h(\zeta^{(k+1)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k)}_{i})}}\cdot{{}_{k}F_{k}}(\alpha_{ij,1},...,\alpha_{ij,k},\beta_{ij,1},...,\widehat{\beta_{ij,j}},...,\beta_{ij,k+1};{\mathrm{i}}z),\ \ \ 1\leq i\leq k,1\leq j\leq k+1,

\displaystyle H(z)_{k+1,j}={{}_{k}F_{k}}(\alpha_{k+1j,1},...,\alpha_{k+1j,k},\beta_{k+1j,1},...,\widehat{\beta_{k+1j,j}},...,\beta_{k+1j,k+1};{\mathrm{i}}z),\ \ i=k+1,\ 1\leq j\leq k+1,

with the variables
\{\alpha_{ij,l}\}
and
\{\beta_{ij,l}\}
given by

\displaystyle\alpha_{ij,i}=\frac{h}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\left(\zeta^{(k+1)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k)}_{i}\right),\ \ \ 1\leq i\leq k,\ 1\leq j\leq k+1,

\displaystyle\alpha_{(k+1,j),i}=1+\frac{h}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\left(\zeta^{(k+1)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k)}_{i}\right),\ \ \ 1\leq j\leq k+1,

\displaystyle\alpha_{ij,l}=1-\frac{h}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\left(\zeta^{(k+1)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}\right),\ \ \ \ l\neq i,\ 1\leq l\leq k,\ 1\leq i,j\leq k+1,

\displaystyle\beta_{ij,l}=1+\frac{h}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\left(\zeta^{(k+1)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}\right),\ \ \ \ l\neq j,\ 1\leq l\leq k+1,\ \ 1\leq i,j\leq k+1.

Lemma 6.15
.

The equation (
197
) has a solution
F_{h}(z)\in{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})\otimes{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
taking the form

\displaystyle F_{h}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\rm diag}(ha^{(k)}_{1},...,ha^{(k)}_{k},1)&0\\
0&{\rm Id}_{n-k-1}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\left(H_{ij}(z)\right)_{i,j=1}^{k+1}&0\\
0&{\rm Id}_{n-k-1}\end{array}\right)\cdot z^{\frac{h[T_{k+1}]}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}},

where
[T_{k+1}]
is the diagonal part of
T_{k+1}
, i.e.,

{[T_{k+1}]}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\rm diag}({\zeta^{(k+1)}_{1}+k},...,{\zeta^{(k+1)}_{k+1}+k})&0\\
0&{\rm diag}({E_{k+2,k+2}},...,{E_{n,n}})\end{array}\right).

Proof.

We can plug the expression into the equation (
197
). Then the first
k
rows of the equation can be verified directly. And the
k+1
-th row can be verified using the following identity for
m
a parameter

\displaystyle\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}\big(m+\frac{h\zeta^{\left({k+1}\right)}_{j}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}-\frac{h\zeta^{\left({k+1}\right)}_{i}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\big)}{\prod_{i=1}^{k}\big(m+\frac{h\zeta^{\left({k+1}\right)}_{j}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}-\frac{h\zeta^{\left(k\right)}_{i}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\big)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m+\frac{h\zeta^{\left(n\right)}_{j}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}-\frac{h\left(e_{{k+1},{k+1}}-k\right)}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}-\sum_{l=1}^{k}\frac{h\beta^{\left(k\right)}_{l}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{1}{m+\frac{h\zeta^{\left({k+1}\right)}_{j}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}-\frac{h}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\big(\zeta^{\left(k\right)}_{l}-1\big)}\frac{h\alpha^{\left(k\right)}_{l}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}.

The identity in turn can be verified by comparing the actions of its left and right hand sides on the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis. See
[
54
]
for more details.

We denote by
C_{h}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T_{k}))
the connection matrix of the equation (
197
).
Then the Stokes matrices
S_{h+}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T_{k}))
and
the normalized connection matrix

\widetilde{C_{h}}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T)):=C_{h}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T_{k}))\cdot\mathcal{L}^{(k+1)}

of the linear system (
197
) are described by

Proposition 6.16
.

(1)
The entries
s_{ij}
of
S_{h+}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T_{k}))
are

\displaystyle s_{j,k+1}=\frac{({\mathrm{i}}h)^{\frac{h(\zeta^{(k)}_{j}+k-1-e_{k+1,k+1}-1)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}e^{\frac{he_{k+1,k+1}}{2}}\prod_{l=1,l\neq j}^{k}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}-1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod_{l=1}^{k+1}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}-1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\cdot{\alpha^{(k)}_{j}},\ \ \ \text{for}\ j=1,...,k;

and

s_{ii}=e^{\frac{-e_{ii}}{2}},\ \ \ i=1,...,n;\hskip 14.22636pts_{ij}=0,\ \ otherwise.

(2)
. The entries
c_{ij}
of the matrix
\widetilde{C_{h}}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T))
are

\displaystyle c_{ij}=-\frac{({\mathrm{i}}h)^{\frac{h(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{j})}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}e^{\frac{h(\zeta^{(k+1)}_{j}-2\zeta^{(k)}_{i})}{2}}\prod\limits_{l=1}^{k+1}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k+1)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})\prod\limits_{l=1}^{k}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}-1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod\limits_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k+1)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})\prod\limits_{l=1,l\neq j}^{k+1}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}-\zeta^{(k)}_{i}+1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\cdot\frac{\alpha^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}{\mathcal{N}^{(k+1)}_{j}}},

for
1\leq j\leq k+1,1\leq i\leq k
;

\displaystyle c_{k+1,j}=\frac{({\mathrm{i}}h)^{\frac{h(e_{k+1,k+1}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{j}-k)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}e^{\frac{-he_{k+1,k+1}}{2}}\prod_{l=1}^{k+1}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k+1)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{{\mathcal{N}^{(k+1)}_{j}}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k+1)}_{j}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})},\ \ \ \text{for}\ 1\leq j\leq k+1;

and

\ c_{ii}=1\ \ \text{for}\ \ k+2\leq i\leq n;\hskip 14.22636ptc_{ij}=0\ \ \text{otherwise}.

Just like in the classical case,
the proposition follows from the known asymptotics of the functions
{}_{k}F_{k}
. In the quantum case, we only need to use the commutative relation (
192
) to commute
a^{(k)}_{j}
and the arguments
\zeta^{(k)}_{j}
in the computation. We refer the reader to
[
54
]
for the full details.

Subdiagonal entries of quantum Stokes matrices.
By the equivariant property, when restricts to
L(\lambda)_{0}\subset L(\lambda)
, the connection matrices of the equations (
190
) and (
197
) are related by

C_{h}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T))=\mathcal{P}_{k}\cdot C_{h}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T_{k}))\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{k},

where
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is given in Lemma
6.13
. By the relation
\widetilde{C_{h}}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T))=C_{h}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T_{k}))\cdot\mathcal{L}^{(k+1)}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{k}\mathcal{P}_{k+1}=\mathcal{L}^{(k+1)}
, we get

\displaystyle C_{h}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T))=\mathcal{P}_{k}\cdot\widetilde{C_{h}}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T))\cdot\mathcal{Q}_{k+1}.

(204)

Plugging (
204
) for all
k=1,...,n-1
into (
189
) and using
\mathcal{P}_{k}\mathcal{Q}_{k}={\rm Id}
lead to

\displaystyle S_{h-}(u_{\rm cat})S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})=\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=2,...,n}}\widetilde{C_{h}}(E_{k},\delta_{k}(T))\right)\cdot e^{hT_{n}}\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=2,...,n}}\widetilde{C_{h}}(E_{k},\delta_{k}(T))\right)^{-1}.

Here recall
T_{n}={\rm diag}\left(\zeta^{(n)}_{1}+(n-1),...,\zeta^{(n)}_{n}+(n-1)\right)\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))\otimes{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
.
Then just as in the classical case, a manipulation of (blocked) Gauss decomposition, as well as the explicit formula of
\widetilde{C_{h}}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T))
and
S_{h+}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T_{k}))
given in Proposition
6.16
, gives rise to the formula of
S_{h+}(E_{k+1},\delta_{k+1}(T_{k}))_{k,k+1}
in Theorem
6.12
.

6.5
The leading terms of quantum Stokes matrices via the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis

Given any
L(\lambda)
, let us think of the associated quantum Stokes matrices as blocked matrices. Let us take a proper norm on the vector space
{\rm End}(\lambda)
, then up to a slight modification, Proposition
3.35
and Proposition
1.4
can be applied to the quantum/blocked cases. That is if we introduce the matrix

g(u;T)=\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=1,...,n-1}}(z_{k})^{\frac{\delta_{k}(T)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))\otimes{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{n}),

(here recall that
z_{k}
are the coordinates in (
56
)) then

Proposition 6.17
.

For any fixed none zero real number
h
, we have as
u\in U_{\rm id}
and
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
,

\displaystyle S_{h\pm}(u)=S_{h\pm}\left(u_{\rm cat},\hskip 5.69054ptg(hu;hT)\cdot hT\cdot g(hu;hT)^{-1}+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}\mathcal{O}(z_{k}^{-1})\right).

(205)

Furthermore, the subdiagonal entries of
S_{h\pm}(u)
satisfy

\displaystyle S_{h+}(u)_{k,k+1}={l}^{(+)}_{k,k+1}(u)+o(l^{(\pm)}_{k,k+1}(u)),\hskip 8.53581pt\text{as}\ u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}\ \text{ from }U_{\rm id},

(206)

\displaystyle S_{h-}(u)_{k+1,k}={l}^{(-)}_{k+1,k}(u)+o(l^{(-)}_{k+1,k}(u)),\hskip 8.53581pt\text{as}\ u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}\ \text{ from }U_{\rm id},

(207)

where the leading terms
{l}^{(\pm)}_{k,k+1}(u)
are given by the expressions
S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k+1}
and
S_{h-}(u_{\rm cat})_{k+1,k}
in Theorem
6.12
provided we replace respectively
(\Delta_{L})^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(\frac{h}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right)
and
(\Delta_{R})_{1,...,k}^{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(\frac{-h}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right)
by

(u_{k}-u_{k-1})^{\frac{he_{kk}-h\zeta^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\left({u_{k+1}-u_{k}}\right)^{\frac{h\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-he_{k+1,k+1}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\cdot(\Delta_{L})^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(\frac{h}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right)

(208)

and

(\Delta_{R})_{1,...,k}^{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(\frac{-h}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right)\cdot(u_{k}-u_{k-1})^{\frac{h\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-he_{kk}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}\left({u_{k+1}-u_{k}}\right)^{\frac{he_{k+1,k+1}-h\zeta^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}.

(209)

By Proposition
6.11
and
6.17
, the leading terms of entries of quantum Stokes matrices, as elements in
{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
, can be written down explicitly in terms of the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis.

6.6
Regularized limits of quantum Stokes matrices from the isomonodromy deformation

In this subsection, let us explain the regularized limit of quantum Stokes matrices given in Proposition
6.17
from the viewpoint of isomonodromy deformation. It follows, from the general theory of isomonodromic deformation of meromorphic linear system of ordinary differential equations
[
47
,
48
]
that

Proposition 6.18
.

As a function of
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
, the entries of the Stokes matrix
S_{h\pm}(u)
satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{\partial S_{h\pm}(u)_{kl}}{\partial u_{i}}=h\sum_{j=1,...,n,\text{ and }j\neq i}\frac{[e_{ij}e_{ji},S_{h\pm}(u)_{kl}]}{u_{i}-u_{j}}\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda)).

(210)

Here
e_{ij}
is seen as an element in
{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
via the given representation of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
on
L(\lambda).

Now let us consider the equation for a function
W(u)\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))

\displaystyle\frac{\partial W}{\partial u_{i}}=h\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{e_{ij}e_{ji}}{u_{i}-u_{j}}\cdot W,\hskip 14.22636pti=1,...,n.

(211)

The equation (
211
) was called the Casimir equation
[
72
]
. In terms of the variables
z_{i}
introduced in (
56
), the equation takes the form

\frac{\partial W}{\partial z_{k}}=\left(\frac{V_{k}}{z_{k}}+Reg\right)\cdot W,\ \text{ for }k=1,...,n-1

where
Reg
stands for some
{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
valued rational function of
z_{k}
regular at
z_{k}=\infty
, and
V_{k}:=\sum_{1\leq j\leq k-1}e_{kj}e_{jk}\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
.

The elements
V_{k}
commute with each other, and are the analog of the Jucys-Murphy elements in the group algebra of
S_{n}
. Then let
W(u)\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
be the solution of the equation (
211
)
with the prescribed asymptotics

\displaystyle W(u)\cdot\overrightarrow{\prod_{k=1,...,n-1}}(z_{k})^{h{V_{k}}}\rightarrow 1,\hskip 8.53581pt\text{ as }z_{k}\rightarrow\infty.

(212)

The existence of
W(u)
follows from the general theory that a formal solution of differential equations with regular singularities is in fact analytic.

It follows from (
210
) and (
211
) that for any
k,l
the element
W(u)^{-1}\cdot S_{\pm}(u;h)_{kl}\cdot W(u)\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))
is a constant on
u\in U_{\rm id}\subset\mathfrak{t}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
. By the asymptotics (
212
) and (
208
), (
209
) at the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
, we have

Proposition 6.19
.

The Stokes matrices
S_{+}(u_{\rm cat};h)\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))\otimes{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
at the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
satisfy

\displaystyle S_{h\pm}(u_{\rm cat})_{kl}:=W(u)^{-1}\cdot S_{\pm}(u;h)_{kl}\cdot W(u),\hskip 8.53581ptk,l=1,...,n.

(213)

Then we notice that letting
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
from
U_{\rm id}
in the identity (
213
) (for
l=k+1
), the asymptotics (
212
) of
W(u)
matches up with the singular part in the expressions (
206
) and (
208
). Just like the classical case, it interprets the leading asymptotics of the quantum Stokes matrices via the isomonodromy approach.

6.7
Representation of quantum groups from quantum Stokes matrices at
u_{\rm cat}

Now we can generalize Theorem
1.16
to the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
.

Theorem 6.20
.

For any fixed none zero real number
h
, the map (with
q=e^{h/2}
)

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{q}(u_{\rm cat}):U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})&\rightarrow{\rm End}(L(\lambda))~;\\
e_{i}&\mapsto\frac{S_{+}(u_{\rm cat})_{i,i}^{-1}\cdot S_{+}(u_{\rm cat})_{i,i+1}}{q^{-1}-q},\\
f_{i}&\mapsto\frac{S_{-}(u_{\rm cat})_{i+1,i}\cdot S_{-}(u_{\rm cat})_{i,i}^{-1}}{q-q^{-1}},\\
q^{h_{i}}&\mapsto S_{+}(u_{\rm cat})_{i,i}\end{split}

defines a representation of the Drinfeld-Jimbo quantum group
U_{q}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
on the vector space
L(\lambda)
.

Proof.

It follows from the fact that the gauge transformation transformation
W(u)
in Proposition
6.19
preserves the defining relations of quantum group. For example, for any
u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
by Theorem
1.16
we have

\displaystyle S_{\pm}(u)_{k,k+1}^{2}S_{\pm}(u)_{k-1,k}-(q+q^{-1})S_{\pm}(u)_{k,k+1}S_{\pm}(u)_{k-1,k}S_{\pm}(u)_{k,k+1}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle S_{\pm}(u)_{k-1,k}S_{\pm}(u)_{k,k+1}^{2}=0.

(214)

Since the conjugation by
W(u)
preserves the above relation (and the other defining relations), the corresponding entries of
S_{\pm}(u_{\rm cat})
satisfy the same relation (
214
) (and the other defining relations).

The above theorem can also be checked directly from the identities (
206
)-(
209
). For example, using (
206
) and (
208
), one checks
S_{\pm}(u_{\rm cat})
satisfy the same relation (
214
) (after taking the regularized limits as
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
).

Remark 6.21
.

More generally, Theorem
1.16
can be generalized from
\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})
to the De Concini-Procesi space. Therefore, the regularized limit of quantum Stokes matrices preserves the underlying quantum group structures.

7
Crystals and cactus group actions arising from the WKB approximation

In the previous section, we introduce the relation between the regularized limits of quantum Stokes matrices at
u_{\rm cat}
and Gelfand-Tsetlin basis. In this section, we deepen the relation between Stokes phenomenon and representation theory by showing that the WKB approximation of quantum Stokes matrices at a caterpillar point gives rise to
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystals. Section
7.1
computes explicitly the WKB approximation. Then Section
7.2
introduces the notion of WKB datum of quantum Stokes matrices. In the end, Section
7.5
gives a realization of the cactus group action on crystals via the wall-crossing formula of Stokes matrices at a caterpillar point.

7.1
WKB approximation of quantum Stokes matrices at caterpillar points

Since the derivative in equation (
6
) is multiplied by a small parameter
1/h
, we will call the leading term, as
h\rightarrow+\infty
, of Stokes matrices of (
6
) as the WKB approximation. In this subsection, we study the WKB approximation of Stokes matrices at
u_{\rm cat}
given in (
6.12
).

First, for any Gelfand-Tsetlin pattern
\Lambda
of
L(\lambda)
given in Section
6.3
, set

\displaystyle x^{(k)}_{j}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle:=-\lambda^{(k)}_{j}+\lambda^{(k-1)}_{j-1}-\lambda^{(k)}_{j-1}+\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j},\hskip 8.53581pt1\leq j\leq k+1,

(215)

\displaystyle y^{(k)}_{j}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{(k)}_{j}-\lambda^{(k-1)}_{j}+\lambda^{(k)}_{j+1}-\lambda^{(k+1)}_{j+1},\hskip 8.53581pt0\leq j\leq k,

(216)

and

\displaystyle X^{(k)}_{j}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=1}^{j}x^{(k)}_{i}(\Lambda),\hskip 8.53581pt1\leq j\leq k+1,

(217)

\displaystyle Y^{(k)}_{j}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=j}^{k}y^{(k)}_{i}(\Lambda),\hskip 8.53581pt0\leq j\leq k.

(218)

Note that
Y^{(k)}_{0}(\Lambda)=Y^{(k)}_{j}(\Lambda)-X^{(k)}_{j}(\Lambda)
for any
j=0,...,k+1
.
Furthermore, we define

\displaystyle wt_{k}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(\Lambda)-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\lambda^{(k-1)}_{i}(\Lambda),

(219)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle={\rm max}\{X_{1}^{(k)}(\Lambda),X_{2}^{(k)}(\Lambda),...,X_{k}^{(k)}(\Lambda)\},

(220)

\displaystyle\phi_{i}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle={\rm max}\{Y_{1}^{(k)}(\Lambda),Y_{2}^{(k)}(\Lambda),...,Y_{k}^{(k)}(\Lambda)\},

(221)

and define the functions
l_{1}(\Lambda)<\cdots<l_{m_{k}}(\Lambda)
of
\Lambda\in P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})
be those ordered labels such that

\displaystyle X^{(k)}_{l_{j}}(\Lambda)=\varepsilon_{k}(\Lambda).

(222)

Let us denote by

P^{k}_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z}):=\{\Lambda\in P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})~|~\Lambda+\delta^{(k)}_{l_{1}}\in P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})\ \text{and}\ \Lambda+\delta^{(k)}_{l_{i}}\notin P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})\ \text{for}\ i=2,...,m_{k}\}.

(223)

In particular, all
\Lambda
satisfying
m_{k}(\Lambda)=1
and
\Lambda+\delta^{(k)}_{l_{i}}\notin P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})
are in
P^{k}_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})
. From (
215
)–(
222
), the function
m_{k}(\Lambda)
is defined for any point
\Lambda
in the real polytope
P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{R})
. Thus
P^{k}_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{R}):=P^{k}_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
is an open dense part of
P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{R})
, whose complements are cut out by various equalities between
\lambda^{(j)}_{i}(\Lambda)
for
j=k-1,k,k+1
. By this reason,
the elements in the subset
P^{k}_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})
are called generic (it gives precise meaning of the word generic in Theorem
1.22
).

Let
\{\xi_{\Lambda}\}
be the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis given in Section
6.3
. Then we have

Proposition 7.1
.

For
k=1,...,n-1
and for any
\Lambda\in P^{k}_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})
, there exists real valued functions
\theta_{k}(\xi_{\Lambda})\in[0,2\pi)
of the patterns
\Lambda
(independent of
q=e^{h/2}
) such that

S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k+1}\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}\sim q^{-wt_{k+1}(\Lambda)-\varepsilon_{k}(\Lambda)+{\mathrm{i}}\theta_{k}}\xi_{\Lambda+\delta^{(k)}_{l_{1}(\Lambda)}},\hskip 8.53581pt\text{as}\ \ q\rightarrow\infty.

(224)

Here recall the pattern
\Lambda+\delta^{(k)}_{l_{1}}
is obtained from
\Lambda
by replacing
\lambda^{(k)}_{l_{1}}
by
\lambda^{(k)}_{l_{1}}+1
.

Proof.
By the asymptotics of gamma function

{\rm In}(\Gamma(1+z))\sim z{\rm In}(z)-z+\frac{1}{2}{\rm In}(z)+\frac{1}{2}{\rm In}(2\pi)+\frac{1}{12z}+o\left(\frac{1}{z}\right),\ \ \text{as}\ |z|\rightarrow\infty,\ \ |{\rm arg}(z)|<\pi,

for
r
a real number and
h\rightarrow+\infty
, we have

{\rm In}\left(\Gamma\left(1+\frac{rh}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)\right)\sim\frac{rh{\rm In}(h)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}+\frac{rh}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}{\rm In}\left(\frac{|r|}{2\pi}\right)-\frac{|r|h}{4}-\frac{rh}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}+\frac{1}{2}{\rm In}\left(\frac{rh}{{\mathrm{i}}}\right).

(225)

Here we use
{\rm In}(\frac{\pm 1}{{\mathrm{i}}})=\mp\frac{\pi{\mathrm{i}}}{2}
to separate the real and imaginary part of the
h
linear terms. By (
187
),

\zeta^{(k)}_{i}\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}=(\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(\Lambda)-i+1)\cdot\xi_{\Lambda},

by abuse of notation, we will take
\zeta^{(k)}_{i}
as the number
\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(\Lambda)-i+1
when the vector
\xi_{\Lambda}
is specified.
Then by (
225
) and the interlacing inequalities between
\lambda^{(i)}_{j}
for
i=k-1,k,k+1
,

\displaystyle{\rm In}\left(\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod_{l=1}^{k+1}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}-1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}-1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\right)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\frac{h{\rm In}(h)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}A^{(k)}_{i}+{\mathrm{i}}h\theta^{(k)}_{i}+\frac{h}{4}B^{(k)}_{i}+C^{(k)}_{i},\hskip 14.22636pt\text{as}\ h\rightarrow+\infty,

where

\displaystyle A^{(k)}_{i}=\sum_{l=1}^{k-1}\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l}+\sum_{l=1}^{k+1}\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}-2\sum_{l=1}^{k}\zeta^{(k)}_{l}+2,

\displaystyle B^{(k)}_{i}=-2\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l})+2\sum_{l=i+1}^{k}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l})+\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l})

\displaystyle\hskip 34.1433pt-\sum_{l=i}^{k-1}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l})+\sum_{l=1}^{i}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l})-\sum_{l=i+1}^{k+1}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}),

\displaystyle C^{(k)}_{i}=\frac{1}{2}{\rm In}\left(\frac{(2\pi{\mathrm{i}})^{2k-2}}{h^{2k}}\cdot\frac{-\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}-1)}{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l})\prod_{l=1}^{k+1}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}-1)\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}(\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l})}\right),

and (since
\theta^{(k)}_{i}
’s are real, they do not affect the leading asymptotics, but for completeness let us still list them)

\displaystyle\theta^{(k)}_{i}=\frac{1}{2\pi}A^{(k)}_{i}-\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}}{2\pi}{\rm In}(\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{l}-\zeta^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi})+\sum_{l=i+1}^{k}(\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{l}-\zeta^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi}){\rm In}(\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}}{2\pi})

\displaystyle\hskip 34.1433pt-\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{l}+1-\zeta^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi}{\rm In}(\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}-1}{2\pi})+\sum_{l=i+1}^{k}(\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{l}-\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-1}{2\pi}){\rm In}(\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}+1-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}}{2\pi})

\displaystyle\hskip 34.1433pt+\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}(\frac{\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l}-\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-1}{2\pi}){\rm In}(\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}+1-\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l}}{2\pi})-\sum_{l=i}^{k-1}(\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l}-1}{2\pi}){\rm In}(\frac{\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l}+1-\zeta^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi})

\displaystyle\hskip 34.1433pt+\sum_{l=1}^{i}(\frac{\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}-\zeta^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi}){\rm In}(\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}}{2\pi})-\sum_{l=i+1}^{k+1}(\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}}{2\pi}){\rm In}(\frac{\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}-\zeta^{(k)}_{i}}{2\pi}).

Finally, the action of
S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k+1}
on
\xi_{\Lambda}
is given by

\displaystyle 2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\cdot({\mathrm{i}}h)^{\frac{h\small{(e_{kk}-e_{k+1,k+1}-1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}e^{\frac{-he_{kk}}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod_{l=1}^{k+1}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k+1)}_{l}-1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\frac{\prod_{l=1,l\neq i}^{k}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k)}_{l}-1}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}{\prod_{l=1}^{k-1}\Gamma(1+h\frac{\zeta^{(k)}_{i}-\zeta^{(k-1)}_{l}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}})}\Delta^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(\frac{h}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right)\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left((h)^{\frac{h\small{(e_{kk}-e_{k+1,k+1}-1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}e^{\frac{h{\rm In}(h)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}A^{(k)}_{i}}\right)\left(e^{\frac{-h\small{(e_{kk}+e_{k+1,k+1}+1)}}{4}}e^{{\mathrm{i}}h\theta^{(k)}_{i}+\frac{h}{4}B^{(k)}_{i}}\right)\cdot 2\pi{\mathrm{i}}e^{C^{(k)}_{i}}\Delta^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}q^{-wt_{k+1}(\Lambda)-X^{(k)}_{i}(\Lambda)+2{\mathrm{i}}h\theta^{(k)}_{i}}\xi_{\Lambda+\delta^{(k)}_{i}}.

Here in the last equality, we use the identities (
186
), (
187
), (
215
) and (
217
) to get

\displaystyle(h)^{\frac{h\small{(e_{kk}-e_{k+1,k+1}-1)}}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}}e^{\frac{h{\rm In}(h)}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}A^{(k)}_{i}}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle e^{\frac{-h\small{(e_{k+1,k+1}+e_{kk}+1)}}{4}}e^{\frac{h}{4}B^{(k)}_{i}+{\mathrm{i}}h\theta^{(k)}_{i}}\cdot\xi_{\Lambda+\delta^{(k)}_{i}}

\displaystyle=q^{-wt_{k+1}(\Lambda)-X^{(k)}_{i}(\Lambda)+2{\mathrm{i}}h\theta^{(k)}_{i}}\xi_{\Lambda+\delta^{(k)}_{i}},

and use the
expression (
188
) of
\Delta^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right)\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}
and the expression of
C^{(k)}_{i}
to get

\displaystyle 2\pi{\mathrm{i}}\cdot e^{C^{(k)}_{i}}\Delta^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(\frac{h}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right)\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{h}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}\right)^{k}e^{C^{(k)}_{i}}\Delta^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right)\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\xi_{\Lambda+\delta^{(k)}_{i}}.

Then the proposition follows from the definitions (
220
) and (
222
) of
\varepsilon_{k}(\Lambda)
and
l_{i}(\Lambda)
with
i=1,...,m_{k}
, and the assumption
m_{k}(\Lambda)=1.
The constant
\theta_{k}
in (
224
) is just
2\theta^{(k)}_{l_{1}}
.

7.2
WKB operators

In this subsection, we introduce a combinatorial structure to encode the WKB leading terms (of entries) of quantum Stokes matrices at a caterpillar point.

Recall that the subset
P_{GT}^{k}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})\subset P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})
is defined in (
223
). Let us denote by
E^{k}_{GT}\subset E_{GT}(\lambda)
the subset consisting of the basis elements parametrized by
P_{GT}^{k}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})
, then the formula (
224
) shows that taking the
q
leading term of
S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k+1}
naturally induces a map

\displaystyle\widetilde{e_{k}}(u_{\rm cat}):\ E^{k}_{GT}\rightarrow E_{GT}~;~\widetilde{e_{k}}(u_{\rm cat})(\xi_{\Lambda})=\xi_{\Lambda+\delta^{(k)}_{l_{1}}}.

In the following, for simplicity, if there is no ambiguity we will write
\widetilde{e_{k}}
for
\widetilde{e_{k}}(u_{\rm cat})
. Equivalently, as we identify
E_{GT}(\lambda)
with
P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})
by mapping
\xi_{\Lambda}
to
\Lambda
,
\widetilde{e_{k}}
can be seen as a map

\displaystyle\widetilde{e_{k}}(u_{\rm cat}):\ P_{GT}^{k}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})~;~\widetilde{e_{k}}({\Lambda})={\Lambda+\delta^{(k)}_{l_{1}}}.

The map has a canonical extension to the whole
P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})
as follows.

First, the expression of
\widetilde{e_{k}}
is universal, i.e., doesn’t depend on the choice of
\lambda
. Thus for any positive integer
N
, if we set
N\lambda=(N\lambda^{(n)}_{1},...,N\lambda^{(n)}_{n})
, then
\widetilde{e_{k}}
can be equivalently seen as
a map from
P^{k}_{GT}(\lambda;\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{N}):=\{\Lambda\in P^{k}_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{R})~|~\lambda^{(i)}_{j}(\Lambda)\in\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{N}\}
to
P_{GT}(\lambda;\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{N}):=\{\Lambda\in P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{R})~|~\lambda^{(i)}_{j}(\Lambda)\in\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{N}\}

\displaystyle\widetilde{e_{k}}^{\frac{1}{N}}(\Lambda):=\Lambda+\frac{1}{N}\delta^{(k)}_{l},

(226)

where
\lambda^{(i)}_{j}(\Lambda+\frac{1}{N}\delta^{(k)}_{l})=\lambda^{(i)}_{j}+\frac{\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}}{N}
.
Now as
N\rightarrow\infty
, the discrete "dynamical system" (
226
) approaches to a unique continuous system in the inner part of the whole real polytope
P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{R})
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{e_{k}}^{t}(\Lambda):=\Lambda+t_{1}\delta^{(k)}_{r_{1}}+\cdots+t_{m}\delta^{(k)}_{r_{m}},

(227)

where the time

t\in\Big[0,\hskip 5.69054pt\sum_{i=1}^{k}({\rm min}(\lambda^{(k+1)}_{i}(\Lambda),\lambda^{(k-1)}_{i-1}(\Lambda))-\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(\Lambda))\Big],

and
t_{1},...,t_{m}
,
r_{1},...,r_{m}
are determined by

\displaystyle{r_{1}}=l_{1}(\Lambda+s\delta^{(k)}_{r_{1}}),\hskip 8.53581pt\text{for all}\ 0\leq s<t_{1},

\displaystyle r_{i}=l_{1}(\Lambda+t_{1}\delta^{(k)}_{r_{1}}+\cdots t_{i-1}\delta^{(k)}_{r_{i-1}}+s\delta^{(k)}_{r_{i}}),\hskip 8.53581pt\text{for all}\ 0\leq s<t_{i},\ for\ i=2,...,m,

\displaystyle t=t_{1}+\cdots+t_{m}.

Here the function
l_{1}(\Lambda)
is defined for any point
\Lambda
in
P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{R})
just as (
215
)–(
222
).

This
continuation is unique, thus canonically determines an extension of
\widetilde{e_{k}}
to the complements of the "generic part"
P^{k}_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})
in
P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})
. It gives rise to

Definition 7.2
.

For each
k
, the WKB operator
\widetilde{e_{k}}
from
E_{GT}\cong P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})
to
E_{GT}\cup\{0\}
is given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{e_{k}}\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}(u_{\rm cat}):=\xi_{\Lambda+\delta^{(k)}_{l_{1}(\Lambda)}}(u_{\rm cat}),\hskip 14.22636pt\forall\Lambda\in P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z})

(228)

Here recall that
l_{1}(\Lambda)
is the integer given by (
222
), and it is supposed that
\widetilde{e_{k}}\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}
is zero if
\Lambda+\delta^{(k)}_{l_{1}(\Lambda)}
doesn’t belong to
P_{GT}(\lambda;{\mathbb{Z}})
.

The above computation and discussion carry to the WKB approximation of the entries
S_{h-}(u_{\rm cat})_{k+1,k}
of lower triangular Stokes matrix
S_{h-}(u_{\rm cat})
. It produces the same set
E_{GT}(\lambda)
, and induces operators
\widetilde{f_{k}}
on
E_{GT}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f_{k}}\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}=\xi_{\Lambda-\delta^{(k)}_{l_{m_{k}}(\Lambda)}},\hskip 14.22636pt\forall\Lambda\in P_{GT}(\lambda;\mathbb{Z}).

(229)

One checks that
\widetilde{e_{k}}
and
\widetilde{f_{k}}
satisfy that
for all
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in P_{GT}(\lambda;{\mathbb{Z}})
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k}(\Lambda)={\rm max}\{j:\widetilde{e_{k}}^{j}(\xi_{\Lambda})\neq 0\},

\displaystyle\phi_{k}(\Lambda)={\rm max}\{j:\widetilde{f_{k}}^{j}(\xi_{\Lambda})\neq 0\},

and

\widetilde{e_{k}}\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}=\xi_{\Lambda}^{\prime}\hskip 8.53581ptif\ and\ only\ if\hskip 8.53581pt\widetilde{f_{k}}\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}^{\prime}=\xi_{\Lambda}.

Definition 7.3
.

We call
(E_{GT}(\lambda),\widetilde{e_{k}},\widetilde{f_{k}},\varepsilon_{k},\phi_{k})
the WKB datum of the Stokes matrices
S_{h\pm}(u_{\rm cat})
at the caterpillar point associated to the representation
L(\lambda)
.

7.3
WKB datum are crystals

In Section
7.3.1
we recall the notion of crystals and their tensor products. In Section
7.3.2
we prove that the WKB datum of quantum Stokes matrices at
u_{\rm cat}
are
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystals. Furthermore, in Section
7.3.3
we also realize the tensor products of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystals by WKB analysis.

7.3.1
Crystals and tensor products

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a semisimple Lie algebra with a Cartan datum
(A,\Delta_{+}=\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in I},\Delta^{\vee}_{+}=\{\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\}_{i\in I},P,P^{\vee})
be a Cartan datum, where
P\subset\mathfrak{h}^{*}
denotes the weight lattice,
I
denotes the set of vertices of its Dynkin diagram,
\alpha_{i}\in I
denote its simple
roots, and
\alpha^{\vee}_{i}
the simple coroots.

Definition 7.4
.

A
\mathfrak{g}
-crystal is a finite set
B
along with maps

\displaystyle wt

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle B\rightarrow P,

\displaystyle\tilde{e}_{i},\tilde{f}_{i}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle B\rightarrow B\cup\{0\},\hskip 5.69054pti\in I,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i},\phi_{i}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle B\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\},\hskip 5.69054pti\in I,

satisfying for all
b,b^{\prime}\in B
, and
i\in I
,

•

\tilde{f}_{i}(b)=b^{\prime}
if and if
b=\tilde{e}_{i}(b^{\prime})
, in which case

wt(b^{\prime})=wt(b)-\alpha_{i},\hskip 8.53581pt\varepsilon_{i}(b^{\prime})=\varepsilon_{i}(b)+1,\hskip 8.53581pt\phi_{i}(b^{\prime})=\phi_{i}(b)-1.

•

\phi_{i}(b)=\varepsilon_{i}(b)+\langle wt(b),\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\rangle
, and if
\phi_{i}(b)=\varepsilon_{i}(b)=-\infty
, then
\tilde{e}_{i}(b)=\tilde{f}_{i}(b)=0.

The map
wt
is called the weight map,
\tilde{e}_{i}
and
\tilde{f}_{i}
are called Kashiwara operators or crystal operators.

Definition 7.5
.

Let
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
be two crystals. The tensor product
B_{1}\otimes B_{2}
is the crystal with the underlying set
B_{1}\times B_{2}
(the Cartesian product) and structure maps

\displaystyle wt(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle wt(b_{1})+wt(b_{2}),

\displaystyle\tilde{e}_{i}(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}(\tilde{e}_{i}(b_{1}),b_{2}),&if\ \varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})>\phi(b_{2})\\
(b_{1},\tilde{e}_{i}(b_{2})),&otherwise\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{i}(b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}(\tilde{f}_{i}(b_{1}),b_{2}),&if\ \varepsilon_{i}(b_{1})\geq\phi(b_{2})\\
(b_{1},\tilde{f}_{i}(b_{2})),&otherwise.\end{array}\right.

7.3.2
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
-crystals from WKB approximation

Let us take the Cartan datum of type
A_{n-1}
, where
I=\{1,2,...,n-1\}
, the weight lattice
P=\mathbb{C}\{v_{1},...,v_{n}\}
, and
\alpha_{i}=v_{i}-v_{i+1}
,
\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
is given by
\langle v_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee}\rangle=\delta_{ij}-\delta_{i,j+1}.
Let us consider the WKB datum, given in Definition
7.4
, of the quantum Stokes matrices at the caterpillar point
u_{\rm cat}
associated to the representation
L(\lambda)
. Let us define a weight map

wt:E_{GT}(\lambda)\rightarrow P~;~\xi_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\sum_{k=1}^{n}wt_{k}(\Lambda)v_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(\Lambda)-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\lambda^{(k-1)}_{i}(\Lambda))v_{k}.

The following theorem gives a realization of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystals from the WKB approximation.

Theorem 7.6
.

The WKB datum
(E_{GT}(\lambda),\widetilde{e_{k}}(u_{\rm cat}),\widetilde{f_{k}}(u_{\rm cat}),\varepsilon_{k},\phi_{k})
with the weight map
wt
is a
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
-crystal.

Proof.
By the formula (
228
) and (
229
), the WKB operators on the finite set
E_{GT}(\lambda)
are:

\displaystyle\widetilde{e_{k}}\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\xi_{\Lambda+\delta^{(k)}_{l}},&if\ \ \varepsilon_{k}(\Lambda)>0,\\
0,&if\ \ \varepsilon_{k}(\Lambda)=0,\end{array}\right.\text{where}\ l={\rm min}\{j=1,...,k~|~X_{j}^{(k)}(\Lambda)=\varepsilon_{k}(\Lambda)\},

\displaystyle\widetilde{f_{k}}\cdot\xi_{\Lambda}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\xi_{\Lambda-\delta^{(k)}_{l}},&if\ \ \phi_{k}(\Lambda)>0,\\
0,&if\ \ \phi_{k}(\Lambda)=0,\end{array}\right.\text{where}\ l={\rm max}\{j=1,...,k~|~Y_{j}^{(k)}(\Lambda)=\phi_{k}(\Lambda)\}.

Then one can verify directly the conditions in Definition
7.4
via the explicit expressions.

To avoid the direct but lengthy computation, one can also compare the WKB datum with the known
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
-crystal structures in literature: the explicit realization (
7.3.2
) and (
7.3.2
) of the WKB operators
\widetilde{e_{k}}
,
\widetilde{f_{k}}
coincide with the known
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
-crystal operators realized on the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis, see e.g.,
[
40
]
; or equivalently, under the natural bijection between semistandard Young tableaux and Geland-Testlin patterns, the WKB datum
(E_{GT},\widetilde{e_{k}},\widetilde{f_{k}},\varepsilon_{k},\phi_{k})
coincides with the
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystal structure on semistandard Young tableaux, see e.g.,
[
43
]
.

7.3.3
Tensor products from WKB approximation

Recall that given any representation
L(\lambda)
, the quantum Stokes matrices at
u_{\rm cat}
produce operators
S_{h\pm}(u_{\rm cat})_{ij}\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda)).
Now given two representations
L(\lambda_{1})
and
L(\lambda_{2})
, let us consider the actions of

\displaystyle S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k}\otimes S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k+1}+S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k+1}\otimes S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})_{k+1,k+1}

(238)

\displaystyle S_{h-}(u_{\rm cat})_{k+1,k}\otimes S_{h-}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k}+S_{h-}(u_{\rm cat})_{k+1,k+1}\otimes S_{h-}(u_{\rm cat})_{k+1,k},

(239)

for
k=1,...,n-1
, on the tensor product
L(\lambda_{1})\otimes L(\lambda_{2})
. Let
\{\xi_{\Lambda_{1}}\}
and
\{\xi_{\Lambda_{2}}\}
be the basis of
L(\lambda_{1})
and
L(\lambda_{2})
respectively. Similar to Proposition
7.1
, one can compute the WKB leading term of the operators in (
238
) under the basis
\xi_{\Lambda_{1}}\otimes\xi_{\Lambda_{2}}
. (Since the diagonal elements
S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k}
of quantum Stokes matrices have a rather simple expression, the computation is direct and is omitted here.) Then following the same argument as previous sections, one verifies

Proposition 7.7
.

The WKB approximation of the operators in (
238
) induces the crystal operators
\widetilde{e_{k}}
and
\widetilde{f_{k}}
on the tensor product
E(u_{\rm cat};\lambda_{1})\otimes E(u_{\rm cat};\lambda_{2})
of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}-
crystals.

7.4
Proof of Theorem
1.22

The discussions in this subsection, particularly Proposition
7.1
, show that for each
k=1,...,n-1
, there exists canonical operators
\widetilde{e_{k}}(u_{\rm cat})
acting on the finite set
E_{GT}(\lambda)
such that for any generic element
\xi(u_{\rm cat})\in E_{GT}(\lambda)
, there exist real valued functions
c_{k1}(\xi)
and
\psi_{k1}(h,u,\xi)
such that

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm lim}\limits_{h\rightarrow+\infty}\left(S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k+1}\cdot e^{c_{k1}(\xi)h+{\mathrm{i}}\psi_{k1}(h,u,\xi)}\xi(u_{\rm cat})\right)=\widetilde{e_{k}}(\xi(u_{\rm cat})).

Then following Proposition
6.17
, the leading terms
{l}^{(+)}_{k,k+1}(u)
of the subdiagonal entries of
S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})
as
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
only differ from
S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k+1}
by some extra terms, appearing in the replacement of
\Delta^{1,...,k}_{1,...,k-1,k+1}\left(\frac{h}{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}}T(\zeta^{(k)}_{i})\right)
in (
208
). As
h\rightarrow+\infty
, the extra terms are fast spin and thus there exist real valued functions
\gamma_{k1}(u,h,\xi)
such that

S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k+1}\cdot e^{{\mathrm{i}}\gamma_{k1}(h,u,\xi)}\xi(u_{\rm cat})={l}^{(+)}_{k,k+1}(u)\cdot\xi(u_{\rm cat}),

that is (by Proposition
6.17
)

S_{h+}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k+1}\cdot e^{{\mathrm{i}}\gamma_{k1}(h,u,\xi)}\xi(u_{\rm cat})=\mathop{\rm lim}\limits_{u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}}S_{h+}(u)_{k,k+1}\cdot\xi(u_{\rm cat}).

Therefore, if we let
\theta_{k1}(h,u,\xi):=\psi_{k1}+\gamma_{k1}
, then

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm lim}\limits_{h\rightarrow+\infty}\left(\mathop{\rm lim}\limits_{u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}}S_{h+}(u)_{k,k+1}\cdot e^{c_{k1}(\xi)h+{\mathrm{i}}\theta_{k1}(h,u,\xi)}\xi(u_{\rm cat})\right)=\widetilde{e_{k}}(\xi(u_{\rm cat})).

Similar results hold for
S_{h-}
.

Furthermore, by Theorem
7.6
the WKB datum
(E(u_{\rm cat};\lambda)=E_{GT},\widetilde{e_{k}}(u_{\rm cat}),\widetilde{f_{k}}(u_{\rm cat}),\varepsilon_{k},\phi_{k})
with the weight map
wt
is a
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
-crystal. It finishes the proof of Theorem
1.22
.

7.5
The cactus group action on the crystals arsing from the wall-crossing formula

Definition 7.8
.

The Cactus group
Cact_{n}
is a group (with a unit) generated by elements
\sigma_{ij}
,
1\leq i<j\leq n
, subject to the set of relations

•

\sigma_{ij}^{2}=1,
if
1\leq i<j\leq n
,

•

\sigma_{ij}\sigma_{kl}=\sigma_{kl}\sigma_{ij}
, if
j<k
,

•

\sigma_{ij}\sigma_{kl}\sigma_{ij}=\sigma_{i+j-l,i+j-k}
, if
i\leq k<l\leq j
.

Let us set
\sigma_{i}:=\sigma_{1,i+1}
,
1\leq i\leq n-1
. It is clear that
\sigma_{i}^{2}=1
, and the elements
\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{n-1}
generate the Cactus group. The cactus group acts on
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystals by the Schützenberger involution, see e.g.,
[
39
]
. In particular, if we take the realization of a crystal by the Gelfand-Teitlin basis, the action can be described by the results in
[
8
,
9
]
as follows.
Given the representation
L(\lambda)
and the Gelfand-Teitlin basis
E_{GT}(\lambda)
,

Theorem 7.9
.

[
8
,
9
]

There is a
Cact_{n}
group action on the set of
E_{GT}(\lambda)
generated by

\sigma_{i}=t_{1}t_{2}t_{1}t_{3}t_{2}t_{1},...,t_{i}t_{i-1}...t_{1},\ \forall i=1,...,n-1,

(240)

where each
t_{j}
for
j=1,...,n-1
is an operator on
E_{GT}(\lambda)
: for any basis element
\xi_{\Lambda}
, the action
t_{j}(\xi_{\Lambda})
of
t_{j}
on
\xi_{\Lambda}
is another basis element
\xi_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
with the pattern
\Lambda^{\prime}
uniquely determined by

\displaystyle\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(\Lambda^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\lambda^{(k)}_{i}(\Lambda),\ \ \ \text{for}\ k\neq j,

(241)

\displaystyle\lambda^{(j)}_{i}(\Lambda^{\prime})

\displaystyle={\rm Max}(\lambda^{(j+1)}_{i}(\Lambda),\lambda^{(j-1)}_{i-1}(\Lambda))-{\rm Max}(\lambda^{(j)}_{i}(\Lambda)-\lambda^{(j+1)}_{i+1}(\Lambda),\lambda^{(j)}_{i}(\Lambda)-\lambda^{(j-1)}_{i}(\Lambda)).

(242)

Here we presuppose that
\lambda^{(j)}_{0}=-\infty
and
\lambda^{(j)}_{j+1}=+\infty
.

Recall that the regularized limits
S_{h\pm}(u_{\rm cat})
simply encode the leading terms of
S_{h\pm}(u)
as
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
from the connected component
U_{\rm id}
. For each
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\tau_{i}\in S_{n}
be the permutation reversing the segment
[1,...,i]
. Then as
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
from the connected component
U_{\tau_{i}}=\{u\in\mathfrak{h}_{\rm reg}(\mathbb{R})~|~u_{\tau_{i}(1)}<\cdots<u_{\tau_{i}(n)}\}
, the regularized limits are given by different
S_{h\pm}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})\in{\rm End}(L(\lambda))\otimes{\rm End}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
. Similar to the Wall-crossing formula at
u_{\rm cat}
(in the classical case) in Proposition
5.18
, we have

Proposition 7.10
.

The off-diagonal elements of
S_{h\pm}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})
are

S_{h+}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k+1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}S_{h+}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})_{i+1-k,i+2-k},&\text{if}\ \ 1\leq k\leq i,\\
S_{h+}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k+1},&\text{if}\ \ i<k\leq n.\end{array}\right.

(243)

and

S_{h-}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})_{k+1,k}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}S_{h-}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})_{i+2-k,i+1-k},&\text{if}\ \ 1\leq k\leq i,\\
S_{h-}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})_{k,k+1},&\text{if}\ \ i<k\leq n.\end{array}\right.

(244)

Similar to Theorem
1.22
, the WKB approximation of
S_{h\pm}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})
also leads to a
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
-crystal

\left(E_{GT}(\lambda),\{\widetilde{e_{k}}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})\}_{k},\{\widetilde{f_{k}}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})\}_{k}\right),

and by Proposition
7.10
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{e_{k}}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\widetilde{e_{i+1-k}}(u_{\rm cat}),&\text{if}\ \ 1\leq k\leq i,\\
\widetilde{e_{k}}(u_{\rm cat}),&\text{if}\ \ i<k\leq n.\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\widetilde{f_{k}}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\widetilde{f_{i+1-k}}(u_{\rm cat}),&\text{if}\ \ 1\leq k\leq i,\\
\widetilde{f_{k}}(u_{\rm cat}),&\text{if}\ \ i<k\leq n.\end{array}\right.

An analog of Theorem
1.22
is then

Theorem 7.11
.

For each
k=1,...,n-1
, there exists canonical operators
\widetilde{e_{k}}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})
and
\widetilde{f_{k}}^{\tau_{i}}(u_{\rm cat})
acting on the finite set
E_{GT}(\lambda)
such that for any generic element
\xi(u_{\rm cat})\in E_{GT}(\lambda)
, there exist real valued functions
c^{\prime}_{ki}(\xi)
and
\theta^{\prime}_{ki}(h,u,\xi)
with
i=1,2
such that

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm lim}\limits_{h\rightarrow+\infty}\left(\mathop{\rm lim}\limits_{u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}\text{ from }U_{\tau_{i}}}S^{\tau_{i}}_{h+}(u)_{k,k+1}\cdot e^{c^{\prime}_{k1}(\xi)h+{\mathrm{i}}\theta^{\prime}_{k1}(h,u,\xi)}\xi(u_{\rm cat})\right)=\widetilde{e_{k}}^{\tau_{i}}(\xi(u_{\rm cat})),

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm lim}\limits_{h\rightarrow+\infty}\left(\mathop{\rm lim}\limits_{u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}\text{ from }U_{\tau_{i}}}S^{\tau_{i}}_{h-}(u)_{k+1,k}\cdot e^{c^{\prime}_{k2}(\xi)h+{\mathrm{i}}\theta^{\prime}_{k2}(h,u,\xi)}\xi(u_{\rm cat})\right)=\widetilde{f_{k}}^{\tau_{i}}(\xi(u_{\rm cat})).

Furthermore, the set
E_{GT}(\lambda)
equipped with the operators
\widetilde{e_{k}}(u_{\rm cat})
and
\widetilde{f_{k}}(u_{\rm cat})
is a
{\mathfrak{gl}}_{n}
-crystal. Here
u\rightarrow u_{\rm cat}
from
U_{\tau_{i}}
means taking the limit
\frac{u_{\tau_{i}(k+1)}-u_{\tau_{i}(k)}}{u_{\tau_{i}(k)}-u_{\tau_{i}(k-1)}}\rightarrow+\infty
.

Now since the actions of
\widetilde{e_{k}}
,
\widetilde{e_{k}}^{\tau_{i}}
, and the action of the generators
\sigma_{i}
on
E_{GT}(\lambda)
are explicitly given. A straightforward but lengthy computation, using the Cauchy inequalities, verifies that

Proposition 7.12
.

The action of the generators
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1,...,n-1}
on
E_{GT}(\lambda)
of
Cact_{n}
given in Theorem
7.9
satisfy

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}\circ\widetilde{e_{k}}=\widetilde{e_{k}}^{\tau_{i}}\circ\sigma_{i},\hskip 8.53581pt\text{ for all }k=1,...,n-1.

(249)

It gives a proof of Theorem
1.23
.
